On the structure of open del Pezzo surfaces Thanks: This project was funded by the National Science Centre, and, grant number 2021/41/B/ST1/02062. For the purpose of Open Access, the authors have applied a CC-BY public copyright license to any Author Accepted Manuscript version arising from this submission.
Karol Palka, Tomasz Pełka

Abstract
. Let ( X ¯ , D ¯ ) (\overline{X},\overline{D}) be an open log del Pezzo surface of rank one, that is, X ¯ \overline{X} is a normal projective surface of Picard rank one, the boundary D ¯ \overline{D} is a reduced nonzero divisor on X ¯ \overline{X} , and − ( K X ¯ + D ¯ ) -(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D}) is ample. We show that, up to well described exceptions in characteristics 2 , 3 2,3 and 5 5 , the smooth part of X ¯ ∖ D ¯ \overline{X}\setminus\overline{D} admits an 𝔸 1 \mathbb{A}^{1} - or an 𝔸 ∗ 1 \mathbb{A}^{1}_{*} -fibration, which extends to a ℙ 1 \mathbb{P}^{1} -fibration of the minimal log resolution of ( X ¯ , D ¯ ) (\overline{X},\overline{D}) . In characteristic 0 0 , this improves a well-known structure theorem of Miyanishi–Tsunoda [ MT84a ] . Within the proof, we classify rational anti-canonical curves contained in smooth loci of canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14J10; 14D06, 14J45, 14R05

1.
Introduction

A pair
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
consisting of a normal projective surface
\overline{X}
and a reduced Weil divisor
\overline{D}
is called a
log del Pezzo surface
if the anti-log canonical divisor
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is ample. It is
open
if
\overline{D}\neq 0
. Its
rank
is the Picard rank of
\overline{X}
. In this article, we assume that the base field
{k}
is algebraically closed, of arbitrary characteristic; and, unless explicitly stated otherwise, consider only log surfaces with reduced boundaries.

Being one of the possible outcomes of the minimal model program in dimension
2
, log del Pezzo surfaces of rank one are of great importance in birational geometry, and have been studied by many authors from various points of view, see
[
MT84a
,
GZ94
,
KM99
]
. In case
\overline{D}\neq 0
, the structure of the open part
\overline{X}\setminus\overline{D}
is described by theorems of Fujita, Miyanishi, Sugie, Russell and Tsunoda
[
Fuj79
,
Sug80
,
MS80
,
Rus81
,
MT84b
]
. These theorems have numerous applications in affine geometry, cf.
[
KR07
,
Koj14
,
PK13
,
DR01
]
or
[
Miy01
, §II.5]
for a discussion. They imply that if
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
then the open surface
\overline{X}\setminus\overline{D}
admits an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration or a very specific (
Platonic
)
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fibration, see
[
MT84a
]
.

The main goal of this article is to generalize the above theorems to algebraically closed fields of arbitrary characteristic, and at the same time to gain control on the base points of the corresponding
\mathbb{A}^{1}
- or
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
- fibrations on the boundary of the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, call it
(X,D)
. More precisely, while
[
MT84a
]
proves the existence of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
- or
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fibrations of
X\setminus D
– which might have base points on
D
, see Remark
3.5
– we construct
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of
X
whose fiber meets
D
at most twice. We introduce the following definition.

Definition 1.1

(Height)
.

Let
\overline{D}
be a reduced Weil divisor on a normal surface
\overline{X}
, and let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the minimal log resolution, where
D
is the reduced total transform of
\overline{D}
. The
height
of the log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, denoted by
0pt(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, is the infimum of the set of integers
{h}
such that
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D={h}
.
The
height
of a normal surface
\overline{X}
is
0pt(\overline{X})\coloneqq 0pt(\overline{X},0)
.

Enriques classification of smooth projective surfaces implies that
0pt(\overline{X},\overline{D})=\infty
if and only if
\overline{X}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
or
\kappa(\overline{X})\geqslant 0
. Any
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration realizing the infimum in Definition
1.1
is said to
witness
the height.

We can now state our first main result, see Proposition
3.2
and Corollary
3.6
for more detailed versions.

Theorem 1.2

(Structure of open del Pezzo surfaces)
.

Let
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a log del Pezzo surface of rank one such that
\overline{X}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\overline{D}
is reduced and nonzero. Then
0pt(\overline{X},\overline{D})\leqslant 2
, unless one of the following holds.

(a)

\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
and the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is also the minimal log resolution of a log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, where
\overline{Y}
is a canonical surface of rank one and
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a cuspidal curve with
p_{a}(\overline{T})=1
, see Proposition
1.5
. In particular,
0pt(\overline{X})=0pt(\overline{Y})+2\in\{4,6\}
, see Proposition
9.1
.

(b)

\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is isomorphic to one of the two log del Pezzo surfaces constructed in Example
3.9
, see Figures
4(b)
,
4(c)
. In particular,
0pt(\overline{X},\overline{D})=3
.

The assumption
\overline{D}\neq 0
is crucial. Indeed, for a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one, the height
0pt(\overline{X})
can be as high as
4
(for arbitrary
\operatorname{char}{k}
), or even
6
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
or
3
, see
[
PP24
, Theorem E(f)]
.

As a consequence of Proposition
3.2
, we get the following Corollaries
1.3
and
1.4
, generalizing the results of
[
Rus81
]
and
[
MT84a
, §8.2]
. We recall that a surface is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled
if it contains an open subset which is isomorphic to a cylinder
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times C
for some curve
C
; and is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled
if it admits a dominant (not necessarily separable) morphism from an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled surface.

Corollary 1.3

(Log surfaces with
\kappa=-\infty
and connected boundary, see
[
Rus81
]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface and let
D
be a connected, reduced divisor on
X
such that
\kappa(K_{X}+D)=-\infty
. Then the following hold.

(a)

We have either
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 2
, or
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\deg D\leqslant 2
.

(b)

The open part
X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled.

Part
(b)
is the original result of
[
Rus81
]
, obtained by generalizing to arbitrary characteristic previous results of Fujita and Miyanishi–Sugie
[
Fuj79
,
MS80
]
, which later gave the foundation for the Miyanishi–Tsunoda theorem
[
MT84a
]
.
In Section
3
we deduce it from Proposition
3.2
, essentially repeating some arguments of
[
Rus81
]
.

In case
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
, the construction of
[
MT84a
, §8.1]
shows that every Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space becomes
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled after a finite base change, hence is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled. We will see in Section
3
that similar construction shows
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruledness for the open parts of exceptional log del Pezzo surfaces in Theorem
1.2
, too. This leads to the following Corollary
1.4
. By a
minimal model
we mean an outcome of the birational part of the minimal model program, see Section
2B
for details.

Corollary 1.4

(
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruledness, cf.
[
MT84a
, §8.2]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface with a reduced divisor
D
such that
\kappa(K_{X}+D)=-\infty
and
(X,D)
admits a minimal model with nonzero boundary (this holds for instance if the intersection matrix of
D
is not negative definite). Then
X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled.

To prove Theorem
1.2
we need to show that log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
as in
1.2
(a)
occur only if
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
. To do this, we classify all log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
such that
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
and
\overline{T}
is a rational curve with
p_{a}(\overline{T})=1
contained in the smooth locus of
\overline{Y}
. In this case
\overline{Y}
is canonical and
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
, see Lemma
2.11
(b)
. The classification is summarized in Proposition
1.5
below, where by the
singularity type
of a normal surface we mean the weighted graph of the exceptional divisor of its minimal resolution.

We note that canonical surfaces of rank one and their anti-canonical systems are well known, various descriptions are given e.g. in
[
Zha02
,
Ye02
,
KN20
]
. We give a self-contained argument based on a simpler approach, essentially independent of
\operatorname{char}{k}
. Namely, using a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of low height we construct a birational map
\overline{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that the total transform of
\overline{T}
is an arrangement of a rational cubic, possibly one conic, and few lines. This allows to avoid technical analysis of elliptic fibrations done in loc. cit.

Proposition 1.5

(Rational anti-canonical curves on canonical del Pezzo surfaces)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a singularity type of a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one other than
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or the quadric cone (listed in the second column of Table
1
). Then the following hold.

(a)

There exists a del Pezzo surface
\overline{Y}
of rank one and type
\mathcal{S}
whose smooth part
\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
contains a complete nodal (respectively, cuspidal) curve
\overline{T}
of arithmetic genus one if and only if the corresponding row of Table
1
contains
\mathrm{N}
(respectively,
\mathrm{C}
or
\mathrm{C}_{d}
) in the last column.

(b)

The isomorphism class of the log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
as in
(a)
is uniquely determined by the singularity types of
\overline{Y}
and
\overline{T}
, except for types denoted in Table
1
by
\mathrm{C}_{d}
. In these cases, the set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})
of isomorphism classes of log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with
\overline{Y}
of type
\mathcal{S}
and cuspidal
\overline{T}
has moduli dimension
d
.

(c)

If
\overline{T}
is nodal then
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
has an automorphism interchanging the analytic branches of
\overline{T}
at the node.

(d)

We have
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
, and the Picard rank of the minimal resolution of
\overline{Y}
equals
10-\overline{T}^{2}
.

For the definition of moduli dimension, see
[
PP24
, Definitions 1.9, 1.10]
or Section
2F
. By this definition, Proposition
1.5
(b)
implies that the minimal log resolutions of all log surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})
are parametrized by a family with base of dimension
d
. Such a family blows down to a family parametrizing all surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})
, cf.
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.15]
. Additional properties of these families are summarized in Table
2
.

Remark 1.6
.

It is important to note that even if
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})=1
, it can happen that the singularity type
\mathcal{S}
is realized by more than one canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, see
[
Ye02
, Table 1.1]
or
[
PP24
, Table 15]
.
Hence there do exist pairs of canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and the same singularity type, such that one of them contains a rational anti-canonical curve in its smooth locus, and the other does not. For an explicit example of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
see
[
PP24
, Example 5.6(a)]
.

The classification of canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one is known
[
Fur86
,
Ye02
,
KN20
,
AN06
]
, cf. the introduction to Section
6
. In Proposition
6.1
we give an independent argument for surfaces of height at least three; cases of lower height being elementary and covered for instance in
[
PP24
]
. It turns out that the only canonical del Pezzo surfaces of height at least
3
are of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
or, if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
. In Sections
7
and
8
we prove that those surfaces are in fact of height
4
, and we describe their geometry, including their automorphism groups. In many cases this computation is known, see e.g.
[
KN20
]
. Nonetheless, we give a self-contained argument which exploits the geometry of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.

.

2.
Preliminaries

We settle the notation and recall some known results which will be useful in this and the forthcoming articles.

2A.
Divisors on surfaces

Let
D
be a divisor on a normal projective surface
X
. We write
D_{\mathrm{red}}
for
D
with reduced structure. By a
component
of
D
we mean an
irreducible
component of
D_{\mathrm{red}}
; we denote their number by
\#D
. We say that
D
is
connected
if
\operatorname{Supp}D
is connected (in the Zariski topology). If divisors
D,T
and
D-T
are effective, we say that
T
is a
subdivisor
of
D
. In this case
\beta_{D}(T)\coloneqq T\cdot(D-T)
is the
branching number
of
T
in
D
.

Let
D
be a reduced divisor on
X
. A component
T
of
D
is a
tip
of
D
if
\beta_{D}(T)\leqslant 1
; it is
branching
in
D
if
\beta_{D}(T)\geqslant 3
. A point
p\in\operatorname{Sing}D
is a
normal crossing
(
nc
) point of
D
if it lies in the smooth locus
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
of
X
and in some local analytic coordinates
(x,y)
at
p
we have
D=\{xy=0\}
. If all components of
D
are smooth and all singular points of
D
are nc, we call
D
a
simple normal crossing
(
snc
) divisor. A
log smooth surface
is a pair
(X,D)
consisting of a smooth projective surface
X
and an snc divisor
D
on
X
.

Given a birational morphism
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
and a divisor
D
on
X
we write
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
if
\overline{D}=\varphi_{*}D
. We denote by
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
the reduced exceptional divisor of
\varphi
, and by
\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D}
the proper transform of
\overline{D}
on
X
.

A
curve
is an irreducible and reduced variety of dimension one. A curve
C
on a smooth projective surface
X
is called an
n
-curve
if
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
C^{2}=n
. A
(-1)
-curve
C
in an snc divisor
D
is
superfluous
if
\beta_{D}(C)\in\{1,2\}
and
C\cdot T\leqslant 1
for every component
T
of
D-C
. This way, the image of
D
after a contraction of
C
is still an snc divisor. An snc divisor is
snc-minimal
if it contains no superfluous
(-1)
-curves.

Let
D
be an snc divisor. We say that
D
is
rational
if all its components are. We say that
D
is
negative definite
if so is its intersection matrix
[T_{i}\cdot T_{j}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant\#D}
, where
T_{1},\dots,T_{\#D}
are components of
D
. The
discriminant
of
D
is
d(D)\coloneqq\det[-T_{i}\cdot T_{j}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant\#D}
, we put
d(0)=1
. For elementary properties of discriminants see
[
Fuj82
, §3]
.

A connected snc divisor with no branching component is a
chain
if it has a tip and
circular
if it does not. A connected snc divisor is a
tree
if it has no circular subdivisor. It is known that a connected reduced divisor has arithmetic genus zero if and only if it is a rational tree. As a consequence, we have the following observation.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
Miy01
, Lemma II.2.2.2]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective rational surface, and let
D
be a connected reduced divisor on
X
such that
|K_{X}+D|=\emptyset
. Then
p_{a}(D)=0
, so
D
is a rational tree.

The exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{D}\longrightarrow 0
yields an exact sequence
H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\longrightarrow H^{1}(D,\mathcal{O}_{D})\longrightarrow H^{2}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D))
, whose first term vanishes because
X
is rational, and the last one is dual to
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X}+D))
, which vanishes by assumption. Thus
H^{1}(D,\mathcal{O}_{D})=0
, so
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{D})=1
since
D
is connected. Taking Euler characteristics in the above exact sequence gives
1=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{D})=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}(-D))=1-p_{a}(D)
by Riemann-Roch. Thus
p_{a}(D)=0
, as needed.

∎

Let
T
be a chain with a chosen
first
tip
T^{(1)}
. Then the components
T^{(1)},\dots,T^{(\#T)}
of
T
are ordered in a unique way such that
T^{(i)}\cdot T^{(i+1)}=1
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\#T-1
.
We write
T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}
for the same chain with an opposite order.

A
type
of an ordered rational chain
T
is a sequence of integers
[a_{1},\dots,a_{\#T}]
, where
a_{i}=-(T^{(i)})^{2}
. We often abuse notation and identify a chain with its type. Moreover, for types
T_{1}=[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]
,
T_{2}=[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]
we write
[T_{1},T_{2}]=[T_{1},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]=[a_{1},\dots,a_{k},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]
etc.
We write
(m)_{k}
for an integer
m
repeated
k
times.

Let again
D
be an snc divisor. An ordered subchain
T
of
D
is a
twig
of
D
if its first tip is a tip of
D
, and no component of
T
is branching in
D
. In this case, the last tip of
T
meets
D-T
, or
T
is a connected component of
D
. A
(-2)
-twig
(chain, fork) is a twig (chain, fork) whose all components are
(-2)
-curves.

A tree
T
whose unique branching component
B
has
\beta_{T}(B)=3
is called a
fork
. A
type
of a rational fork with branching component
B
consists of an integer
b\coloneqq-B^{2}
and an (unordered) set of types
\{T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\}
of its twigs meeting
B
. We denote this type by

\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
.
If
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
are admissible chains, we put

\delta_{T}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}.

We say that
T
is
admissible
if
b\geqslant 2
and
\delta_{T}>1
. The latter condition means that
\{d(T_{1}),d(T_{2}),d(T_{3})\}
is one of the
Platonic
triples
\{2,3,5\}
,
\{2,3,4\}
,
\{2,3,3\}
or
\{2,2,k\}
for some
k\geqslant 2
; in particular one of the twigs
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
is of type
[2]
, cf.
[
Miy01
, §I.5.3.4]
.

2B.
Dlt log surfaces and minimal models

We now recall the basic notions of dlt, log terminal and log canonical surfaces, see
[
Kol13
, §2.1]
. Let
\overline{X}
be a normal surface, and let
\overline{D}
be a divisor on
\overline{X}
with coefficients between
0
and
1
(in this article, we will only consider the case when
\overline{D}
is reduced). A
log resolution
of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a birational morphism
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
such that
X
is smooth and
D_{\mathrm{red}}
is snc, where
D=\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D}+\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
. A log resolution is
minimal
if it not birationally dominated by any other log resolution.

Assume that the divisor
K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Put
E\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
. Since
E
is negative definite, the formulas

(1)

\varphi^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=K_{X}+\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D}+\sum_{T\subseteq E}\operatorname{cf}_{E}(T)\,T\quad\mbox{and}\quad\operatorname{ld}_{E}(T)=1-\operatorname{cf}_{E}(T)

uniquely define, for each component
T
of
E
, rational numbers
\operatorname{cf}_{E}(T)
and
\operatorname{ld}_{E}(T)
, called the
coefficient
and
log discrepancy
of
T
, respectively. Here the sum runs over all components of
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
.
They can be easily computed from the weighted graph of
E

[
Kol92
, §3.2]
, cf.
[
Pal24
, §4C and §7]
or Lemmas
2.2
,
2.3
below. If
\varphi
is a minimal resolution of singularities of
\overline{X}
, i.e.
E
contains no
(-1)
-curves, then
\operatorname{cf}_{E}(T)\geqslant 0
for every component
T
of
E
. We skip the subscript
E
whenever it is clear from the context.

A log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is
log terminal
, (respectively,
log canonical
or
canonical
) if for every exceptional divisor
T
of some (equivalently, any) log resolution
\varphi
we have
\operatorname{cf}(T)<1
(respectively,
\operatorname{cf}(T)\leqslant 1
or
\operatorname{cf}(T)\leqslant 0
). It is
divisorially log terminal
(
dlt
) if
\operatorname{cf}(T)<1
for every exceptional divisor
T
such that
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is not log smooth near
\varphi(T)
. A normal surface
\overline{X}
is log terminal (or (log) canonical) if so is
(\overline{X},0)
.

Assume that
\overline{D}
is reduced. Then the log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is dlt if and only if the exceptional divisor of a minimal log resolution
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a sum of some admissible twigs of
D
and connected components of
D
which are admissible chains or forks
[
Kol13
, 3.31, 3.40]
, cf.
[
Pal24
, Lemma 4.5]
. A surface singularity is canonical if and only if it is du Val, i.e. its minimal resolution is a
(-2)
-chain or fork
[
Kol13
, 3.26]
.

Lemma 2.2

(Formulas for coefficients, see
[
Miy01
, II.3.3]
or
[
Kol92
, §3.2]
)
.

(a)

Let
T
be an ordered admissible chain. Put

T^{(<j)}=\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}T^{(i)},\quad T^{(>j)}=\sum_{i=j+1}^{\#T}T^{(i)}.\qquad\mbox{Then}\quad\operatorname{cf}_{T}(T^{(j)})=1-\frac{d(T^{(>j)})+d(T^{(<j)})}{d(T)}.

(b)

Let
T=\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
be an admissible fork, with branching component
B
. Put

\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})},\quad e=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d(T_{i}-T_{i}^{-})}{d(T_{i})},

where
T_{i}^{-}\coloneqq T_{i}^{(\#T_{i})}
is the component of
T_{i}
meeting
B
. Then
\delta>1
,
e<2\leqslant b
, and

\operatorname{cf}_{T}(B)=1-\frac{\delta-1}{b-e},\quad\operatorname{cf}_{T}(T_{i}^{(j)})=1-\frac{(1-\operatorname{cf}_{T}(B))\cdot d(T_{i}^{(<j)})+d(T_{i}^{(>j)})}{d(T_{i})}.

(c)

Let
T
be an admissible twig of a reduced divisor
D\neq T
, and let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the contraction of
T
. Then the coefficients with respect to the pair
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
are given by

\operatorname{cf}(T^{(j)})=1-\frac{d(T^{(>j)})}{d(T)}

Explicit formulas for log discrepancies in Lemma
2.2
imply the following.

Lemma 2.3

(Coefficients decrease in subgraphs
[
KM99
, L.1(2)]
, cf.
[
Pal24
, Lemma 7.6]
)
.

Let
D
and
D^{\prime}
be rational chains or rational forks, such that
D
is admissible, the graph of
D^{\prime}
is a subgraph of the graph of
D
, and if
C^{\prime}
is a component of
D^{\prime}
corresponding to a component
C
of
D
then
C^{2}\leqslant(C^{\prime})^{2}\leqslant-2
. Then
D^{\prime}
is admissible, too, and we have

\operatorname{cf}_{D}(D)\leqslant\operatorname{cf}_{D^{\prime}}(C^{\prime}).

We now recall basic consequences of the minimal model program for dlt log surfaces, see
[
KM98
, Theorem 3.47]
. We say that a dlt log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is
minimal
if it is an outcome of the birational part of the MMP, that is, there is no divisorial contraction which is negative with respect to
K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D}
. Every log smooth (or, more generally, dlt) log surface
(X,D)
admits a morphism onto a minimal dlt log surface
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
of the same Kodaira dimension, say
\kappa
, such that either
\kappa\geqslant 0
and
K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min}
is nef; or
\kappa=-\infty
and there is a morphism
f\colon X_{\min}\longrightarrow B
of relative Picard rank one such that
\dim B\leqslant 1
and
-(K_{X_{\min}}+D_{\min})
is
f
-ample. We call
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
a
minimal model
of
(X,D)
, and refer to the morphism
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\min},D_{\min})
as an
MMP run
.

We say that a dlt log surface is
almost minimal
if it admits an MMP run with exceptional divisor contained in the boundary, see
[
Pal24
, Lemma 3.16(2)]
; cf.
[
Miy01
, p. 107]
and the discussion in
[
Pal24
, Remark 3.24]
. If
(X,D)
is log smooth, then an MMP run
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\min},D_{\min})
factors through the minimal log resolution
(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
of
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
, which we call an
almost minimal model
of
(X,D)
. It follows from
[
Pal24
, Lemma 4.9]
that the morphism
(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
is in fact an snc-minimalization of
D+E
, where
E
is a sum of certain
(-1)
-curves characterized in loc. cit.

2C.
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth log surface. A
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
of
X
is a morphism
p\colon X\longrightarrow B
onto a curve
B
whose general fiber
F
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We call
F\cdot D
the
height
of
p
(with respect to
D
). By Definition
1.1
, the
height
of
(X,D)
is the minimal height of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
; a
witnessing

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration is any
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration realizing this minimum. Height of a pair
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
consisting of a normal surface
\overline{X}
and a reduced Weil divisor
\overline{D}
on
\overline{X}
is defined as the height of its minimal log resolution. For a normal surface
\overline{X}
we put
0pt(\overline{X})=0pt(\overline{X},0)
.

Note that by Tsen’s theorem, cf.
[
Rei97
, C.4]
, every
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of a smooth surface is trivial over some dense open subset of the base.

Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow B
of a smooth projective surface
X
. A curve
C\subseteq X
is
vertical
if
p(C)
is a point; otherwise
C
is horizontal, it is an
n
-section if
C\cdot F=n
for a fiber
F
. Given a reduced divisor
T
we write
T_{\mathrm{vert}}
and
T_{\mathrm{hor}}
for the sum of its vertical and horizontal components, respectively. Every fiber of
p
is obtained by inductively blowing up over a
0
-curve. This leads to the following description of degenerate fibers.

Lemma 2.4

(Degenerate fibers)
.

Let
F
be a degenerate fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of a smooth projective surface. Then
F_{\mathrm{red}}
is a rational tree and its
(-1)
-curves are non-branching. Furthermore, the following hold.

(a)

If some
(-1)
-curve
L
has multiplicity
1
in
F
then
L
is a tip of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
and
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L
contains a
(-1)
-curve.

(b)

Assume that
F
contains exactly one
(-1)
-curve
L
. Then
F
has exactly two components of multiplicity
1
, they are tips of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
, and one of the following holds.

(i)

F_{\mathrm{red}}=[T,1,T^{*}]
for some admissible chain
T
. Here
T^{*}
is a type of an admissible chain which is
adjoint
to
T
, it is uniquely determined by
T
, see
[
Fuj82
, §3.9]
.

(ii)

Both components of multiplicity one belong to the same connected component of
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L
. The other connected component, if exists, is a chain.

(c)

Assume that
F
consists of
(-1)
- and
(-2)
-curves only. Then
F_{\mathrm{red}}
is of one of the following types.

(i)

[1,(2)_{k},1]
for some
k\geqslant 0
. In this case,
F=F_{\mathrm{red}}
.

(ii)

[2,1,2]
or
\langle 2;[1,(2)_{k}],[2],[2]\rangle
for some
k\geqslant 0
. In this case, the
(-2)
-tips of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
have multiplicity
1
in
F
, and all the remaining components of
F
have multiplicity
2
.

Contracting all fibers to
0
-curves gives a morphism onto a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})
, and since
\rho(\mathbb{F}_{m})=2
one gets the following formula proved in
[
Fuj82
, 4.16]
, cf.
[
Pal15
, Lemma 2]
.

Lemma 2.5

(Number of vertical curves off
D
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth log surface with a fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration. Let
\nu_{\infty}
be the number of fibers contained in
D
. For a fiber
F
let
\sigma(F)
be the number of components of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
not contained in
D
. Then

\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}+\nu_{\infty}+\rho(X)=\#D+2+\textstyle\sum_{F}(\sigma(F)-1).

where the sum runs over all fibers
F
not contained in
D
.

Recall that a normal surface
\overline{X}
is
del Pezzo
if
-K_{\overline{X}}
is ample. Lemma
2.5
applied to the minimal resolution of a del Pezzo surface of rank one gives the following.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one, let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be its minimal resolution, and let
D=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
. Then the following hold.

(a)

A component of a degenerate fiber is a
(-1)
-curve if and only if it is not contained in
D
.

(b)

For a degenerate fiber
F
let
\sigma(F)
be the number of
(-1)
-curves in
F_{\mathrm{red}}
, i.e. the number of components not contained in
D
. Then
\sum_{F}(\sigma(F)-1)=\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}-1
.

(a)

Since
\pi
is the minimal resolution,
D
contains no
(-1)
-curves.
In turn, every component
L
of a degenerate fiber such that
L\not\subseteq D
satisfies
L^{2}<0
and, since
-K_{\overline{X}}
is ample,
0>\pi(L)\cdot K_{\overline{X}}\geqslant L\cdot K_{X}
because
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}-K_{X}
is effective by minimality of
\pi
. Thus
L
is a
(-1)
-curve, as needed.

(b)
We have
\rho(X)-\#D=\rho(\overline{X})=1
, and
D
contains no fibers since it is negative definite. Hence the claim follows from Lemma
2.5
.

∎

We note that a del Pezzo surface of rank one is rational if and only if all its singularities are. The
“only if”
part is elementary, while the
“if”
part is proved in
[
Che97
, §2]
and
[
Fuj95
, Corollary 1.9]
for
{k}=\mathbb{C}
, and in general follows from
[
Sch01
, Theorem 2.2(i)]
. We recall the argument for the readers’ convenience.

Lemma 2.7
.

Del Pezzo surface of rank one is rational if and only if it has only rational singularities.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one, let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be its minimal resolution and let
D=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. Assume first that
X
is rational, so
H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
. From the second page of the Leray spectral sequence we get an exact sequence

0=H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\longrightarrow H^{0}(\overline{X},R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\longrightarrow H^{2}(\overline{X},R^{0}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
Since
\overline{X}
is normal, we have
R^{0}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}
. Moreover, by Serre criterion
\overline{X}
is Cohen–Macaulay, so by Serre duality, see
[
KM98
, Theorem 5.71 and Proposition 5.75]
, we have
H^{2}(\overline{X},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}})\cong H^{0}(\overline{X},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(K_{\overline{X}}))^{*}=0
since
-K_{\overline{X}}
is ample. We conclude that
H^{0}(\overline{X},R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
, so the singularities of
\overline{X}
are rational, as needed.

Conversely, assume that
\overline{X}
has only rational singularities, so
D
is a sum of rational trees, see
[
Né22
, 7.1.2(iv)]
. Since
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo, we have
\kappa(X)=-\infty
, so
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration over some curve
B
. Since
D
is negative definite, it contains no fibers, so the equality
\#D=\rho(X)-1
implies that
D
has a horizontal component. Since the latter is rational, so is
B
and therefore
X
, as needed.

∎

2D.
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-(uni)ruled surfaces

Recall that a surface is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled
if it contains an open subset isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times C
for some curve
C
; and it is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled
if it admits a dominant morphism from an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled one. The following equivalence is known.

Lemma 2.8

(
[
Miy01
, Lemma II.2.9.1]
)
.

A smooth surface is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled if and only if it admits a log smooth completion of height at most
1
.

Let
V
be a smooth surface and let
(X,D)
be its log smooth completion. If a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow B
has height at most one with respect to
D
then by Tsen’s theorem
p|_{V}
restricts to a projection
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times B^{\circ}\longrightarrow B^{\circ}
over some dense open subset
B^{\circ}\subseteq B
. Conversely, if
V
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled then the open embedding
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times C\hookrightarrow V
extends to a morphism
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times C\longrightarrow X
, and blowing up over
D
we can assume that this is an open embedding, too. The projection
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times C\longrightarrow C
extends to the required
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most one.

∎

It follows from Lemma
2.8
that every
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled surface is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibered. The converse holds if
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
, see
[
Miy01
, Lemma III.1.3.1]
, but fails if
\operatorname{char}{k}>0
, as shown by the following example.

Example 2.9

(
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration which is non-trivial over all dense open subsets of the base)
.

Assume
p\coloneqq\operatorname{char}{k}>0
. Let
\pi\colon\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
be the projection, and let
H\coloneqq\{(x,[y_{0}:y_{1}])\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}:xy_{0}^{p}=y_{1}^{p}\}
,
S\coloneqq(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\setminus H
. Then
\pi|_{S}\colon S\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration. Since every morphism
\mathbb{A}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
is constant, every curve on
S
which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is vertical, in particular
\pi|_{S}
is the unique
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration of
S
. Since
H\cdot F=p
for a general fiber
F
of
\pi
, the proper transform of
H
is a
p
-section of every birational model of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
. It follows that
\pi|_{S}
cannot be completed to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
1
, hence is non-trivial over any open dense subset of the base
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
. As a consequence,
S
is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled.

Lemma 2.10

(
[
Miy01
, Corollary II.2.11.1]
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface such that
D
contains a rational twig which is not negative definite. Then
X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled.

Contracting superfluous
(-1)
-curves in
D
and its images we can assume that
D
has a rational twig
T
whose
k
-th component
C
has
C^{2}\geqslant 0
. Blowing up
C^{2}
times over
C\cap(D-C)
, or over any point of
C
if
D=C
, we can assume
C=[0]
. Assume
k
is smallest possible. If
k=1
, i.e. if
C
is a tip of
D
, then the linear system
|C|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height 1 with respect to
D
, so the claim follows from Lemma
2.8
. Otherwise, let
C_{-}
,
C_{+}
be the
(k-1)
-th and
(k+1)
-th component of
T
(if
C
meets
D-T
we let
C_{+}
be the component of
D-T
meeting
C
). Blow up
-C_{-}^{2}
times at
C\cap C_{+}
, each time on the proper transform of
C_{+}
, and contract the total transform of
C
minus the exceptional
(-1)
-curve. Then the reduced total transform of
D
is a chain whose
(k-1)
-th component, the proper transform of
C_{-}
, is a
0
-curve. We conclude by induction on
k
.

∎

2E.
Rational curves of arithmetic genus
0
and
1
contained in the smooth locus

Lemma 2.11

(
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s and rational elliptic curves in the smooth locus)
.

Let
\overline{Y}
be a normal projective surface of rank one, and let
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
be a projective curve contained its smooth locus. The following hold.

(a)

If
\overline{T}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
then
\overline{Y}
is isomorphic to a cone over a rational normal curve, which is the image of
\overline{T}
.

(b)

Assume that
\overline{T}
is a rational curve of arithmetic genus one. Then
\overline{Y}
is a canonical del Pezzo surface and
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
. Moreover, if
\overline{T}
is reducible then
\overline{Y}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or to the quadric cone.

We can assume
\overline{Y}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, otherwise the result is clear. Let
\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
be the minimal resolution,
B=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
and
T=\pi^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}
. Since
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
,
\pi
is an isomorphism near
T
, in particular
T\cong\overline{T}
.

(a)
Since
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
, the number
m=\overline{T}^{2}
is a positive integer. It is enough to prove that
Y
is isomorphic to the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
, see
[
Har77
, Example V.2.11.4]
.

Blow up
m
times at some point
p\in T
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
T
. Denote the resulting morphism by
\varphi
. Put
B^{\prime}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}B
,
T^{\prime}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}T
and write
(\varphi^{*}T)_{\mathrm{red}}=T^{\prime}+H+V
, where
H=[1]
and
V=[(2)_{m-1}]
. Note that
\varphi
is an isomorphism near
B^{\prime}
. The linear system
|T^{\prime}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
H
is a
1
-section and
B^{\prime}+V
is vertical. If there are no degenerate fibers then
B^{\prime}+V=0
, so
m=1
,
Y=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
T
is a line, as needed. Let
F
be a degenerate fiber. By Lemma
2.5

F_{\mathrm{red}}
has a unique component off
B^{\prime}+V
, say
L_{F}
. Since
B^{\prime}+V
contains no
(-1)
-curves,
L_{F}
is the unique
(-1)
-curve in
F
, so it has multiplicity at least
2
in
F
, and thus
L_{F}\cdot H=0
. Since
B^{\prime}\cdot H=0
,
L_{F}
meets
V
and
B^{\prime}
. Hence there is only one degenerate fiber, supported on
B^{\prime}+L_{F}+V=[m,1,(2)_{m-1}]
. Thus
B=[m]
, so
Y=\mathbb{F}_{m}
, as claimed.

(b)
Since
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
, the condition
p_{a}(\overline{T})=0
implies that
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
, so
\overline{Y}
is del Pezzo.

Assume that
T
is reducible. Then
T
has a component
T_{0}
with
p_{a}(T_{0})=0
. Since
Y\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, part
(a)
shows that
Y
is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
for some
m\geqslant 2
, and
T_{0}
is disjoint from the
(-m)
-curve
\Xi_{m}
. Since
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
,
T
is disjoint from
\Xi_{m}
, too, so
T\in|kT_{0}|
for some
k\geqslant 1
. Now
0=2p_{a}(T)-2=k(m(k-1)-2)
, so
k=m=2
. We conclude that
Y\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
, so
\overline{Y}
is a quadric cone, as needed.

Assume now that
T
is irreducible. Since
\overline{Y}
is del Pezzo, we have
\kappa(Y)=-\infty
, so
Y
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration onto some curve
C
. Since the curve
T
is singular, it is horizontal, and since
T
is rational, so is the base
C
. It follows that
Y
is a rational surface, so by Riemann–Roch
\dim|K_{Y}+T|\geqslant p_{a}(T)-1=0
. Thus
|K_{Y}+T|\neq\emptyset
.

Because the resolution
\pi
is minimal, we have
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{Y}}=K_{Y}+B^{\#}
for some effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
B^{\#}
supported on
B
. Since
T=\pi^{*}\overline{T}=-\pi^{*}K_{\overline{Y}}
, we get
K_{Y}+T=-B^{\#}
. Thus
B^{\#}=0
, so
\overline{Y}
is canonical, as needed.

∎

2F.
Representing families and moduli dimension

We now recall the definition of
moduli dimension
from
[
PP24
, Definitions 1.9, 1.10]
used in Proposition
1.5
(b)
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow B
be a smooth surjective morphism between smooth connected varieties, and let
\mathcal{D}
be a reduced divisor on
\mathcal{X}
such that the restriction of
f
to each component of
\mathcal{D}
is a smooth and surjective morphism with irreducible fibers. Let
\operatorname{Fib}(f)
be the set of isomorphism classes of fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})\coloneqq(f^{-1}(b),\mathcal{D}|_{f^{-1}(b)})
. We say that
f
is
almost faithful
if
f
is equivariant with respect to the action of some finite group
G
such that two fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})
and
(X_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}})
are isomorphic if and only if
b
and
b^{\prime}
lie in the same orbit. The image of
G
in
\operatorname{Aut}(B)
is called the
symmetry group
of
f
. We say that
f
is
almost universal
if it is almost faithful and furthermore the formal germ of
f
at each
b\in B
is a semiuniversal deformation of
(X_{b},D_{b})
, see
[
Ser06
, Definition 2.2.6]
.

We say that a set
\mathcal{C}
consisting of isomorphism classes of log surfaces
has moduli dimension
d
if there is an almost universal family
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
with
\dim B=d
which
represents

\mathcal{C}
, i.e. has
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Fib}(f)
. In this case
d=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b}))
for any
b\in B
, cf.
[
Ser06
, Proposition 3.4.17]
.
More generally, if
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
consists of some isomorphism classes of log surfaces with reduced boundaries, we say that
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
has moduli dimension
d
if so does the set of isomorphism classes of minimal log resolutions of log surfaces in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.

In this article, we construct almost faithful families representing the sets
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})
of isomorphism classes of log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, where
\overline{Y}
is a canonical surface of rank
1
and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, and
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a cuspidal member of
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
. If
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})=1
then the base of such a family is a point, otherwise their bases and symmetry groups are listed in Table
2
below. The constructions of these families are based on the constructions of surfaces
\overline{Y}
which have already appeared in the literature, e.g. in
[
KN22
]
.

3.
Improvements of the theorem of Miyanishi–Tsunoda

We now state our first main result, Proposition
3.2
, and derive its consequences, such as Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
1.3
. The proof will be given in Section
4
. We study minimal models of log smooth surfaces of negative Kodaira dimension whose boundary does not contract to log terminal singularities, e.g. log smooth completions of open del Pezzo surfaces as in Theorem
1.2
. We bound their height and precisely describe their geometry.

Recall that a log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
of negative Kodaira dimension is
minimal
if and only if it is a log Mori fiber space, i.e.
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is
f
-ample for some morphism
f\colon\overline{X}\longrightarrow B
of relative Picard rank one with
\dim B\leqslant 1
.

To formulate our result, it will be convenient to distinguish the following types of degenerate fibers.

Definition 3.1

(Some particular types of degenerate fibers in case
0pt=2
, see Figure
1
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface with a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections. We say that

(a)

F
is
columnar
if
F_{\mathrm{red}}
is a chain meeting
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in both tips, such that
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L\subseteq D
, where
L
is the unique
(-1)
-curve in
F_{\mathrm{red}}
. In this case,
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[T,1,T^{*}]
for some admissible chain
T
, see Lemma
2.4
(b)(b.i)
.

(b)

F
is
quasi-columnar
if either
F=[0]
and
F
is a component of
D
; or
F_{\mathrm{red}}+D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a fork with exactly one component off
D
, which is the last tip of a vertical twig of type
[(2)_{s},1]
for some
s\geqslant 0
which does not meet
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. In this case,
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[(2)_{s},1,s+1]
or
\langle s+2;T,[(2)_{s},1],T^{*}\rangle
for some admissible chain
T
.

Proposition 3.2

(Description of almost minimal log surfaces, generalizing
[

]

)
.

Let

\overline{D}\neq 0

(3.1)

and

(3.2)

\nu\leqslant 2

(3.3)

(3.4)

\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}

\nu=3

(F_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}=[T_{i},1,T_{i}^{*}]

\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}>1
, see Fig.

Moreover,
H_{-}=[m]
for some
m\geqslant 2

,

(4)

\operatorname{char}{k}=2

Remark 3.3

(Adjusting minimal models in case
3.2
(3)
: Fig.
3

\rightarrow
Fig.
2
)
.

Given a log smooth surface of negative Kodaira dimension, its minimal model
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is as in Proposition
3.2
. In case
3.2
(3)
, we can choose
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
so that its minimal log resolution
(X,D)
is as in Figure
2
, that is, either as in
3.2
(3.1)
–
(3.2)
with at most one degenerate fiber, which is columnar, or a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space as in
3.2
(3.4)
.

Indeed, suppose
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is not of the above type, so
(X,D)
is as in Figure
3
. Let

(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a contraction of
F_{1}
to a
0
-curve
F^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
. Then
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a minimal log resolution of another minimal model. We have
F^{\prime}\subseteq D^{\prime}
in cases
(3.1)
,
(3.2)
and
F^{\prime}\not\subseteq D
in case
(3.3)
, so
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is as in Figure
3
, as needed.

Remark 3.4

(
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled cases of Proposition
3.2
)
.

Let
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be an open log del Pezzo surface as in Proposition
3.2
(1)
,
(2)
, or
(3.1)
–
(3.3)
. Then
\overline{X}\setminus\overline{D}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled.
Indeed, in case
3.2
(2)
we have
0pt(\overline{X},\overline{D})=1
, so
\overline{X}\setminus\overline{D}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled by Lemma
2.8
, otherwise
D
contains a twig which is not negative definite, see Figure
2
, so
\overline{X}\setminus\overline{D}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled by Lemma
2.10
.

Remark 3.5

(Deep base points of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibrations in case
0pt=2
)
.

In cases
3.2
(3.1)
–
(3.3)
, the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration of
X\setminus D
has a base point on
D
. In fact, for any
m\geqslant 2
one easily constructs examples where any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration of
X\setminus D
requires at least
m
blowups on
D
to have its base points resolved. To see one such example, take
X=\mathbb{F}_{m}
and
D=\Xi_{m}+F+C=[m,0,-m]
, where
\Xi_{m}=[m]
is the negative section,
F
is a fiber, and
C=[-m]
is a section disjoint from
\Xi_{m}
, see Figure
2(b)
. Write
\{q\}=F\cap C
. Then
0pt(X,D)=2
and
X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibered. Indeed, blow up
m
times at
q
, each time on the proper transform of
C
. The latter becomes a
0
-curve whose linear system gives an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration of
X\setminus D
.

Let now
V\subseteq X
be a closure of a smooth fiber of some
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration of
X\setminus D
. Then
V
is a curve meeting
D
in one point. Put
d=V\cdot F
. We have
V\cdot C=V\cdot(mF+\Xi_{m})>V\cdot\Xi_{m}
, so since
C
and
\Xi_{m}
are disjoint, we have
V\cdot\Xi_{m}=0
and
V\cdot C=dm>d
. In particular,
V\cap D=\{q\}
and
V
has a cusp of multiplicity
d
at
q
. Blow up at
q
, let
F^{\prime}
be the exceptional curve, and let
V^{\prime},C^{\prime}
be the proper transforms of
V,C
. Then
V^{\prime}\cdot F^{\prime}=d
and
V^{\prime}\cdot C^{\prime}=d(m-1)
; in particular,
V^{\prime}
still has a cusp of multiplicity
d
. Repeating this argument with
(V^{\prime},F^{\prime},C^{\prime})
instead of
(V,F,C)
we see that it takes at least
m
blowups to resolve the cusp of
V
, as claimed.

Corollary 3.6

(Proposition
3.2
without minimality assumption)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface such that
\kappa(K_{X}+D)=-\infty
. Assume that
(X,D)
admits a minimal model with nonzero boundary (for instance
D
is not negative definite). Then one of the following holds.

(a)

We have
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\deg D\leqslant 2
.

(b)

We have
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 2
.

(c)

We have
0pt(X,D)=3
and there is a birational morphism
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
such that
\deg{p}=2
,
\psi
is an isomorphism near
\psi^{-1}_{*}{p}
and it contracts some connected component of
D
.

(d)

We have
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
0pt(X,D)=3
, and
(X,D)
admits a morphism onto a log surface as in Example
3.9
.

(e)

We have
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
,
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 6
and
(X,D)
admits a morphism onto a log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with
\overline{Y}
canonical of rank one, and
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
cuspidal, contained in
\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, see Proposition
3.2
(6)
.

Let
\overline{\psi}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a morphism onto a minimal model of
(X,D)
with
\overline{D}\neq 0
. We have
\kappa(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=-\infty
, so
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is as in Proposition
3.2
. Since
(X,D)
is log smooth, the morphism
\overline{\psi}
factors through a morphism
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
onto the minimal log resolution
(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
.

Pulling back
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations from
X_{\textnormal{am}}
we get
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 0pt(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
, so in cases
3.2
(2)
–
3.2
(4)
we get that
(b)
holds. Exceptional cases
3.2
(5)
and
3.2
(6)
yield
(d)
and
(e)
, respectively. Consider case
3.2
(1)
. If
\psi=\mathrm{id}
then
(a)
holds, so assume
\psi\neq\mathrm{id}
, let
p
be a base point of
\psi^{-1}
and let
L_{p}\subseteq X
be the first exceptional curve over
p
. A pencil of lines through
p
pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
(X,D)
of height at most
\deg D_{\textnormal{am}}
if
p\in D_{\textnormal{am}}
or
L_{p}\not\subseteq D
; and
\deg D_{\textnormal{am}}+1
otherwise. Since
\deg D_{\textnormal{am}}\leqslant 2
we get either
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 2
, so
(b)
holds; or
0pt(X,D)=3
,
\deg D_{\textnormal{am}}=2
,
p\not\in D_{\textnormal{am}}
and
L_{p}\subseteq D
, in which case
L_{p}
is contained in a connected component of
D
contracted by
\psi
, so
(c)
holds.

∎

Let
\pi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the minimal log resolution. Since
\rho(\overline{X})=1
, we have
\overline{D}^{2}>0
, so
D
is not negative definite. Thus
(X,D)
is as in Corollary
3.6
. Since by assumption
\overline{X}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, case
3.6
(a)
does not hold. Case
3.6
(c)
does not hold, either, since by the minimality of
\pi
no connected component of
\overline{D}
contracts to a smooth point.
Case
3.6
(b)
asserts that
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 2
, as needed.

In the remaining cases
3.6
(d)
,
(e)
we have a birational morphism
\overline{\psi}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, where
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
is a log del Pezzo surface of rank one from Example
3.9
; or a log surface as in
3.6
(e)
, consisting of a canonical surface of rank one and an irreducible member of
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
. Since
(X,D)
is log smooth,
\overline{\psi}
factors through a morphism
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,T)
onto a minimal log resolution of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
. We have
(\rho(X)-\#D)-(\rho(Y)-\#T)=1-1=0
, so
\#D-\#T=\rho(X)-\rho(Y)
and therefore
\operatorname{Exc}\psi\subseteq D
. Since
D
is snc-minimal, it follows that
\psi
is an isomorphism. Thus in case
3.6
(e)
we get that
1.2
(a)
holds. In case
3.6
(d)
, we check directly that, since
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is ample,
\overline{D}
is the image of the unique
(-1)
-curve in
D
, see Figures
4(b)
,
4(c)
; hence
1.2
(b)
holds, as claimed.

∎

As in the proof of Corollary
3.6
above, we can assume that
(X,D)
is a minimal log resolution of its minimal model
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
. Assume
\overline{D}\neq 0
, so
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is as in Proposition
3.2
. Since
D
is connected, the only possible cases are
3.2
(1)
,
(2)
,
(3.1)
and
(3.2)
. Thus
1.3
(a)
holds, and
1.3
(b)
follows from Remark
3.4
.

Therefore, we can assume
\overline{D}=0
. If
\overline{X}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
then the assertion is clear, so we can assume
\overline{X}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Then
\overline{X}
is a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one, with exactly one singularity. The exceptional divisor
D
of its minimal resolution is an admissible chain or fork. We claim that

(2)

h^{0}(-K_{X}-D)\geqslant K_{X}\cdot(K_{X}+D)>0.

We have
h^{2}(-K_{X}-D)=h^{0}(2K_{X}+D)=0
. By Lemma
2.7

X
is rational, so the Riemann-Roch theorem gives
h^{0}(-K_{X}-D)\geqslant p_{a}(D)+K_{X}\cdot(K_{X}+D)
. Since
p_{a}(D)=0
, this proves the first inequality of (
2
). Since
D
is admissible, every component
T
of
D
has
K_{X}\cdot T\geqslant 0
and
\operatorname{cf}(T)\in[0,1)
, see Lemma
2.2
. Thus
K_{X}\cdot(K_{X}+D)\geqslant K_{X}\cdot(K_{X}+\sum_{T}\operatorname{cf}(T)\,T)=K_{\overline{X}}^{2}>0
because
-K_{\overline{X}}
is ample. This ends the proof of (
2
).

The inequality (
2
) implies that
K_{X}+D+G=0
for some effective divisor
G
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
. Its fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot(D+G)=-F\cdot K_{X}=2
, so
0pt(X,D)\leqslant F\cdot D\leqslant 2
, which proves
1.3
(a)
. To prove
1.3
(b)
we may assume that
0pt(X,D)=2
, so
F\cdot D=2
and
G
is vertical.

If the chosen
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration has no degenerate fibers then
X
is a Hirzebruch surface and
D
is the negative section, contrary to the fact that
0pt(X,D)=2
. Thus we can assume that the support of
G
is contained in the sum of degenerate fibers. Since
K_{X}\cdot G=-K_{X}\cdot(K_{X}+D)<0
by inequality (
2
), such
G
contains a
(-1)
-curve
G_{0}
. If
|K_{X}+D+G_{0}|=\emptyset
then by Lemma
2.1

D+G_{0}
is a sum of disjoint rational trees, so
G_{0}
meets the chain
D
at most once; and
\kappa(K_{X}+D+G_{0})=\kappa(K_{X}+D)=-\infty
. Thus replacing
(X,D)
by its image after the contraction of
G_{0}
, we can eventually assume that
|K_{X}+D+G_{0}|\neq\emptyset
. Since
|K_{X}+D+G_{0}|=|G_{0}-G|
, we get
G=G_{0}
. For every component
C
of
D+G
we have
\beta_{D+G}(C)=C\cdot(D+G-C)=-C\cdot(K+C)=2
, so
D+G
is circular. It follows that the fiber
F_{G}
containing
G
is columnar.

If
L\neq G
is another
(-1)
-curve then
0=L\cdot(K_{X}+D+G)\geqslant L\cdot D-1
, so
L\cdot D\leqslant 1
, and as before we replace
(X,D)
by its image after the contraction of
L
. Thus we can assume that
F_{G}
is a unique degenerate fiber. Let
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
be a contraction of
F_{G}
to a
0
-curve. Then
\psi_{*}D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is either a
2
-section, or a sum of two non-disjoint
1
-sections on a Hirzebruch surface. It follows that some component of
\psi_{*}D_{\mathrm{hor}}
has positive self-intersection number, so some component of the chain
D
has non-negative self-intersection number. Lemma
2.10
implies that

X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibered, as needed.

∎

As before, we can assume that
(X,D)
is as in Proposition
3.2
. In cases
3.2
(1)
,
(2)
, or
(3.1)
–
(3.3)
, the result follows from Remark
3.4
. Consider case
(3.4)
,
\operatorname{char}{k}\not\in\{2,3,5\}
. Then
X\setminus D
admits a
Platonic

\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fibration over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, see Figure
2(e)
. The construction in
[
MT84a
, §7.5]
shows that there is a finite morphism
\nu\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that the normalized pullback of
X\setminus D
along
\nu
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled, as needed.

Thus for the remaining part of the proof we can assume that
p\coloneqq\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
, and
(X,D)
is as in
3.2
(3.4)
or
(4)
–
(6)
. We will argue as in
[
MT84a
, §7.5]
: first, in Claim
1
we choose a suitable
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
(or, in one case, its blowup
\widetilde{X}
), and next, in Claim
2
, we pull it back by a totally ramified
p
-fold covering
\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We show that the normalized pullback of
X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled, hence
X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled, as needed.

For a log smooth surface
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
with a fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
f\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we introduce the following terminology. For a vertical curve
C
we denote by
\mu(C)
its multiplicity in a fiber. We call a fiber
F

good
if it has exactly one component not contained in
D
, call it
C
, and the following holds:

(i)

every connected component of
F_{\mathrm{red}}-C
meets at most one component of
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
,

(ii)

\mu(C)\in\{1,p\}
.

As we will see, good fibers will
“become”
nondegenerate after taking the covering, cf. Figure
5
. For example, a columnar fiber
satisfies condition
(i)
, so it is good if and only if its unique
(-1)
-curve has multiplicity
p
.

We say that
F
is
bad
if it is not good. We denote by
v
the number of bad fibers, and put
h=\#\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
.

Claim 1
.

There exists a birational morphism
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})\longrightarrow(X,D)
, with
\widetilde{D}
being the reduced total transform of
D
, and a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
f\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that, with the above notation, the following hold.

(a)

Every horizontal component
H
of
\widetilde{D}
is either a
1
-section, or a
p
-section such that
f|_{H}
is totally ramified.

(b)

The horizontal components are disjoint, and
h\leqslant 3
.

(c)

We have either
h\leqslant 1
, or
v\leqslant 1
, or

(h,v)=(2,2)
and one of the bad fibers satisfies condition
(i)
.

We study each case of Proposition
3.2
separately. In case
3.2
(6.2)
with
\overline{Y}
of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
the log surface
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
will be a blowup of
X
at one point, see Figure
5
; in the remaining cases we will take
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})=(X,D)
.

In cases
3.2
(3.4)
,
3.2
(4)
and
3.2
(5)
, the required properties
(a)
–

are satisfied by the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations described in the corresponding statements of

, see Figures

and

. Indeed, in case
3.2
(3.4)

D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two disjoint

-sections, so
(a)
and

hold. The only degenerate fibers are the three columnar ones, and the multiplicities of their
(-1)
-curves are the Platonic triples
\{2,2,n\}
,
\{2,3,3\}
,
\{2,3,4\}
or
\{2,3,5\}
. Since
p\in\{2,3,5\}
, at least one of those multiplicities equals
p
, hence at least one of those fibers is good. Thus we get
(c)
, case
(h,v)=(2,2)
.

In case
3.2
(4)
we have

and
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of one
2
-section
H
such that

is totally ramified, so
(a)
–

hold. Eventually, in case
3.2
(5)

D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
1
section and a
2
-section as above, so
(a)
and
(b)
hold. Part
(c)
holds, too: indeed, Figure

shows that we get

in
3.2
(5.1)
and case
(h,v)=(2,2)
of

in

(5.2)
.

It remains to consider case
3.2
(6)
. Then
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a minimal log resolution of a surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, where
\overline{Y}
is a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, and
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
is an irreducible, cuspidal member, contained in
\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Let
(Y,D_{Y})
be the minimal log resolution of
\overline{Y}
: it is shown in Figures
6
,
9
or
10
. Put
s=\#D_{Y}
. The proper transform
T_{Y}
of
\overline{T}
on
Y
is again an irreducible, cuspidal member of
|-K_{Y}|
, which is disjoint from
D_{Y}
, and by adjunction meets every
(-1)
-curve on
Y
once. Some of these
(-1)
-curves are drawn as dashed lines in Figures
6
,
9
and
10
. Moreover, by Noether’s formula we have
T_{Y}^{2}=K_{Y}^{2}=10-\rho(Y)=9-s
. The log surface
(X,D)
is obtained by resolving the cusp of
T_{Y}
, which replaces
T_{Y}
by a fork
\langle 1;[2],[3],[s-3]\rangle
. The last twig of this fork is the proper transform of
\overline{T}
, which we denote by
T
. The list in
3.2
(6)
shows that
s\geqslant 6
,
the singularity type of
\overline{Y}
is listed in Table
1
, excluding the first row, and the corresponding entry in the last column is
\mathrm{C}
.

We will use the following consequence of the Hurwitz formula
[
Har77
, Corollary IV.2.4]
.

(3)

Let
g\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a morphism of degree
p
, ramified
with ramification indices
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
. If
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(e_{i}-1)\geqslant 2p-1

then
g
is totally ramified.

Assume first that
p=2
. Consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
shown in Figure
6(b)
,
9(g)
or
10
. Since
s\geqslant 6
and
\overline{Y}
is not of type
\mathrm{E}_{s}
, see Table
1
, this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration has
u\geqslant 2
degenerate fibers. Let
f
be its pullback to
X
.
Then
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
2
-section
T
and either: a
1
-section if
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
, see Figure
10
; or a
2
-section if
\overline{Y}
is of type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see Figure
9(g)
; or two
2
-sections if
\overline{Y}
is of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see Figure
6(b)
. In any case, we have
h\leqslant 3
and the components of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
are disjoint, so
(b)
holds. The degenerate fibers of
f
are: the pullback of the fiber passing through the cusp of
T_{Y}
, and pullbacks of the degenerate fibers from
Y
. Let
F
be such a fiber and, in case
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, assume that it is not of type
[1,1]
(the rightmost fiber in Figure
6(b)
). Then
F
has exactly one component not contained in
D
which meets all components of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}-T
, hence
F
is good. Thus
(c)
holds, with
v\leqslant 1
. Moreover,
F
meets each
2
-section
H
in
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in one point, which is therefore a ramification point of
f|_{H}
. Since there are at least three such fibers, by (
3
)
f|_{H}
is totally ramified, so
(a)
holds, too, as needed.

Assume now that
\overline{Y}
is of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
or
2\mathrm{A}_{4}
, see Figure
9(a)
or
9(f)
. Then
p=3
or
5
, respectively, see Table
1
. In each case
D_{Y}
has a connected component
C_{Y}=[(2)_{p-1}]
, and there is a
(-1)
-curve
L_{Y}
such that
L_{Y}\cdot D_{Y}=2
and

meets

once, in a tip. Let

be the preimages of
C_{Y}
,
L_{Y}
of

. Then

supports a good fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The horizontal part of
D
consists of a
p
-section
H_{p}=[2]
in the preimage of

, and a

-section

in the preimage of the cusp of
\overline{T}
, so
(b)
holds. By Lemma
2.6
, every fiber has exactly one component not in

. The connected components of

are: a
(-2)

-curve meeting

, a
(-2)
-curve meeting

, and a chain

which is disjoint from

if

, and meets

once if

is of type

. It follows that the degenerate fibers other than

are supported on chains

and

, so we get condition

, case
(h,v)=(2,2)
. Let
L_{2}
,

be the
(-1)
-curves in those fibers, they have
\mu(L_{2})=2
,
\mu(L_{3})=3
. If
\overline{Y}
is of type

, so

is a

-section, we have

. Thus

is ramified with index

at

,

, and with index at least

at

. By (

)

is totally ramified. Similarly, if
\overline{Y}
is of type

then

is ramified at

with index

, at

with index
\geqslant 3
, at

with index
\geqslant 2
, and at each point of
L_{2}\cap H_{5}
, with index
\geqslant 2
if

, and
\geqslant 3
if
\#L_{2}\cap H_{5}=1
. By (
3
),

is totally ramified, which proves

(a)
.

Consider the remaining cases with

, see Table

and Figure

. We reduce the proof to cases

and

, and

is an isomorphism near each of the remaining fibers, so properties

–

of

It remains to consider the case when

and

, see Figure

. Note that, since the cubic

The linear system

Let
f\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration chosen in Claim

, and let
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
be the normalization of a fiber product of

with a totally ramified covering

given by

.

Claim 2
.

The following hold.

(a)

The only singular points of
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
are Hirzebruch-Jung singularities, isomorphic to the normalization of a hypersurface singularity
\{\tau^{p}=x^{a}y^{b}\}
, where
\{x=0\}
,
\{y=0\}
are local equations of some vertical curves.

(b)

If a vertical curve
C
has multiplicity
\mu(C)\in\{1,p\}
then its preimage
C_{\nu}
is a vertical curve with
\mu(C_{\nu})=1
.

(c)

The horizontal part of the reduced preimage of
D
on
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
consists of
h
disjoint
1
-sections.

This claim follows from a direct local computation, analogous to the one in
[
MT84a
, §7.2]
. Fix a point
z_{\nu}\in\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
, and let
z
be its image on
\widetilde{X}
. If
z
lies on the intersection of two vertical curves, we get a singularity at
z_{\nu}
as described in
(a)
. Assume that
z
lies on exactly one vertical curve, say
C
, and let
c=\mu(C)
. Let
(x,y)
be local coordinates of
\widetilde{X}
at
z
, such that
C=\{x=0\}
. Let
t
and
\tau
be parameters of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
at the images of
z
and
z_{\nu}
. Then
t=ux^{c}
, where
u
is a regular, nonvanishing function on some neighborhood of
z
. Denoting the pullbacks of functions on
\widetilde{X}
to
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
by the same letters, we get
\tau^{p}=t=ux^{c}
. Write
c=p\overline{c}+r
for some integer
r\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
. Then the rational function
\xi\coloneqq\tau x^{-\overline{c}}
satisfies
\xi^{p}=ux^{r}
, hence is regular as
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
is normal. We get
\tau=\xi x^{\overline{c}}
, so
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
is regular at
z_{\nu}
, and if
c\in\{1,p\}
then
\tau
is a local equation of
C_{\nu}
, so
\mu(C_{\nu})=1
, as claimed in
(b)
.
To prove
(c)
we can assume that
z\in H
and
C
is a nondegenerate fiber. If
H
is a
1
-section then we can choose
(x,y)
so that
y
is a local equation of
H
, and then its preimage
\{y=0\}
is a
1
-section. Otherwise, by Claim
1
(a)

H
is a
p
-section and
(C\cdot H)_{z}=p
, so we can choose
(x,y)
in such a way that
H=\{x=y^{p}\}
. Then the total transform of
H
is given by
\{x^{p}=y^{p}\}
, so its (reduced) preimage
\{x=y\}
is a
1
-section, as needed. By Claim
1
(b)
the horizontal components of
D
are disjoint, hence so are their preimages, as needed.

∎

Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{v}
be the bad fibers of
f
. By Claim
1
(c)
we can assume that
F_{2}
satisfies
(i)
. Let
X^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
be the minimal resolution. Let
D^{\prime\prime}
be the reduced total transform of
X^{\prime\prime}
of
(D+F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}
if
v\geqslant 1
and of
D
otherwise. By Claim
2
(c)

D^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of
h
disjoint
1
-sections. Let
(X^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a maximal contraction of superfluous vertical
(-1)
-curves in
D^{\prime\prime}
and its images, such that the components of
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
remain disjoint. Since the rational map
X^{\prime}\dashrightarrow X
restricts to a dominant morphism
X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}\longrightarrow X\setminus D
, it is enough to show that
X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled. If
h=1
this follows from Lemma
2.8
, so we can assume
h\geqslant 2
.

Claim 3
.

For a fiber
F
of
f
, denote by
F^{\prime}
its reduced total transform on
X^{\prime}
. The following hold.

(a)

If
F
is a good fiber then
F^{\prime}
is a
0
-curve not contained in
D^{\prime}
.

(b)

If
v\geqslant 1
then
F_{1}^{\prime}
is a
0
-curve contained in
D^{\prime}
.

(c)

If
v=2
then
F_{2}^{\prime}
is either columnar, see Definition
3.1
(a)
, or a
0
-curve not contained in
D^{\prime}
.

(a)
Let
C
be the unique component of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
off
\widetilde{D}
. Condition
(ii)
and Claim
2
(b)
imply that its preimage
C^{\prime\prime}
on
X^{\prime\prime}
is a curve of multiplicity
1
in a fiber
F^{\prime\prime}
. Hence
F^{\prime\prime}-C^{\prime\prime}
contracts to smooth points, and by condition
(i)
the images of components of
D^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
after this contraction remain disjoint, so
F^{\prime}
is the image of
C^{\prime\prime}
, as needed.

(b)
We have
F_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq D^{\prime}
by the definition of
D^{\prime\prime}
. If
F_{1}^{\prime}
contains a
(-1)
-curve then the latter meets at least two components of
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. By Claims
1
(b)
and
2
(c)
,
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of at most three
1
-sections, so there is at most one
(-1)
-curve in
F_{1}^{\prime}
, and it has multiplicity
1
, which is impossible. Thus
F_{1}^{\prime}=[0]
, as required.

(c)
Since
F_{2}
is bad but satisfies
(i)
, it does not satisfy
(ii)
. By Claim
1
(a)
, this implies that the component of
F_{2}
not contained in
\widetilde{D}
does not meet
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. By Claim
1
(c)
we have
h=2
, so by
(i)
the two horizontal components of
\widetilde{D}
meet
F_{2}
in two connected components of
F_{2}\cap\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{vert}}
. Let
F_{2}^{\prime\prime}
be the total transform of
F_{2}
on
D^{\prime\prime}
. Now
F_{2}^{\prime\prime}\cap D^{\prime\prime}
has two connected components meeting
D^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so they both contain components of multiplicity one. It follows that its image
F_{2}^{\prime}
is columnar or nondegenerate, as needed.

∎

If
v\geqslant 2
then
(h,v)=(2,2)
by Claim
1
(c)
, and Claim
3
shows that
D^{\prime}
is a chain with a
0
-curve. Otherwise Claim
3
implies that
X^{\prime}
is a Hirzebruch surface, and
D^{\prime}
is a sum of
h\geqslant 2
disjoint
1
-sections and at most
1
fiber. In any case,
D^{\prime}
has a twig which is not negative definite, so
X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled by Lemma
2.10
.

∎

Example 3.7

(Proof of the Corollary
1.4
in case
3.2
(6)
with
\overline{Y}
of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
, see Figure
5
)
.

We now follow the above proof more closely in case studied at the end of Claim
1
, that is, when
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is as in
3.2
(6)
, with
\overline{Y}
of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
. The
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
f
from Claim
1
is shown in the top-left part of Figure
5
.
The one in the bottom-right is constructed by taking the normalized pullback
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
of
f
along the morphism
\mathbb{P}^{1}\ni x\mapsto x^{3}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
and resolving the singularities of
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
.
Alternatively, one can first blow up the points of
\widetilde{X}
which will become singularities of
\widetilde{X}_{\nu}
, and then take the normalized pullback. This blowup is described in the bottom-left. The key point is that, as shown in Claim
2
(c)
, after taking the pullback the
3
-sections
T
,
L_{2}
become
1
-sections. Now we can contract degenerate fibers to
0
-curves such that the images of the last three fibers (called
“good”
in the proof) are not contained in the boundary. This way we get
3
horizontal and
1
vertical line on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Restricted to the open part, our map becomes a dominant (non-separable) morphism from a cylinder
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{3\mbox{ points}\}
to
X\setminus D
, so the latter is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled, as needed.

Similar description for Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber spaces in non-special field characteristics is given in
[
MT84a
, §7.5]
.

We end this section with the construction of exceptional log del Pezzo surfaces from Proposition
3.2
(4)
,
(5)
, see Figure
4
. They occur only if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
.

Example 3.8

(Exceptions of height
2
in
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
from Proposition
3.2
(4)
, see Figure
4(a)
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Let
{c}
be a smooth conic, and let
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{\nu}
for some
\nu\geqslant 3
be lines tangent to
{c}
: since
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, they all meet at one point, say
p_{0}\not\in{c}
. Write
\{p_{j}\}={l}_{j}\cap{c}
Blow up at
p_{0}
and, for each
j\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
, at
p_{j}
and its infinitely near point on the proper transform of
{c}
. Denote the resulting morphism by
\varphi\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
, let
C_{Y}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{c}
, let
A_{j}
be the
(-1)
-curve in
\varphi^{-1}(p_{j})
, and let
D_{Y}=\varphi^{*}({c}+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}{l}_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}-A_{0}-\sum_{j=2}^{\nu}A_{j}
. Then the pencil of lines through
p_{0}
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
2
of
(Y,D_{Y})
as in Figure
4(a)
, i.e. such that
D_{Y}=\langle 1;[2],[2],[2\nu-4]\rangle+2\nu\cdot[2]
,
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=[2\nu-4]
is a
2
-section, and all degenerate fibers are supported on chains
[2,1,2]
, where the
(-2)
-tips are contained in
D_{Y}
and the
(-1)
-curve meets
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
once. This way, we get one example of a log surface as in
3.2
(4)
.

The remaining ones are constructed by blowing up further within the fibers, as follows. Write
\{q_{j}\}=C_{Y}\cap A_{j}
. Let
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a composition of blowups over
q_{2},\dots,q_{\mu}
for some
\mu\leqslant\nu
such that each
\psi^{-1}(q_{j})
is a chain with a unique
(-1)
-curve
A_{j}^{\prime}
, and for
j\geqslant 3
this
(-1)
-curve meets
\psi^{-1}_{*}C_{Y}
. Let
D=\psi^{*}(D_{Y}+\sum_{j=2}^{\mu}A_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}-\sum_{j=2}^{\mu}A_{j}^{\prime}
. Then
\rho(X)=\#D
and
(X,D)
is as in Proposition
3.2
(4)
. In particular,
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 2
. If the inequality was strict then by Lemma
2.5
some fiber of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration would be contained in
D
: this is impossible since
D
is snc-minimal and contains no
0
-curves.

Let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the contraction of all components of
D
except the
(-1)
-curve. Then
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is dlt,
\rho(\overline{X})=1
, and using Lemma
2.2
(c)
we compute that
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is ample: indeed, we have
\overline{D}\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=A_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(K_{X}+A_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}T_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}T_{2}^{\prime}+(1-\frac{1}{d})D_{\mathrm{hor}})<0
, where
A_{1}^{\prime}=[1]
is the proper transform of
\overline{D}
,
T_{i}^{\prime}=[2]
are the vertical twigs of
D
meeting
A_{1}^{\prime}
, and
d\geqslant 1
is the discriminant of the twig of
D
containing
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Thus
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is ample, as needed. We note that the underlying surface
\overline{X}
is a del Pezzo surface of rank one and height 2, obtained in
[
PP24
, Lemma 5.17(3)]
for
k_{2}=2
.

Example 3.9

(Exceptions of height
3
in
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
from Proposition
3.2
(5)
, see Figures
4(b)
and
4(c)
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Let
{c}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a conic, and let
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{3}
be lines tangent to
{c}
: as in Example
3.8
, the assumption
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
implies that these lines meet at some point
p_{0}
. Write
\{p_{j}\}={l}_{j}\cap{c}
. Blow up at
p_{0}
, twice at
p_{1},p_{2}
and three times at
p_{3}
, each time on the proper transform of
{l}_{j}
. Next, blow up further over
p_{1}
as follows: once on the proper transform of
{c}
in case
3.2
(5.1)
; and twice on the proper transform of
{l}_{1}
in case
3.2
(5.2)
. Denote the resulting morphism by
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
, let
A_{j}
be the
(-1)
-curve in
\varphi^{-1}(p_{j})
, let
L_{j}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{j}
, and let
D=\varphi^{*}({c}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}-A_{1}-A_{2}-A_{3}
. Then
(X,D)
, together with the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
3
given by the pencil of lines through
p_{0}
, is as in Proposition
3.2
(5)
.

Let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the contraction of
D-A_{0}
. Then
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is dlt;
\rho(\overline{X})=1
; and
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is ample: indeed, by Lemma
2.2
(c)
we have
\overline{D}\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=A_{0}\cdot(K_{X}+A_{0}+\operatorname{cf}(L_{1})\,L_{1}+\frac{1}{2}L_{2}+\frac{2}{3}L_{3})=\operatorname{cf}(L_{1})-\frac{5}{6}<0
, as
\operatorname{cf}(L_{1})=\frac{3}{4}
or
\frac{4}{5}
in case
3.2
(5.1)
and
(5.2)
, respectively.
We note that the underlying surfaces
\overline{X}
are a del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height 2, listed in
[
PP24
, Lemma 5.17]
as follows: case
3.2
(5.1)
corresponds to (2) with parameters
\nu=k_{2}=3
,
k_{3}=l=3
, and case
3.2
(5.2)
to (4) with
\nu=l_{2}=3
,
l_{3}=k_{3}=2
.

We claim that
0pt(X,D)=3
. Suppose the contrary, and fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most
2
. Since
0pt(\overline{X})=2
, the curve
A_{0}
lies in some fiber
F_{0}
. Since
A_{0}
meets
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}
, Lemma
2.4
shows that
F_{0}\cdot(L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3})\geqslant 2\geqslant F_{0}\cdot D_{0}
by assumption, so
F_{0}\cdot(L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3})=2
and
D-(L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3})
is vertical. Pulling back
-K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}={l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
we compute that
-K_{X}=L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3}+2A_{0}-\varepsilon A_{1}
, where
\varepsilon=3,1
in case
3.2
(5.1)
and
(5.2)
, respectively. By adjunction
2=-K_{X}\cdot F_{0}=2-\varepsilon A_{1}\cdot F_{0}
, so
A_{1}
is vertical. Hence the vertical divisor
D-(L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3}+A_{0})+A_{1}
contains a chain
[2,2,1,3,2,2]
in case
3.2
(5.1)
, or a fork
\langle 3;[2],[2,1],[2,2,2]\rangle
in case
3.2
(5.2)
. This is a contradiction, since the latter are not negative semi-definite.

4.
Proof of Proposition
3.2
: description of almost minimal models

In this section we prove Proposition
3.2
. We keep notation from its statement, so
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a minimal dlt surface of negative Kodaira dimension, and
\pi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is its minimal log resolution.

We can and do assume that
X\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
0pt(X,D)\geqslant 2
, as otherwise cases
3.2
(1)
or
3.2
(2)
hold.
We now list some consequences of this assumption and introduce notation for the remaining part of the proof.

Lemma 4.1

(Some elementary properties of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, cf.
[
Miy01
, II.3.14.5]
)
.

The following hold.

(a)

The dlt log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a log del Pezzo surface of rank one, i.e.
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is ample and
\rho(\overline{X})=1
.

(b)

Let
n\coloneqq\#\overline{D}
. Then
n=\#D-\rho(X)+1
.

(c)

The divisor
R\coloneqq\pi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D}
is connected. We denote by
D_{0}
the connected component of
D
containing
R
.

(d)

The exceptional divisor of
\pi
is the sum of
D-D_{0}
and of all maximal admissible twigs of
D_{0}
.

(e)

One of the following holds:

(i)

n\in\{1,2\}
and
D_{0}
is a rational chain,

(ii)

n=1
and
D_{0}
is a rational fork with admissible twigs such that
\delta_{D_{0}}>1
,
\beta_{D_{0}}(R)=3
and
R^{2}\geqslant-1
.

(a)
Since
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is minimal and
\kappa(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=-\infty
, there is a morphism
f\colon\overline{X}\longrightarrow B
of Picard rank one, such that
\dim B\leqslant 1
and
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is
f
-ample, see Section
2B
. We need to prove that
\dim B=0
. Suppose
\dim B=1
. Then a fiber
F
of
f
satisfies

0>F\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=2p_{a}(F)-2+F\cdot\overline{D}

so
p_{a}(F)=0
and
F\cdot\overline{D}\leqslant 1
. Thus
f\circ\pi
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height one with respect to
D
, so
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 1
, a contradiction.

(b)
We have
\overline{D}=\pi_{*}D
, so
\#D-\rho(X)=\#\overline{D}-\rho(\overline{X})=n-1
by
(a)
and by the definition of
n
.

(c)

We have
\rho(\overline{X})=1
, so all components of
\overline{D}
intersect each other. In particular
\overline{D}
is connected. Since
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is dlt, all singular points of
\overline{D}
are snc, and lie in
\overline{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Thus
R\cong\overline{D}

is connected, too, as claimed.

(d)
This follows from the description of peeling for reduced
D
, see
[
Miy01
, II.3.5.2]
or
[
Pal24
, Lemma 4.7(1)]
.

(e)
We have
n=\#\overline{D}>0
by assumption. Let
C
be a component of
R
, and let
T_{1},\dots,T_{v}
be the connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}\pi
meeting
C
. By
(d)
they are admissible twigs of
D
. By Lemma
2.2
(c)
the coefficient in
\pi^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
of the component of
T_{i}
meeting
C
equals
1-\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}
. By
(a)
the divisor
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
is ample, so

\begin{split}0&\ >\pi(C)\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=C\cdot(K_{X}+C)+C\cdot(R-C)+\sum_{i=1}^{v}\left(1-\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}\right)=\\
&=2p_{a}(C)-2+\beta_{R}(C)+v-\sum_{i=1}^{v}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}\geqslant 2p_{a}(C)-2+\beta_{R}(C)+\frac{1}{2}v.\end{split}

It follows that
p_{a}(C)=0
and either
\beta_{R}(C)=1
,
v\leqslant 1
or
\beta_{R}(C)=0
,
v\leqslant 3
. In the first case,
n=2
and
D_{0}
is a rational chain, so
(e)(e.i)
holds. In the second case,
n=1
and either
D_{0}
is a rational chain, so
(e)(e.i)
holds, or
D_{0}
is a fork,
\beta_{D}(R)=3
,
\delta_{D_{0}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}>1
and
R^{2}\geqslant-1
since
D_{0}
is not negative definite, so
(e)(e.ii)
holds.

∎

4A.
Case when
D_{0}
is a chain

In this section we consider case
4.1
(e)(e.i)
, i.e. when the connected component
D_{0}
of
D
containing the proper transform
R
of
\overline{D}
is a rational chain. Our goal is to prove that
(X,D)
is as in Proposition
3.2
(3.1)
–
(3.3)
, so
0pt(X,D)=2
and
X\setminus D
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-ruled by Remark
3.4
. The arguments here are independent of
\operatorname{char}{k}
.

By Lemma
4.1
(e)

\overline{D}
has
n=1
or
2
components. We treat those cases separately in Lemmas
4.3
and
4.2
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume
n=2
. Then
(X,D)
is as in Proposition
3.2
(3.2)
and
D
contains a non-degenerate fiber, see Figures
2(b)
,
2(d)
.

Let
R_{1},R_{2}
be the components of
R
. We have
\pi(R_{i})^{2}>0
since
\rho(\overline{X})=1
. Denoting by
T^{(j)}
the
j
-th component of the twig
T
meeting
R_{i}
, we have
\pi^{*}\pi(R_{i})=R_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{\#T}c_{j}\,T^{(j)}
, where
c_{j}\coloneqq d(\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}T^{(i)})/d(T)<1
. Thus
\pi(R_{i})^{2}=R_{i}^{2}+c_{\#T}<R_{i}^{2}+1
. We conclude that
R_{i}^{2}>\pi(R_{i})^{2}-1>-1
, so
R_{i}^{2}\geqslant 0
.

Suppose
R_{i}^{2}\geqslant 1
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then
\pi(R_{i})^{2}\geqslant R_{i}^{2}\geqslant 1
, and
\pi(R_{i})^{2}=R_{i}^{2}
if and only if
R_{i}
is a tip of
D
. Since
\pi(R_{1})\cdot\pi(R_{2})=R_{1}\cdot R_{2}=1
and
\pi(R_{1})
,
\pi(R_{2})
are numerically proportional, we get
\pi(R_{1})^{2}=\pi(R_{2})^{2}=1
. Hence
D_{0}=R
, so
\pi(R_{i})\subseteq\overline{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
. Lemma
2.11
(a)
gives
\overline{X}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, contrary to our assumptions.

Thus we can assume that
R_{1}^{2}=0
. Consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration induced by
|R_{1}|
. Since by assumption
0pt(X,D)\geqslant 2
, we have
\beta_{D}(R_{1})=2
, and
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, namely
R_{2}
and some
H_{1}\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\pi
.
Write
H_{1}=[m]
for some
m\geqslant 2
. We have
\#D-\rho(X)=n-1=1
by Lemma
4.1
(b)
, so Lemma
2.6
(b)
shows that every fiber other than
R_{1}
has exactly one component not in
D
. If there are no degenerate fibers then
X=\mathbb{F}_{m}
and
D=[m,0,-m]
as in
3.2
(3.2)
, see Figure
2(b)
. Let
F
be a degenerate fiber. Since
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
contains no
(-1)
-curves,
F
has a unique
(-1)
-curve, which therefore has multiplicity at least
2
in
F
, so it does not meet
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
