. Now because
D
has no circular subdivisor, Lemma
2.4
(b)(b.i)
implies that
F
is columnar. Since both
H_{1}
and
R_{2}
are non-branching in
D
, we infer that there is only one degenerate fiber. Let
\eta\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
be the contraction of all components of
F
not meeting
H_{1}
. Then
\eta(R_{2})
is a
1
-section disjoint from the negative section
\eta(H_{1})
, so
\eta(R_{2})^{2}=m
. We have
R_{2}^{2}=\eta(R_{2})^{2}-1=1-m
, so again
D
is as in
3.2
(3.2)
.

∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Assume
n=1
.
Then
(X,D)
is as in Proposition
3.2
(3.1)
–
(3.3)
, see Figures
2(a)
,
2(b)
,
3(e)
.

If
R=D_{0}
then, since
X\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, Lemma
2.11
(a)
implies that
X=\mathbb{F}_{m}
and
3.2
(3.1)
holds. Thus we can assume
D_{0}\neq R
. Put
k\coloneqq R^{2}\geqslant 0
. If
R
is a tip of
D_{0}
then, since
0pt(X,D)\geqslant 2
, we have
R^{2}>0
. In this case, we choose a point
p\in R\setminus\operatorname{Exc}\pi
; otherwise we choose
p\in R\cap\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. In any case, let
\eta\colon(Y,B)\longrightarrow(X,D)
be a composition of
k
blowups at
p
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
R
, where
B=(\eta^{*}D)_{\mathrm{red}}
. Then the linear system of
F_{R}\coloneqq\eta^{-1}_{*}R
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
such that
B_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, say
H_{1},H_{2}
, such that
H_{2}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\eta
. Let
T_{i}
be the twig of
B
meeting
H_{i}
and not containing
F_{R}
.

Case 1:

There is a vertical curve
A\not\subseteq B
, disjoint from
\operatorname{Exc}\eta
, meeting at most one chain
T_{i}+H_{i}
.

We have
A\cdot F_{R}=0
because
A
is vertical. Since
A
meets at most one
T_{i}+H_{i}
, the fiber
F_{R}
lies in a rational twig of
A+B
. By Lemma
2.10

Y\setminus(A+B)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibered, so

\kappa(K_{Y}+A+B)=-\infty
by Iitaka’s easy addition theorem
[
Miy01
, II.1.13.1]
, cf.
[
Fuj82
, Corollary 6.14]
.

Put
V=\eta(A)
. Since
A
is disjoint from
\operatorname{Exc}\eta
, the sum
D+V
is snc. Moreover,
\kappa(K_{X}+D+V)=\kappa(K_{Y}+B+A)=-\infty
, and
0pt(X,D+V)\geqslant 0pt(X,D)\geqslant 2
. Let
(\overline{X}^{\prime},\overline{D}^{\prime})
be a minimal model of
(X,D+V)
: it is a dlt log del Pezzo surface of rank one, see Lemma
4.1
(a)
. Let
\psi\colon(X,D+V)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be the induced morphism onto its minimal log resolution. We have
\#\overline{D}^{\prime}\geqslant\#(D+V)-(\rho(X)-\rho(\overline{X}^{\prime}))=n+1=2
. By Lemma
4.1
(e)
we have
\#\overline{D}^{\prime}=2
, so
\operatorname{Exc}\psi\subseteq D+V
. Lemma
4.2
shows that
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is as in Figures
2(b)
,
2(d)
.

In particular,
D^{\prime}
has two components with a nonnegative self-intersection number, say
F^{\prime}=[0]
and
H^{\prime}=[1-m]
for some
m\geqslant 2
. Since
D+V
has exactly one such, namely
R
, we infer that
F^{\prime}
or
H^{\prime}
contains a base point
p
of
\psi^{-1}
. In particular,
\psi\neq\mathrm{id}
. Since
\operatorname{Exc}\psi\subseteq D+V
and
D
has no
(-1)
-curves, it follows that
V
is a
(-1)
-curve, and
\{p\}=\psi(V)
is the unique base point of
\psi^{-1}
.

We have
\psi^{-1}_{*}(F^{\prime}+H^{\prime})=R+C
for some component
C
of
D-R
. Then
C^{2}\leqslant 0
, so
p\in\psi(C)
, and
C
either lies in an admissible twig of
D_{0}
, or in a connected component of
D-D_{0}
which is an admissible chain or fork. In the latter case,
\{p\}=F^{\prime}\cap H^{\prime}
and
D
is as in
3.2
(3.1)
, see Figures
3(a)
,
3(b)
.
Consider the former case. If
p\in F^{\prime}
then
(X,D)
is as in
3.2
(3.2)
, see Figures
3(c)
,
3(d)
. Assume
p\in H^{\prime}\setminus F^{\prime}
. If
\rho(X^{\prime})>2
then
(X,D)
is as in
3.2
(3.3)
, see Figure
3(e)
; and if
\rho(X^{\prime})=2
then
(X,D)
is as in
3.2
(3.1)
with
(\nu,m)=(2,2)
, see Figure
3(b)
.

Case 2:

Every vertical curve
A\not\subseteq B
meets either
\operatorname{Exc}\eta
or
T_{i}+H_{i}
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Since
n=1
, Lemma
4.1
(b)
gives
\#B-\rho(Y)=\#D-\rho(X)=n-1=0
. By Lemma
2.5
, there is exactly one fiber
F
with two components not in
B
. Denote those components by
A_{1},A_{2}
.

If each
A_{j}
meets
T_{i}+H_{i}
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
then
F
satisfies
F\cdot B_{\mathrm{hor}}>2
or has a circular subdivisor, which is impossible. Therefore, say,
A_{1}
meets
\operatorname{Exc}\eta
. In particular,
\eta\neq\mathrm{id}
. Recall that
\operatorname{Exc}\eta=[1,(2)_{k-1}]\subseteq H_{1}+T_{1}
, and
H_{1}
is the first tip of
\operatorname{Exc}\eta
. Our assumption implies that both
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
meet
H_{1}+T_{1}
. If one of them, say
A_{1}
, meets
H_{1}
then
F\cdot H_{1}\geqslant(A_{1}+T_{1})\cdot H_{1}\geqslant 2
, which is false. Hence
0\neq T_{1}\subseteq F
and both
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
meet
T_{1}
.

Suppose
F^{\prime}\neq F
is another degenerate fiber. Then
F^{\prime}
has only one component not in
D
, which is its unique
(-1)
-curve. Since
B
has no circular subdivisors, Lemma
2.4
(b)
implies that
F^{\prime}
is columnar. In particular,
F^{\prime}
contains
T_{1}+T_{2}
, a contradiction since
T_{1}\subseteq F
. Thus
F
is the unique degenerate fiber.

We can assume that
A_{1}
is a
(-1)
-curve. Indeed, if
A_{2}
is the unique
(-1)
-curve in
F
then by Lemma
2.4
(b)

T_{1}+A_{1}
meets the
1
-section
H_{1}
in a tip, so
T_{1}\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\eta
. In particular,
A_{2}
meets
\operatorname{Exc}\eta
, too, so interchanging the roles of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
we can guarantee that
A_{1}
is a
(-1)
-curve, as needed.

Let
T
be the component of
T_{1}
meeting
A_{1}
, so
T
lies in a
(-2)
-chain
T_{1}^{\eta}\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\eta-H_{1}
. Since
A_{1}+T_{1}^{\eta}
is a proper subdivisor of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
, it is negative definite, so
T
is one of the tips of
T_{1}^{\eta}
.

Assume that
T
meets
H_{1}
. Then after the contraction of
A_{1}
,
T
becomes a
(-1)
-curve of multiplicity one in the image of
F
, hence a tip of that fiber. Thus
A_{1}
is a tip of
F
. It follows that
\widetilde{F}\coloneqq F-A_{1}+H_{1}
is a fiber of another
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
, such that the vertical part of
B
is contained in
\widetilde{F}
; and the horizontal part of
B
consists of two sections:
F_{R}
and
H_{2}
. Lemma
2.5
implies that
\widetilde{F}
is the only degenerate fiber. Since
\operatorname{Exc}\eta\subseteq\widetilde{F}_{\mathrm{red}}
, this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration is a pullback of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
. The fiber
\eta_{*}\widetilde{F}
has a unique
(-1)
-curve, namely
\eta(A_{2})
, and meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in different connected components of
D\cap\eta_{*}\widetilde{F}
. Lemma
2.4
(b)
implies that
\eta_{*}\widetilde{F}
is columnar.
Let
\upsilon\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
be the contraction of all vertical curves not meeting
H_{2}
. Then
\upsilon(H_{2})
is the negative section, and
\upsilon(F_{R})
is a
1
-section meeting
\upsilon(H_{1})
once, so
\upsilon(H_{1})^{2}=m+2
and thus
3.2
(3.1)
holds, see Figure
2(c)
.

Assume now that
T
does not meet
H_{1}
. We have
\eta(A_{1})=[0]
, so
\eta(A_{1})\cdot D\geqslant 0pt(X,D)\geqslant 2
by assumption. Suppose
T_{1}\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\eta
, so
T
is a tip of
B
. Then the above inequality implies that
A_{1}
meets a component
V
of
F
contained in
B-T_{1}
. Then after contraction of
A+T_{1}\subseteq F
, the image
H_{1}
meets the images of two components of
F
, namely
V
and
A_{2}
, so
H_{1}\cdot F\geqslant 2
, a contradiction.

Thus
T_{1}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\eta
, so
\beta_{B}(T)\geqslant 2
. After the contraction of
A_{1}
the image of
T
becomes a
(-1)
-curve, so it is not branching in the image of
F
. It follows that
\beta_{B}(T)=2
and
A_{1}\cdot B=1
. Thus
\eta(A_{1})=[0]
meets
D
in the common point of
R
and some component
H
of
\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. Write
H=[m]
. Consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration induced by
|\eta(A_{1})|
. The horizontal part of
D
consists of two
1
-sections:
R
and
H
. Lemma
2.5
implies that every degenerate fiber has only one
(-1)
-curve, hence is columnar and meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in a tip of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
. Since
H
meets at most one component of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
, there is at most one such fiber. Let
\upsilon\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
be the contraction of all vertical curves which are disjoint from
H
. As before, we infer that
\upsilon_{*}D_{\mathrm{hor}}=[m,-2-m]
and
3.2
(3.1)
holds.
∎

4B.
Case when
D_{0}
is a fork: elementary properties

By Lemma
4.1
(e)
and the results of Section
4A
, to prove Proposition
3.2
we can and do assume that the connected component
D_{0}
of
D
containing
R\coloneqq\pi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D}
is a fork, with
R
as a branching component, and admissible twigs
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
satisfying
\delta_{D_{0}}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}>1
. Out aim is to prove that
(X,D)
is a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space as in Proposition
3.2
(3.4)
, or, if
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
, as in one of the exceptional cases
3.2
(4)
–
(6)
.

In case
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
this assertion is proved by Miyanishi and Tsunoda in
[
MT84a
]
, see
[
Miy01
, §II.5]
for a self-contained account. Below, we essentially follow the same path as in loc. cit., simplifying and completing some arguments to cover the case of arbitrary characteristic. We compare these approaches in Remark
4.11
.
Most of the proof is independent of
\operatorname{char}{k}
, the dependence appears only at the very end of Section
4D
.

An important ingredient of the proof is an application of a two-ray game, which can be summarized as follows. We extract some exceptional divisor
\widecheck{T}
over
\overline{X}
, see
[
Kol13
, 1.39]
, getting a log terminal surface
\widecheck{X}
of rank two, such that one extremal ray of
\operatorname{NE}(\widecheck{X})
is spanned by
\widecheck{T}
. It follows from the cone theorem, see Lemma
4.4
below, that the other ray of
\operatorname{NE}(\widecheck{X})
is spanned by a curve
\widecheck{A}
which can be contracted by a morphism of relative Picard rank one. The proof proceeds by analyzing this contraction.

We call the curve
\widecheck{A}
the
dual of
\widecheck{T}
. We now list its basic properties.

Lemma 4.4

(The dual curve)
.

Let
T
be a component of
D-R
. Let
\widecheck{A}
be the curve dual to
T
, and let
A
be its proper transform on
X
. Then one of the following holds.

(a)

\widecheck{A}^{2}=0
, and for a general choice of
\widecheck{A}
we have
A=[0]
,
A
is disjoint from
D-R-T
and meets
T
.

(b)

\widecheck{A}^{2}<0
, and
A
is a
(-1)
-curve such that
A+D-R-T
is negative definite.

Write
\pi=\pi_{T}\circ\tau_{T}
, where
\pi_{T}\colon\widecheck{X}\longrightarrow\overline{X}
is the extraction of
T
, so
\tau_{T}\colon X\longrightarrow\widecheck{X}
is the contraction of
D-R-T
. Put
\widecheck{R}=\tau_{T}(R)
,
\widecheck{T}=\tau_{T}(T)
and
\widecheck{D}=\widecheck{R}+\operatorname{cf}(T)\widecheck{T}
, so
\pi_{T}^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=K_{\widecheck{X}}+\widecheck{D}
. We have
\widecheck{R}\cdot(K_{\widecheck{X}}+\widecheck{D})=\overline{D}\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})<0
, so
K_{\widecheck{X}}+\widecheck{D}
is not nef; and
\widecheck{T}\cdot(K_{\widecheck{X}}+\widecheck{D})=\widecheck{T}\cdot\pi_{T}^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=0
, so
\widecheck{T}
is not
(K_{\widecheck{X}}+\widecheck{D})
-negative. By the cone theorem, see
[
Fuj21
, Theorem 5.5]
, there is a
(K_{\widecheck{X}}+\widecheck{D})
-negative curve
\widecheck{A}\neq\widecheck{T}
which can be contracted by a morphism
\varphi\colon\widecheck{X}\longrightarrow B
of relative Picard rank one. In particular,
\widecheck{A}^{2}\leqslant 0
. Let
A\coloneqq(\tau_{T}^{-1})_{*}\widecheck{A}
be the proper transform of
\widecheck{A}
on
X
.

Consider the case
\widecheck{A}^{2}=0
, and take for
\widecheck{A}
a general fiber of
\varphi
. Then
\widecheck{A}\not\subseteq\widecheck{D}
and
\widecheck{A}
does not pass through
\operatorname{Sing}\widecheck{X}
, so
A^{2}=\widecheck{A}^{2}=0
and
A\cdot T=\widecheck{A}\cdot\widecheck{T}
. Since
\rho(\widecheck{X})=2
and
\widecheck{T}
is not a fiber, we have
\widecheck{A}\cdot\widecheck{T}>0
, as needed.

Consider the case
\widecheck{A}^{2}<0
. Then
A+\operatorname{Exc}\tau_{T}=A+D-R-T
is negative definite, in particular
A^{2}<0
. We have
0>\widecheck{A}\cdot(K_{\widecheck{X}}+\widecheck{D})\geqslant A\cdot(K_{X}+R)
, so either
A\neq R
and thus
A\cdot K_{X}<0
, so
A
is a
(-1)
-curve; or
A=R
and
A
is a
(-1)
-curve by Lemma
4.1
(e)(e.ii)
, as needed.

∎

We now list some simple conditions which imply that
(X,D)
is a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space.

Lemma 4.5

(Sufficient condition for a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space)
.

Assume that
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, one of which is
R
. Then the other section is a branching component of
D-D_{0}
; and
(X,D)
is a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space as in Proposition
3.2
(3.4)
, see Figure
2(e)
.

Put
H=D_{\mathrm{hor}}-R
. By assumption, at least two twigs of
D_{0}
are vertical, say
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq D_{\mathrm{vert}}
. Let
F_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
be the fiber containing
T_{i}
. By Lemma
2.6
(b)

F_{i}
has exactly one component not in
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
, say
L_{i}
, which is its unique
(-1)
-curve. By Lemma
2.4
(a)

F_{i}
is a chain of type
[T_{i}^{*},1,T_{i}]
, where
T_{i}^{*}
is a connected component of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
meeting
H
in the last tip, and not meeting
R
. If
H\subseteq T_{3}
then
T_{3}
contains two connected components as above, namely
T_{1}^{*}
and
T_{2}^{*}
, which is impossible. Therefore,
H\not\subseteq D_{0}
. In particular,
T_{3}
is vertical, too, so
H
meets three twigs of
D_{0}
, namely
T_{1}^{*}
,
T_{2}^{*}
and
T_{3}^{*}
. The connected component of
D
containing
H
contracts to a log terminal singularity, so it is an admissible fork
H+\sum_{i}T_{i}^{*}
. If
F
is a degenerate fiber other than the three fibers
[T_{i}^{*},1,T_{i}]
found above, then by Lemma
2.6
(b)

F
has a unique
(-1)
-curve, meeting both
1
-sections
H
and
R
: this is impossible as such
(-1)
-curve has multiplicity at least
2
in
F
. We conclude that all the remaining fibers are smooth, so
(X,D)
is a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space, see Proposition
3.2
(3.4)
, as needed.

∎

The condition
\delta_{D_{0}}>1
implies that at least one of the twigs
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
is of type
[2]
, cf. Section
2A
. Say that
T_{3}=[2]
and put
T\coloneqq T_{3}
.

Lemma 4.6

(Bounds on
\operatorname{cf}(C)
)
.

Let
C
be a component of
D
, and let
\operatorname{cf}(C)
be the coefficient of
C
in
\pi^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})-K_{X}
, see formula (
1
). Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
\operatorname{cf}(R)=1
and
\operatorname{cf}(C)\in[0,1)
for every component
C
of
D-R
.

(b)

We have
\operatorname{cf}(T)=\frac{1}{2}
.

(c)

Let
C
be a component of
T_{i}
. Then
\operatorname{cf}(C)\in[\frac{1}{d(T_{i})},1-\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}]
and
\operatorname{cf}(C)=1-\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}
if and only if
C
meets
R
.

(d)

Let
A
be a
(-1)
-curve on
X
. Then
\sum_{C}\operatorname{cf}(C)\,C\cdot A<1
. In particular, if
A\neq R
then
A
is disjoint from
R
.

Part
(a)
follows from the fact that
R=\pi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D}
, and
\pi
is a minimal log resolution of a dlt log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
. Parts
(b)
,
(c)
follow from Lemma
2.2
(c)
. Part
(d)
follows from ampleness of
-(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
: indeed, since
A\not\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\pi
we have
0>\pi(A)\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=-1+\sum_{C}\operatorname{cf}(C)\,C\cdot A
by adjunction.

∎

4C.
Case when
D_{0}
is a fork and
R^{2}\geqslant 0

Recall from Lemma
4.1
(e)
that
R^{2}\geqslant-1
. In this section we settle the case when the inequality is strict.

Lemma 4.7
.

Assume that
R^{2}\geqslant 0
. Then
(X,D)
is a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space, see Proposition
3.2
(3.4)
.

Let
A
be the proper transform of the curve dual to
T
, see Lemma
4.4
. Suppose
A^{2}=0
, so case
4.4
(a)
holds. Consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration induced by
|A|
. Since
A
is disjoint from
D-R-T
, the twigs
T_{1},T_{2}
are vertical. Since by assumption
R^{2}\geqslant 0
, it follows that
R
is horizontal. We have
0>\pi(A)\cdot K_{\overline{X}}=A\cdot(K_{X}+R+\frac{1}{2}T)
, so
A\cdot(R+\frac{1}{2}T)<2
. Thus
A\cdot R=A\cdot T=1
, so
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of
1
-sections
R
and
T
; contrary to Lemma
4.5
.

Thus case
4.4
(b)
holds, so
A=[1]
and
A+D-R-T
is negative definite. Since
A+D-R-T
is not negative definite,
A
meets
T
. Thus by Lemma
4.6
(d)
we have
A\cdot R=0
,
A\cdot T=1
and
\sum_{C\neq T}\operatorname{cf}(C)\,C\cdot A<\frac{1}{2}
.

By Lemma
4.6
(c)
the coefficient of each component of
T_{i}
equals at least
\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}
. The above inequality and the condition
\delta_{D_{0}}>1
imply that
A
does not meet both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
. Say that
A\cdot T_{1}=0
. We consider two cases.

Case 1:

A
is disjoint from
T_{2}
.
Since
A+(D-R)
is not negative definite,
A
meets
D-D_{0}
.

Suppose that
A
meets no
(-2)
-curve in
D-T
. Then every component
C
of
D-D_{0}
meeting
A
has self intersection number at most
-3
. Lemmas
2.3
and
2.2
(a)
yield
\operatorname{cf}(C)\geqslant\operatorname{cf}_{C}(C)=1-\frac{2}{-C^{2}}\geqslant\frac{1}{3}
, so Lemma
4.6
(d)
implies that
A
meets
D-T
once, in a
(-3)
-curve
C
such that
\operatorname{cf}(C)<\frac{1}{2}
. Applying Lemma
2.3
we infer from the latter inequality that the connected component of
D
containing
C
is a chain
[3,2,\dots,2]
. But then
A+(D-R)
is negative definite, a contradiction.

Thus
A
meets some
(-2)
-curve in
D-D_{0}
, call it
C
. Since
A+(D-R-T)
is negative definite,
C
and
T
are the only
(-2)
-curves in
D
meeting
A
; and if
C
is not a tip of
D
then
C
meets two components of
D
of self-intersection at most
-3
. In the latter case, by Lemma
2.3
the coefficient of
C
equals at least the coefficient of the middle component in the chain
[3,2,3]
, which equals
\frac{1}{2}
by Lemma
2.2
(a)
: thus
\operatorname{cf}(C)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
, contrary to Lemma
4.6
(d)
. We conclude that
C
is a
(-2)
-tip of
D-D_{0}
.

If
A\cdot D=2
then the linear system
|C+2A+T|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration like in Lemma
4.5
, as needed. Suppose
A\cdot D\geqslant 3
, so
A
meets some component
V
of
D-D_{0}-C
. Recall that since
A+(D-R-T)
is negative definite, we have
V^{2}\leqslant-3
. By Lemma
4.6
(d)
we have
\operatorname{cf}(V)<\frac{1}{2}
, so as before we see that such
V
is unique, and it is a tip of a connected component
D_{V}=[3,2,\dots,2]
of
D
. Lemma
4.6
(d)
implies that
\operatorname{cf}(C)<\frac{1}{2}-\operatorname{cf}(V)\leqslant\frac{1}{6}
.

Let
D_{C}
be the connected component
D_{C}
of
D
containing
C
. Suppose
C\neq D_{C}
. If
D_{C}=D_{V}
then, since
C
is a tip of
D
, we have
\operatorname{cf}(C)+\operatorname{cf}(V)=1
, which is impossible. Now, since
D_{C}\neq D_{V}
and
D_{C}+A+D_{V}
is negative definite, the component of
D_{C}
meeting
C
is not a
(-2)
-curve, so by Lemma
2.3

\operatorname{cf}(C)\geqslant\frac{1}{5}>\frac{1}{6}
; a contradiction.

Thus
C=D_{C}
. The linear system
|C+2A+T|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
1
-section
R
and a
2
-section
V
. By Lemma
2.6
(b)
each degenerate fiber contains exactly one component off
D
, which is its unique
(-1)
-curve. Since such a
(-1)
-curve cannot meet a
1
-section
R
, we conclude that there are exactly three such fibers, each containing exactly one twig of
D_{0}
.
There is a morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
contracting those fibers to
0
-curves which is an isomorphism near
R
. Then
\psi(R)
is a
1
-section with a nonnegative self-intersection number; and
\psi(V)
is a
2
-section disjoint from
\psi(R)
. This contradicts numerical properties of the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
. Indeed, denoting by
\Xi_{m}=[m]
a negative section, we see that
\psi(R)-\Xi_{m}
and
\psi(V)-2\Xi_{m}
are numerically equivalent to some multiples of a fiber, hence
0=(\psi(R)-\Xi_{m})\cdot(\psi(V)-2\Xi_{m})=-\Xi_{m}\cdot(\psi(V)+2\psi(R))-2m\leqslant 0
, so
m=0
, i.e.
\mathbb{F}_{m}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
\psi(V)\cdot\Xi_{0}=0
, so
\psi(V)
consists of two horizontal lines, a contradiction.

Case 2:

A
meets some component
C
of
T_{2}
.

By Lemma
4.6
(d)
,
(b)
we have
\operatorname{cf}(C)<1-\operatorname{cf}(T)=\frac{1}{2}
, so by Lemma
4.6
(c)

C
does not meet
R
, in particular
\#T_{2}\geqslant 2
.

Suppose
C
is a
(-2)
-tip of
D
.
If
A
meets a component
V
of
D-D_{0}
then the fact that
\#T_{2}\geqslant 2
and
V+A+T_{2}
is negative definite implies that
V^{2}\leqslant-4
, so
\operatorname{cf}(V)\geqslant\operatorname{cf}_{V}(V)=\frac{1}{2}
, which is impossible by Lemma
4.6
(d)
. Thus
A
meets
D
only in
C
and
T
. The linear system
|C+2A+T|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, namely
R
and the second component of
T_{2}
, a contradiction with Lemma
4.5
.

Suppose
T_{1}\neq[2]
. Since
\delta_{D_{0}}>1
, we have
d(T_{2})\leqslant 5
. Since
\#T_{2}\geqslant 2
we get
T_{2}=[2,3]
,
[(2)_{k}]
or
[3,2]
. In the first case
C
is a
(-2)
-tip of
D
, and the same is true in the second case as
A+T_{2}
is negative definite: thus the previous paragraph shows that these cases cannot occur. Eventually, if
T_{2}=[3,2]
then
\operatorname{cf}(C)=\frac{3}{5}>\frac{1}{2}
, a contradiction with Lemma
4.6
(d)
.

Thus
T_{1}=[2]
. Case
1
above shows that, interchanging the roles of
T_{1}
and
T
if needed, we can assume that there is a
(-1)
-curve
A_{1}
meeting
D
in
T_{1}
and some component
C_{1}
of
T_{2}
which is disjoint from
R
, and not a
(-2)
-tip of
D
. Let
\eta\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X
be a composition of
R^{2}
blowups at
R\cap T_{2}
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
R
, let
D^{\prime}=(\eta^{*}D)_{\mathrm{red}}
and let
A,A_{1}^{\prime},T,T_{1}^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}
be the proper transforms of
A,A_{1},T,T_{1}
and
R
. Then
|R^{\prime}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X^{\prime}
such that
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of three
1
-sections, namely
T^{\prime},T_{1}^{\prime}
and the component
H
of
T_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\eta^{*}T_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}
meeting
R^{\prime}
. The
1
-section
H
is not the proper transform of
C
or
C_{1}
, since the latter are disjoint from
R
. Thus
T_{2}^{\prime}-H+A^{\prime}+A_{1}^{\prime}
is contained in some degenerate fiber
F
. Since the
(-1)
-curves
A,A_{1}^{\prime}
meet
1
-sections
T,T_{1}^{\prime}
, they have multiplicity
1
in
F
, and by Lemma
2.5

F-A^{\prime}-A_{1}^{\prime}
is contained in
D
, so
F=A+(T_{2}^{\prime}-H)+A_{1}^{\prime}=[1,2,\dots,2,1]
. Thus
C
or
C_{1}
is a
(-2)
-tip of
D
, a contradiction.
∎

4D.
Case when
D_{0}
is a fork and
R^{2}=-1

By Lemmas
4.1
(e)
and
4.7
above we can assume
R^{2}=-1
. We study several cases, depending on the shape of
D_{0}
. The core of the argument is still independent of
\operatorname{char}{k}
, but once we restrict the possible shapes of
D
it will be clear that some of them occur only for special values of
\operatorname{char}{k}
.

We have
D_{0}=\langle 1,T_{1},T_{2},[2]\rangle
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
we denote by
C_{i}
the component of
T_{i}
meeting
R
.

Lemma 4.8

(Case
D_{0}=\langle 1;[\dots,2],T_{2},[2]\rangle
)
.

Assume
R=[1]
and
C_{1}=[2]
. Then
(X,D)
is either a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space, see
3.2
(3.4)
, or
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
(X,D)
is as in
3.2
(4)
or
(5.1)
, see Figures
4(a)
,
4(b)
.

Put
F_{1}=C_{1}+2R+T
, so
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
. The linear system
|C_{1}+2R+T|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
2
-section
C_{2}
and, if
T_{1}\neq[2]
, a
1
-section
H\subseteq T_{1}
meeting
C_{1}
. If
T_{1}=[2]
put
H=0
. Let
F_{2},\dots,F_{\nu}
be the remaining degenerate fibers; they are not contained in
D
.

We contract these fibers to
0
-curves in such a way that the image of
C_{2}
is smooth and disjoint from the image of
H
: to do this, we contract inductively
(-1)
-curves in the images of the fiber which meet
H+C_{2}
at most once, and if there is no such then the fiber is smooth. Since the image of
C_{2}
is a smooth and rational
2
-section on a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
, numerical properties of
\mathbb{F}_{m}
imply that
m\in\{0,1\}
, and we can choose the last contraction so that
m=1
. Now the negative section is disjoint from the image of
C_{2}
, hence equal to
H
if
H\neq 0
. Contracting it we get a morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that
{c}\coloneqq\psi(C_{2})
is a conic, and
{l}_{i}\coloneqq\psi_{*}F_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
are lines meeting at some point
p_{0}\not\in{c}
.

Claim
.

For each
i\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
, the line
{l}_{i}
is tangent to
{c}
at some point
p_{i}\neq p_{0}
.

Suppose
{l}_{2}
meets
{c}
in two points, say
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
. If neither of them is a base point of
\psi^{-1}
then
L_{2}\cdot C_{2}=2
, so
L_{2}
cannot be a component of
D
, hence
L_{2}=[1]
by Lemma
2.6
(a)
, and
1>2\operatorname{cf}(C_{2})\geqslant 1
by Lemma
4.6
(d)
,
(c)
; a contradiction. Say that
q_{2}
is a base point of
\psi^{-1}
, and let
A_{2}\subseteq F_{2}
be a
(-1)
-curve in
\psi^{-1}(q_{2})
.

Suppose
A_{2}
is the unique
(-1)
-curve in
F_{2}
. Then
L_{2}
is the component of
T_{2}
meeting
C_{2}
, so
(F_{2}-L_{2})\cdot C_{2}=1
. Since
T_{2}
lies in a twig of
D
and meets both
R
and
L_{2}
, it follows that
C_{2}
meets
F_{2}-L_{2}
off
D
, in a component of multiplicity one, but there is no such, a contradiction.

Thus by Lemma
2.6
(b)
we have
H\neq 0
, the fiber
F_{2}
has exactly two
(-1)
-curves, and each fiber
F_{j}
for
j\geqslant 3
has exactly one. Let
A_{0}
be a
(-1)
-curve in
\psi^{-1}(p_{0})
, so since
H\neq 0
, we have
A_{0}\subseteq F_{j}
for some
j\geqslant 2
. If, say,
A_{0}\subseteq F_{3}
then
A_{3}
is a unique
(-1)
-curve in
F_{3}
, so
L_{3}\subseteq D
and
{l}_{3}\cap{c}
is not a base point of
\psi^{-1}
, which means that
L_{3}\cdot C_{3}=2
, which is impossible for two components of
D
. Hence
A_{0}\subseteq F_{2}
, so the
(-1)
-curves in
F_{2}
are
A_{0}
and
A_{2}
. In particular,
L_{2}
is a component of
D
, and
q_{1}
is not a base point of
\psi^{-1}
, so
L_{2}
meets
C_{2}
. Now
A_{2}
meets the twig
T_{2}
in
C_{2}
and in some component of
T_{2}-C_{2}\subseteq L_{2}
, say
C
. By Lemma
4.6
(d)
,
(c)
we have
1>\operatorname{cf}(C_{2})+\operatorname{cf}(C)\geqslant 1
, a contradiction.

∎

Assume
\nu\leqslant 2
. Let let
s_{j}
be the number of blowups within
\psi
which are not isomorphisms near the common point of the images of
F_{j}
and
C_{2}
. Since
\sum_{j}s_{j}={c}^{2}-C_{2}^{2}\geqslant 6
, we have
\nu=2
and
s_{2}\geqslant 4
. Thus the pencil of conics tangent to
{c}
at
p_{1},p_{2}
pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
as in Lemma
4.5
, with
1
-sections being the proper transforms of second exceptional curves over
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. We conclude that
(X,D)
is a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space.

Thus we can assume
\nu\geqslant 3
; note that in this case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Say that the chain
T_{2}-C_{2}
lies in a fiber
F_{2}
.

Assume that
T_{1}=[2]
, so
H=0
. Then by Lemma
2.6
(b)
each
F_{i}
has exactly one
(-1)
-curve, say
A_{i}
, and hence meets
C_{2}
either in
A_{i}
or, in case
i=2
, in the chain
T_{2}-C_{2}
. It follows from Lemma
2.4
(b)
that each
F_{i}
is as described in
3.2
(4)
, which ends the proof in case
T_{2}=[2]
.

Assume now that
T_{1}\neq[2]
, so
C_{1}
meets a
1
-section
H
. Interchanging the roles of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
if needed, we can assume that
T_{2}\neq[2]
, too.
Suppose that
T_{2}\neq C_{2}
, and the fiber
F_{2}
containing the chain
T_{2}-C_{2}
has only one
(-1)
-curve, say
A_{2}
. Then
F_{2}
has no component of multiplicity
1
off
D
, so it meets both
H
and
C_{2}
in components of
D
, of multiplicity
1
and
2
, respectively. Thus
(F_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}
is a sum of
A_{2}
and chains
(T_{1}-C_{1}-H)
,
(T_{2}-C_{2})
, containing tips of multiplicity
1
and
2
in
F
, respectively. It follows that
(F_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}=[3,1,2,2]
or
\langle k;[2],[2],[(2)_{k-2},1]\rangle
. In either case we get
\delta_{D_{0}}<1
, a contradiction.

Thus we can assume that
F_{2}\supseteq T_{2}-C_{2}
has at least two
(-1)
-curves. By Lemma
2.6
(b)

F_{2}
has exactly two
(-1)
-curves, and each
F_{j}
for
j\geqslant 3
has exactly one. In particular, for
j\geqslant 3
the component of multiplicity
1
of
F_{j}
meeting
H
is contained in
D
. Since there is at most one such component, we conclude that
\nu=3
, and the chain
T_{1}-C_{1}-H
is nonzero and contained in
F_{3}
. Since
F_{3}
does not contain
T_{2}-C_{2}
, it meets
C_{2}
in a
(-1)
-curve. It follows that
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
or
\langle 2;[1],[2],[2]\rangle
, but in the latter case
\#T_{1}=5
, which together with the assumption
T_{2}\neq[2]
leads to a contradiction with the condition
\delta_{D_{0}}>1
. Thus
T_{1}
is a
(-2)
-chain
[2,2,2]
. The inequality
\delta_{D_{0}}>1
implies that
T_{2}=[2,2]
or
[3]
. In the first case, we conclude by interchanging the roles of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.

Consider the second case. Then
D=\langle 1;[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2},2^{|3|}],[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]^{|3|},[2]\rangle+[2]^{|3|}+V
, where
H
is the middle
(-2)
-curve in
T_{1}
,
C_{2}=T_{2}
, and the
(-1)
-curve in
F_{3}
meets
D
in the components decorated by
|3|
. Moreover,
V
is a sum of admissible chains and forks such that
(F_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}=U+V+U^{\prime}
for some
(-1)
-curves
U,U^{\prime}
. One of those
(-1)
-curves, say
U
, meets the
1
-section
H
, so
U
is a tip of
F_{2}
. Now
\psi
is the contraction of
U+H
and all vertical curves not meeting
H
. As before, let
s_{j}
be the number of blowups within
\psi
which are not isomorphisms near the common point of the images of
F_{j}
and
C_{2}
. Then
s_{1}=s_{2}=2
, so
s_{3}={c}^{2}-C_{2}^{2}-s_{1}-s_{2}=3
. It follows that
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}=\langle 2;[1,3],[1],[2]\rangle
, where the first
(-1)
-curve meets
H
, the second
(-1)
-curve meets
C_{2}
, and the
(-3)
-curve equals
L_{3}
. Thus
D
is of the same type as in
3.2
(5.1)
. The
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration described there is induced by the pencil of conics tangent to
{c}
at
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
; its degenerate fibers are supported on
T_{1}+U=\langle 2;[2],[2],[1]\rangle
,
C_{2}+U^{\prime}+V-L_{3}=[3,1,2,2]
, and
G+L+T_{3}=[2,1,2]
, where
G
is the
(-2)
-curve in
F_{3}
and
L
is the proper transform of the line joining
p_{1}
with
p_{2}
. We have seen in Example
3.9
that
0pt(X,D)=3
, as needed.

∎

By Lemma
4.8
we can assume that the tips of
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
meeting
R
are not
(-2)
-curves. Together with the inequality
\delta_{D_{0}}>1
this implies that, say,
T_{2}=[3]
and
T_{1}=[2,3]
or
[k]
for some
k\in\{3,4,5\}
. We now consider each of these two cases separately.

Lemma 4.9
.

Assume that
D_{0}=\langle 1;[2,3],[3],[2]\rangle
. Then
(X,D)
is as in Proposition
3.2
(5.2)
, see Figure
4(c)
.

Let
G
be the
(-2)
-tip of the twig
T_{1}=[2,3]
, so
\operatorname{cf}(G)=\frac{2}{5}
. Let
A
be the proper transform of the dual curve of
G
, see Lemma
4.4
. Suppose
A^{2}=0
, so
4.4
(a)
holds. Consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration induced by
|A|
. Since
D_{0}-G
is not negative semi-definite,
R
is horizontal, so
D_{\mathrm{hor}}=R+G
. We have
0>\pi(A)\cdot K_{\overline{X}}=A\cdot(K_{X}+R+\frac{2}{5}G)
, so
A\cdot(R+\frac{2}{5}G)<2
, which implies that
R
is a
1
-section and
G
is a
2
-section, as it cannot be a
1
-section by Lemma
4.5
. Hence the components of
D_{0}
meeting
R
lie in different fibers, and have multiplicity
1
in each. Lemma
2.6
(b)
implies that at least two of these fibers have only one
(-1)
-curve each. In particular, there is a fiber
F
with a unique
(-1)
-curve, say
A
, meeting
R
in a
(-3)
-curve, say
C
. Such a
(-1)
-curve
A
has multiplicity at least
3
in
F
, so it does not meet the
2
-section
G
. Thus
2=F\cdot G=C\cdot G\leqslant 1
, a contradiction.

Thus by Lemma
4.4

A
is a
(-1)
-curve and
A+D-R-G
is negative definite. In particular,
A
meets
T_{1}
.

If
A
meets the
(-3)
-curve
C_{1}=T_{1}-G
then since
\operatorname{cf}(C_{1})=\frac{4}{5}
, Lemma
4.6
(d)
implies that
A\cdot C_{1}=1
,
A\cdot(D_{0}-C_{1})=0
, and
A
meets
D-D_{0}
in
(-2)
-curves only: indeed if a component of
D
has self-intersection at most
-3
, then by Lemmas
2.3
and
2.2
(a)
its coefficient is at least
\frac{1}{3}
, which is bigger than
1-\operatorname{cf}(C_{1})
. Since
A+D-R-G
is negative definite,
A
meets at most one such
(-2)
-curve, say
V
, and
V
does not meet any
(-2)
-curves in
D
. On the other hand, since
A+D-R
is not negative definite,
V
meets a component of
D
, so by Lemma
2.3

\operatorname{cf}(V)
equals tat least the coefficient of the
(-2)
-tip of the chain
[2,3]
, which equals
\frac{1}{5}
by Lemma
2.2
(a)
. Thus
\operatorname{cf}(C_{1})+\operatorname{cf}(V)\geqslant 1
, contrary to Lemma
4.6
(d)
.

Thus
A
meets
D_{0}
only in
G
, and
A\cdot G\leqslant 2
. Suppose
A\cdot G=2
. Let
\varphi
be the contraction of
A+R-T_{1}
, followed by a blowup at the image of
C_{1}\cap G
. Then the proper transforms of
G
and
C_{1}
are disjoint curves of self-intersection
1
and
2
, contrary to the Hodge index theorem.

Thus
A\cdot G=1
. Since
A+D-R
is not negative definite,
A
meets some component
V
of
D-D_{0}
. If
V=[2]
then
A\cdot V=1
because
A+D-R-G
is negative definite, so
|G+2A+V|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
C_{1}
is a
1
-section and meets a vertical chain
D-T_{1}=[2,1,3]
in the middle component: this is impossible. Thus
V^{2}\leqslant-3
. Lemmas
2.3
and
2.2
(a)
give
\operatorname{cf}(V)\geqslant 1-\frac{2}{-V^{2}}\geqslant\frac{1}{3}
. Since by Lemma
4.6
(d)
we have
\sum_{V}\operatorname{cf}(V)V\cdot A_{1}<1-\operatorname{cf}(G)=\frac{3}{5}
, such
V
is unique, meets
A
normally and has self-intersection
-3
.

Let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\widehat{X},\widehat{D})
be the contraction of
D-T_{2}
. Then
\widehat{D}
, the image of
T_{2}
, satisfies
p_{a}(\widehat{D})=2
,
\widehat{D}^{2}=T_{2}^{2}+10=7
, and is contained in the smooth part of
\widehat{X}
. Since
\rho(\widehat{X})=1
, the equality
\widehat{D}\cdot(K_{\widehat{X}}+\widehat{D})=2
implies that
K_{\widehat{X}}=-\frac{5}{7}\widehat{D}
. The image
\widehat{A}
meets
\widehat{D}
only in the cusp, with multiplicity
2
, so
-\frac{10}{7}=\widehat{A}\cdot K_{\widehat{X}}=A\cdot(K_{X}-G+\operatorname{cf}(V)V)=-2+\operatorname{cf}(V)
, and therefore
\operatorname{cf}(V)=\frac{4}{7}
.

The chain
A+D_{0}-T_{2}=[1,2,3,1,2]
supports a fiber
F_{0}
of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
1
-section
V
and and a
2
-section
T_{2}
. By Lemma
2.6
(b)
every degenerate fiber
F\neq F_{0}
meets the
2
-section
T_{2}
in its unique
(-1)
-curve, which therefore has multiplicity
2
in
F
. It follows that
F
is supported on a chain
[2,1,2]
or
\langle 2;[2],[2],[1,2,\dots,2]\rangle
, and meets
V
in a
(-2)
-tip. As a consequence, the connected component
D_{V}
of
D
containing
V
is a sum of
V
and chains
[2]
,
[2,2,2]
or forks
\langle 2;[2],[2],[(2)_{k}]\rangle
meeting
V
in a tip (in the latter case, this tip lies in the short twig). Using Lemmas
2.3
and
2.2
we check directly that the equality
\operatorname{cf}(V)=\frac{4}{7}
occurs only if
D_{V}=[2,3,2,2,2]
, so there are two degenerate fibers other than
F
, supported on
[2,1,2]
and
\langle 2;[1,2],[2],[2]\rangle
. Thus the type of
D
is as in
3.2
(5.2)
, and the
(-1)
-curves corresponding to decorations
|3|
,
|1|
are
A
and the
(-1)
-curve in the last fiber, call it
A^{\prime}
. To see the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration as in
3.2
(5.2)
, let
U
,
H
be the
(-2)
-curves meeting
V
, where
U
is a tip of
D
. Then the chain
T_{1}+A+V+U=[3,2,1,3,2]
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
1
-section
R
and
2
-section
H
; and the chain
T_{2}+A^{\prime}+(D_{V}-H-V-U)=[3,1,2,2]
supports another fiber. By Lemma
2.6
(b)
the remaining part of
D
, which consists of two disjoint
(-2)
-curves, lies in one fiber, supported on
[2,1,2]
, as needed. Note that
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, since the restriction of this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration to
H
is a morphism
\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
2
, ramified at three points. Example
3.9
shows that
0pt(X,D)=3
.

∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Assume
D_{0}=\langle 1;[k],[3],[2]\rangle
for some
k\in\{3,4,5\}
. Then
(X,D)
is either a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space as in
3.2
(3.4)
, or
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
and
(X,D)
is as in Proposition
3.2
(6)
.

Let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be the contraction of
D-T_{1}
. Then
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
and
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a cuspidal curve with
p_{a}(\overline{T})=1
, so by Lemma
2.11
(b)

\overline{Y}
is canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one. We have
K_{\overline{Y}}^{2}=\overline{T}^{2}=T_{1}^{2}+6\leqslant 3
, so by Noether’s formula the minimal resolution of
\overline{Y}
has Picard rank
10-K_{\overline{Y}}^{2}\geqslant 7
. Proposition
1.5
shows that
\overline{Y}
is either of one of the types listed in
3.2
(6)
, or of type
\mathrm{E}_{k+3}
. In the first case, using the computation of
0pt(X,D)
given in Proposition
9.1
, we conclude that
(X,D)
is as in Proposition
3.2
(6)
, as needed.

Consider the latter case. Then on the minimal resolution of
\overline{Y}
there is a
(-1)
-curve
A_{Y}
meeting the exceptional divisor only once, in the first tip of the longest twig
V_{Y}=[(2)_{k-1}]
, see Figures
10(g)
–
10(i)
: this easy fact can be read off from the structure of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, see
[
PP24
, Remark 4.5]
. By adjunction,
A_{Y}
meets the proper transform of
\overline{T}
once, too. Let
A,V
be the proper transforms of
A_{Y}
,
V_{Y}
on
X
. Then
V+A+T_{1}=[(2)_{k-1},1,k]
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, namely
R
and the branching component of
D-D_{0}
. It follows from Lemma
2.6
that all degenerate fibers are columnar, see Lemma
2.4
(b)(b.i)
; so
(X,D)
is a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space as in
3.2
(3.4)
.

∎

Remark 4.11

(Comparison with
[
Miy01
, §II.5]
)
.

The above proof of Proposition
3.2
in case
D_{0}
is a fork essentially follows the steps of original argument
[
MT84a
]
, which assumes
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
. It is explained in
[
Miy01
, §II.5]
. We now sketch the comparison between loc. cit. and the proof above.

Like the proof of Lemma
4.7
above, loc. cit. begins with extracting the twig
T=[2]
and playing a two-ray game. The argument then splits into two cases depending on whether the other ray is spanned by
R
, or not.

In each of these cases, Theorems 5.6.1 and 5.4.1 show that
X
admits a
permissible pencil
, that is, a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that the three twigs of
D_{0}
lie in different fibers, and the one containing
T
is supported on a chain
[2,1,2]
, see §II.5.2 loc. cit. The assumption
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
is used exactly once, in the proof of Lemma 5.5.6 loc. cit. In this lemma, we have
D_{0}=\langle 1;[2],T_{2},[2]\rangle
for some admissible chain
T_{2}
. Like in Lemma
4.8
above, loc. cit. considers a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
with a degenerate fiber supported on
D_{0}-T_{2}=[2,1,2]
. Then
D_{\mathrm{hor}}=T
is a
2
-section. By Lemma
2.6
(b)
every degenerate fiber has a unique
(-1)
-curve, so it meets
T
in a ramification point of
p|_{T}
. Now, the assumption
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
is used to deduce that there are at most two such fibers (inequality
t\leqslant 1
at the bottom of p. 165 loc. cit). This is true whenever
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, but if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
one gets new possibilities having at least three degenerate fibers, see Proposition
3.2
(4)
and Lemma
4.8
.

Next, §II.5.2 loc. cit. shows that, if
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
, any permissible pencil
\Lambda
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
4.5
, so
(X,D)
is a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space as in
3.2
(3.4)
. This is done by constructing morphisms
X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
which map
\Lambda
to a pencil of lines, and a multi-section in
D
to a curve
{c}
, tangent to the image of each degenerate member of
\Lambda
. The existence of such a curve
{c}
is then obstructed by Lemma 5.2.5 loc. cit., which holds whenever
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid\deg{c}
. However, if
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
then the resulting
{c}
may exist: this leads to exceptions
3.2
(4)
–
(6)
.

5.
Elliptic fibrations and planar cubics

We now move on to the proof of Proposition
1.5
. As a preliminary step, we set up a tool to produce elliptic fibrations, which will be useful in our forthcoming articles, too.

Abusing the terminology slightly, we say that a curve
C
on a smooth projective surface
X
is
elliptic
if
p_{a}(C)=1
. A
quasi-elliptic fibration
is a morphism
X\longrightarrow B
whose general fiber is elliptic; an
elliptic fibration
is a quasi-elliptic fibration whose general fiber is smooth. We note that given an smooth curve
C
on
X
with
C^{2}=0
, the linear system
|C|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
in case
C
is rational. In case
C
is elliptic an analogous statement is false, as the linear system may consist only of
C
. We will use the following refinement to construct quasi-elliptic fibrations from certain arrangements of curves. An explicit example is given in Remark
5.3
below.

Lemma 5.1

(Producing elliptic fibrations)
.

Let
X
be a smooth rational projective surface. Let
E
be a connected effective divisor on
X
such that
p_{a}(E)=1
. Assume that
E
is irreducible, or that the linear system
|E|
has no fixed components. Then the following hold.

(a)

Assume that
E^{2}=1
. Then there is a point
p\in E^{\mathrm{reg}}
such that after blowing up at
p
, the proper transform of
E
becomes a fiber of an elliptic or a quasi-elliptic fibration over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which admits a section.

(b)

Put
k=E^{2}
and
k_{\varepsilon}=k-\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon=1
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
E
is either a rational cuspidal curve, or a sum of two smooth rational curves tangent to each other with multiplicity
2
; and
\varepsilon=0
otherwise.

Assume that
k_{\varepsilon}\geqslant 2
. Fix an open dense subset
U\subseteq E^{\mathrm{reg}}
and
k_{\varepsilon}-2
points
p_{1},\dots,p_{k_{\varepsilon}-2}\in U
. Then there is a nonempty open subset
V\subseteq U
, which is dense unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, and has the following property. For any
1+\varepsilon
points
p_{k_{\varepsilon}-1},\dots,p_{k-1}\in V
there is a point
p_{k}\in U
such that the points
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
are pairwise distinct, and after blowing up at
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
, the proper transform of
E
is a fiber as in
(a)
.

(a)
Since the surface
X
is rational, the Riemann–Roch theorem gives
\dim|E|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(E-K)\cdot E=E^{2}-p_{a}(E)+1=1
. In particular, if
E
is irreducible then
E
cannot be a fixed component of
|E|
. Thus in any case, the linear system
|E|
has no fixed components, and
\dim|E|\geqslant 1
.

It follows that
|E|
has a member
E_{0}
which contains no components of
E
. Because
E^{2}=E\cdot E_{0}=1
, this member meets
E
exactly once. Let
\varphi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X
be a blowup at the point
E_{0}\cap E
. The pencil generated by the proper transforms
\varphi^{-1}_{*}E
and
\varphi^{-1}_{*}E_{0}
provides a morphism
\pi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
, whose fibers have arithmetic genus one. The fiber
\varphi^{-1}_{*}E
of
\pi
is connected since
E
is. It follows that
\pi
is its own Stein factorization, i.e. all fibers of
\pi
are connected. Thus
\pi
is a (quasi-)elliptic fibration. The curve
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
is its rational section.

(b)
We can assume that
E^{2}=2+\varepsilon
. Indeed, in order to reduce to this case we blow up at
p_{1},\dots,p_{k-r}
, and then replace
E
by its proper transform, and
U
by the preimage of
U\setminus\{p_{1},\dots,p_{k-r}\}
.

We consider first the case
\varepsilon=0
; the other case is treated in an analogous way below. Then
E^{2}=2
. We define a (set-theoretic) mapping
s\colon E^{\mathrm{reg}}\longrightarrow E^{\mathrm{reg}}
, as follows. Blowing up at
q
and applying
(a)
to the proper transform of
E
, we see that there is a member
E_{q}\in|E|\setminus\{E\}
passing through
q
. If all such members are tangent to
E
at
q
, we put
s(q)=q
, otherwise we choose one which is not, and write
E_{q}\cap E=\{q,s(q)\}
.

We claim that
s
is injective. Suppose that for different
q_{1},q_{2}\in E^{\mathrm{reg}}
we have
s(q_{1})=s(q_{2})
. Let
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X
be a blowup at
s(q_{1})
, let
E^{\prime}
be the proper transform of
E
, and let
H
be the exceptional curve. Fix
p\in H
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, the pencil generated by
E^{\prime}
and
E_{q_{i}}
as above has a member passing through
p
, call it
E_{i}^{\prime}
. After blowing up at
p
, the pencil generated by the proper transforms of
E_{1}^{\prime}
and
E_{2}^{\prime}
induces a fibration over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, such that the proper transform of
E
is a
1
-section. Thus
E\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, a contradiction.

Put
Z=\{q\in E^{\mathrm{reg}}:s(q)=q\}
. We claim that
U\setminus Z
contains a nonempty, open subset
V^{\prime}
of
E^{\mathrm{reg}}
, which is dense in
E^{\mathrm{reg}}
unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Suppose that for some component
C
of multiplicity one in
E
, the set
Z\cap C
is infinite. Choose two points
q_{1},q_{2}\in Z\cap C
. Then there are members
E_{i}\in|E|
such that
(E_{i}\cdot E)_{q_{i}}=2
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Consider a pencil
\mathcal{E}\subseteq|E|
generated by
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
. Resolving base points of
\mathcal{E}
, we get a fibration which restricts to a morphism
C\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree two. By definition of
s
, this morphism is ramified at every point of the infinite set
Z\cap C
. Hence it is totally ramified. It follows that
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, and by
[
Har77
, Proposition IV.2.5]
the curve
C
is rational. Moreover, if
E
has two local analytic branches at some point
p\in C
, then they are tangent to each other: indeed, otherwise a member
E_{p}\in\mathcal{E}
passing through
p
would satisfy
(E_{p}\cdot E)_{p}\geqslant 3
, which is impossible. Since
p_{a}(E)=1
, it follows that either
E=C
and
E
is a rational, cuspidal curve, or
C
meets
E-C
in one point, with multiplicity two. Since
\varepsilon=0
, we can always choose a component
C
of
E
which is not of this type. This proves the claim.

Since
s\colon E^{\mathrm{reg}}\longrightarrow E^{\mathrm{reg}}
is injective, we can choose a set-theoretic mapping
r\colon E^{\mathrm{reg}}\longrightarrow E^{\mathrm{reg}}
such that
r\circ s=\mathrm{id}
. Since
U\subseteq E^{\mathrm{reg}}
is dense, the set
E^{\mathrm{reg}}\setminus U
is finite. Thus
V\coloneqq V^{\prime}\setminus r(E^{\mathrm{reg}}\setminus U)
is an open dense subset of
V^{\prime}
. Hence
V
is a nonempty, open subset of
E^{\mathrm{reg}}
, dense unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. For every
q\in V
we have
s(q)\neq q
because
q\in V^{\prime}\subseteq U\setminus Z
, and
s(q)\in U
since
q=r(s(q))\not\in r(E^{\mathrm{reg}}\setminus U)
. Thus for every
p_{1}\in V
, there is a
p_{2}\coloneqq s(p_{1})\in U
such that after blowing up at
p_{1},p_{2}
the proper transform of
E
induces a fibration as needed.

In case
\varepsilon=1
, we define a mapping
(s_{1},s_{2})\colon E^{\mathrm{reg}}\longrightarrow E^{\mathrm{reg}}\times E^{\mathrm{reg}}
in an analogous way, so that after blowing up at
q
,
s_{1}(q)
and
s_{2}(q)
, the proper transform of
|E|
induces a (quasi)-elliptic fibration; and if possible, we have
\#\{q,s_{1}(q),s_{2}(q)\}>1
. The set
\{q\in E:q=s_{1}(q)=s_{2}(q)\}
is finite. Indeed, otherwise a component
C
of
E
containing infinitely many points of
Z
would, like before, admit a totally ramified morphism
C\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree three, so
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, which is false. Hence there is a point
q\in U
such that a member of
|E|
passing through
q
meets
U\setminus\{q\}
normally. We blow up at
q
, replace
E
by its proper transform and
U
by the preimage of
U\setminus\{q\}
. Then we are back in case
E^{2}=2
, and the choice of
q
guarantees that the set
Z
defined above does not contain the proper transform of the component of
E
containing
q
. Arguing as before we get the required fibration.

∎

Remark 5.2

(Necessity of additional blowups in Lemma
5.1
)
.

In Lemma
5.1
(b)
one cannot take
V=U
. Indeed, let
\overline{E}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a smooth cubic, and fix pairwise distinct points
q_{1},\dots,q_{7},q\in\overline{E}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{7}q_{7}=-2q
in the group law on
\overline{E}
. Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a blowup at
q_{1},\dots,q_{7}
, put
E=\pi^{-1}_{*}\overline{E}
, so
E^{2}=2
. Take
U=E
. Now if
\tau
is a blowup at
p_{1}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(q)
, the unique base point of
|\tau^{-1}_{*}E|
is infinitely near to
q
, so we cannot choose
p_{2}\neq p_{1}
.

The additional blowup if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
is necessary, too. Indeed, assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and let
\overline{E}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a cuspidal cubic. It has an inflection point
p\in\overline{E}
; hence
\overline{E}^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a structure of a group scheme isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, with
p
as a neutral element. Blow up seven times over
p
, each time on the proper transform of
\overline{E}
, denote the resulting morphism by
\pi
and put
E=\pi^{-1}_{*}\overline{E}
. As before, we have
E^{2}=2
. Take any
p_{1}\in E^{\mathrm{reg}}
and let
\tau
be a blowup at
p_{1}
. Then as before, a unique base point of
|\tau^{-1}_{*}E|
is infinitely near to
p_{1}
: indeed, if
E^{\prime}\in|E|
meets
E
at
p_{1},p_{2}\in E^{\mathrm{reg}}
, then
\pi(p_{1})+\pi(p_{2})=0
in the group law on
\overline{E}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
, hence
p_{1}=p_{2}
since
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
.

Remark 5.3

(A typical application of Lemma
5.1
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface, and let
E
be an effective, connected divisor on
X
. Assume that there is a birational morphism
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\widecheck{X}
such that, putting
\widecheck{E}\coloneqq\varphi_{*}E\subseteq X
, we have
E=\varphi^{*}\widecheck{E}
and
\widecheck{E}
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
5.1
; e.g. is a nodal curve with
p_{a}(\widecheck{E})=1
and
\widecheck{E}^{2}\geqslant 3
. Then by Lemma
5.1
(b)
, after
\widecheck{E}^{2}
sufficiently general blowups on the proper transform of
\widecheck{E}
, the proper transform of
|E|
induces an elliptic fibration.

For example, take
E=T+2A
, where
T=[3]
,
A=[1]
,
A\cdot T=2
and
A
meets
T
in two points. Then applying Lemma
5.1
(a)
to the image of
E
after the contraction of
A
, we infer that there is a point
p\in T\setminus A
such that after blowing up
p
, the proper transform of
|E|
induces a (quasi)-elliptic fibration. This proper transform is
T^{\prime}+2A^{\prime}
, where
T^{\prime}=[4]
,
A^{\prime}=[1]
and
T^{\prime}\cdot A^{\prime}=2
. For more general settings where such a divisor induces a (quasi-)elliptic fibration see
[
MKM83
, Theorem 3.3]
.

In the proof of Proposition
1.5
we will construct a birational map
\overline{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that the proper transform of
\overline{T}
is a planar cubic, and study the latter using its group law or explicit equations, which we now recall.

Lemma 5.4

(Group laws on planar cubics)
.

Let
{q}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a cubic curve with a chosen inflection point
p\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Then we have an isomorphism
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\ni q\mapsto q-p\in\mathrm{Cl}^{0}({q})
, which endows
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
with a group scheme structure, with neutral element
p
. Moreover, we have
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
if
{q}
is nodal and
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
if
{q}
is cuspidal.

This result is proved in
[
Sil09
, Propositions III.2.2 and III.2.5]
. See also
[
Har77
, Examples II.6.10.2, II.6.11.4 and Exercises II.6.6, II.6.7]
for the proof in case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
.

∎

Lemma 5.5

(Classification of rational planar cubics)
.

Let
{q}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a cubic parametrized by
\nu\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Then for some coordinates
[s:t]
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
[x:y:z]
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, exactly one of the following holds.

(a)

\nu[s:t]=[s^{2}t:st^{2}:s^{3}+t^{3}]
and
{q}=\{x^{3}+y^{3}=xyz\}
.

In this case
{q}
has a node at
\nu[0:1]=\nu[1:0]=[0:0:1]
with tangent lines
\{x=0\}
,
\{y=0\}
. Inflection points of
{q}
are
\nu[\zeta:-1]=[\zeta:-1:0]
for all
\zeta
such that
\zeta^{3}=1
. The corresponding inflectional tangents are
\{3\zeta x+3\zeta^{2}y+z=0\}
. In particular,
{q}
has exactly one inflection point if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
and three otherwise.

(b)

\nu[s:t]=[s^{3}:s^{2}t:t^{3}]
and
{q}=\{y^{3}=x^{2}z\}
.

In this case
{q}
has a cusp at
\nu[0:1]=[0:0:1]
with tangent line
\{x=0\}
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
then
\nu[1:0]=[1:0:0]
is the unique inflection point, with tangent
\{z=0\}
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
or
3
then
{q}
is
strange
, i.e. there is a point
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that for every
[s:t]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
, the line
{l}_{[s:t]}
tangent to
{q}
at
\nu[s:t]
passes through
p
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
then
p=[1:0:0]
is the inflection point of
{q}
, and
{l}_{[s:t]}=\{s^{2}z=t^{2}y\}
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
then
p=[0:1:0]
, and each line
{l}_{[s:t]}=\{s^{3}z=t^{3}x\}
is an inflectional tangent.

(c)

\operatorname{char}{k}=3
,
\nu[s:t]=[s^{2}t:s(s+t)^{2}:-(s+t)t^{2}]
and
{q}=\{x^{2}y+y^{2}z+z^{2}x=0\}
.

In this case
{q}
has a cusp at
\nu[1:1]=[1:1:1]
with tangent line
{l}_{0}\coloneqq\{x+y+z=0\}
and no inflection point. For every
p\in{l}_{0}
there is a unique line passing through
p
and tangent to
{q}
; and for every
p_{1}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
there are points
p_{2},p_{3}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and lines
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{3}
such that
({l}_{i}\cdot{q})_{p_{i}}=2
,
({l}_{i}\cdot{q})_{p_{i+1}}=1
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, where
p_{4}=p_{1}
.

This result is classical, see e.g.
[
Mul99
]
. We sketch the argument for the convenience of the reader.

Assume fist that
{q}
is nodal. We can choose coordinates so that the node is at
\nu[0:1]=\nu[1:0]=[0:0:1]
, with tangent lines
\{x=0\}
and
\{y=0\}
. Then, after a diagonal change of coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we have
\nu[s:t]=[s^{2}t:st^{2}:p]
for some cubic form
p\in{k}[s,t]
. After a further triangular change of coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we can assume that
p=as^{3}+bt^{3}
for some
a,b\in{k}^{*}
. After a diagonal change of coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we get a parametrization as in
(a)
.

Assume now that
{q}
is cuspidal; and put that cusp at
\nu[0:1]=[0:0:1]
, with tangent line
\{x=0\}
.

Consider the case when
{q}
has an inflection point, say
p\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. We can put
p
at
\nu[1:0]=[1:0:0]
, with inflectional tangent
\{z=0\}
. Then after diagonal change of coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we get
\nu
as in
(b)
.

Consider the case when
{q}
has no inflection points. Fix coordinates so that
\{z=0\}
is tangent to
{q}
at
\nu[1:0]=[1:0:0]
, and meets
{q}
normally at
\nu[1:1]=[1:1:0]
. Then, after a diagonal change of coordinates,
\nu[s:t]=[s^{3}:s^{2}t:t^{2}(t-s)]
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
then the line
\{x=27z+9y\}
meets
{q}
only at
\nu[3:1]\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, so the latter is an inflection point; contrary to our assumption. Thus
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. To get a parametrization in
(c)
, replace
\nu
with
\psi\circ\nu\circ\varphi
, where
\varphi[s:t]=[t-s:s+t]
and
\psi[x:y:z]=[x-y+z:z:y+z]
.

The properties of
{q}
in cases
(a)
and
(b)
follow by a direct computation. We now prove the last statement of
(c)
. Let
p_{1}=\nu[s:t]\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
for some
[s:t]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{[1:1]\}
. The tangent line
{l}_{1}
to
{q}
at
p_{1}
is given by
{l}_{1}=\{t(s+t)^{2}x+st^{2}y=s^{2}(s+t)z\}
. We have
{l}_{0}\cap{l}_{1}=\{[s:-(s+t):t]\}
. This point uniquely determines
[s:t]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which proves the first part. Define
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
by
\sigma[x:y:z]=[z:x:y]
, so
\sigma^{3}=\mathrm{id}
. The equation of
{q}
shows that
\sigma
preserves
{q}
, and the line
{l}_{1}
meets
{q}
at
p_{2}\coloneqq\sigma(p)
. Thus the line
\ell_{2}
tangent to
{q}
at
p_{2}
meets
{q}
at
p_{3}\coloneqq\sigma(p_{2})
, and eventually the line
{l}_{3}
tangent to
{q}
at
p_{3}
meets
{q}
at
\sigma(p_{3})=\sigma^{3}(p_{1})=p_{1}
, as needed.

∎

Remark 5.6

(A cubic without a natural group law)
.

Lemmas
5.4
and
5.5
show that for every irreducible planar cubic
{q}
, exactly one of the following holds.

(a)

The smooth locus
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
of
{q}
admits a group law such that the sum of three points
p,q,r\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is zero if and only if there is a line
{l}
meeting
{q}
at
p,q,r
(counted with multiplicities).

(b)

We have
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, and
{q}
is projectively equivalent to the cuspidal curve
\{x^{2}y+y^{2}z+z^{2}x=0\}
described in Lemma
5.5
(c)
.

6.
Classification of canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one

We now recall the classification of canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one, summarized in Table
1
. Current proofs build on the pioneering work of du Val
[
dV34
]
, together with modern insights of, among others, Demazure
[
Dem80
]
, Hidaka–Watanabe
[
HW81
]
and Bindschadler–Brenton–Drucker
[
BBD84
]
. The latter article classifies all canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one whose minimal resolutions have the maximal number of exceptional components (namely,
8
). A complete classification for
{k}=\mathbb{C}
was given by Furushima
[
Fur86
, Theorem 2]
. We refer to
[
AN06
]
for a full statement and a detailed historical discussion. For a general algebraically closed field
{k}
of characteristic other than
2,3
or
5
, Ye
[
Ye02
]
proved that the classification is the same as in case
{k}=\mathbb{C}
. Eventually, case
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
was recently settled by Kawakami–Nagaoka
[
KN20
]
. Here new singularity types appear, and some already occurring in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
acquire additional moduli, cf.
[
PP24
, Table 15]
.

Below we provide a characteristic-independent argument leading to a classification based on the geometry of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of low height. We restrict our attention to canonical del Pezzo surfaces
\overline{X}
of height at least
3
. Indeed, if
0pt(\overline{X})=1
or
2
then the geometry of
\overline{X}
is simple; and an elementary exercise, or an application of
[
PP24
, Theorem A]
, shows that
\overline{X}
is constructed as in Remark 4.5 or Section 5A loc. cit. The minimal resolutions of these surfaces, together with witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations are shown in Figures
9
and
10
; some of those fibrations were used e.g. in
[
MZ88
]
. In these figures solid lines represent all components of
D
which are
(-2)
-curves, while dashed lines correspond to
(-1)
-curves. Thickening of some lines refers to a discussion in §
9
.

Proposition 6.1

(Canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank
1
,
0pt\geqslant 3
, see Figure
6
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a canonical del Pezzo surface of Picard rank
1
. Assume
\overline{X}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
0pt(\overline{X})\neq 1,2
. Then
0pt(\overline{X})=4
and one of the following holds.

(a)

The surface
\overline{X}
is of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
, isomorphic to the one constructed in Example
7.1
, see Figure
6(a)
.

(b)

We have
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. The surface
\overline{X}
is of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, isomorphic to one of the surfaces constructed in Example
8.1
(c)
, see Figure
6(b)
. The class of such surfaces has moduli dimension
2
.

Let
\pi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},0)
be a minimal log resolution. Because
\overline{X}
is canonical, the exceptional divisor
D
consists of
(-2)
-curves and
K_{X}=\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}
. By
[
BT22
, Proposition 2.12]
the linear system
|-K_{X}|
has no fixed component, and its general member
E
is irreducible and reduced, cf.
[
Dem80
, p. 41]
or
[
Ye02
, Lemma 3.1]
. We have
p_{a}(E)=1
and
E^{2}=K_{\overline{X}}^{2}>0
, since
-K_{\overline{X}}
is ample. By Lemma
2.7
,
X
is rational. Noether’s formula gives
E^{2}=10-\rho(X)=9-\#D
.

We claim that
\#D\geqslant 7
. Suppose the contrary. We have
\rho(X)>2
, since otherwise
X
is either
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, with
D
either zero or the negative section, contrary to our assumptions. Thus
X
contains a
(-1)
-curve
L
. We have
E\cdot(K_{X}+D+L)=-K_{X}^{2}-K_{X}\cdot L=\#D-8<0
, so
|K_{X}+D+L|=\emptyset
. By Lemma
2.1

D+L
is a sum of disjoint rational trees.

Suppose
L
is the unique
(-1)
-curve in
X
. Since
X
is rational and
\rho(X)>2
,
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration with a degenerate fiber
F
. Then
F
contains
L
, so it is a unique degenerate fiber. By Lemma
2.6
(b)
we have
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L\subseteq D
and
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=1
. Since
D+L
is a sum of trees, we have
F_{\mathrm{red}}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=1
. Because
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L\subseteq D
consists of
(-2)
-curves, Lemma
2.4
(c)
implies that all components of
F
have multiplicity at most two in
F
. Hence
2\geqslant F\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{X})
, a contradiction.

Therefore,
\overline{X}
contains at least two
(-1)
-curves, say
L
and
A
. As before, we have
E\cdot(K_{X}+D+L+A)=-K_{X}^{2}+2=\#D-7<0
, so
|K_{X}+D+L+A|=\emptyset
and
D+L+A
is a sum of rational trees.

Since
\pi(L)^{2}>0
, the intersection matrix of
D+L
is not negative definite. Thus
L\cdot D\geqslant 1
, and if the equality holds then
D+L
contains a fork of type
\langle 2;[2],[2],[1,(2)_{k}]\rangle
for some
k\geqslant 0
. Such a fork supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most
2
, which is impossible. Hence
L\cdot D\geqslant 2
and
A\cdot D\geqslant 2
.

Let
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
be components of
D
meeting
L
. Suppose
L\cdot D\geqslant 3
and
A\cdot D\geqslant 3
. The assumption
\#D\leqslant 6
implies that, after renaming
L
and
A
if needed, we get that
L\cdot D=3
and
G_{1},G_{2}
are connected components of
D
. Now
G_{1}+L+G_{2}=[2,1,2]
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height two, a contradiction.

Therefore, say,
L\cdot D=2
. We have
\beta_{D}(G_{1}+G_{2})\geqslant 3
, since otherwise
G_{1}+L+G_{2}=[2,1,2]
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most two. It follows that the connected component
D_{L}
of
D+L
containing
L
has at least six components. Put
W=D+L-D_{L}
. Since
\#(D+L)\leqslant 7
, we have
\#W\leqslant 1
. Since
A\cdot D\geqslant 2
and
D+A+L
is a sum of trees, we have
W=[2]
,
A\cdot W=1
and
A\cdot D_{L}=1
. As before, the components
W,W^{\prime}
of
D
meeting
A
satisfy
\beta_{D}(W+W^{\prime})\geqslant 3
. We get
D=W+G_{1}+G
, where
G=\langle 2;[2],[2],[2]\rangle
meets both
L
and
A
in a branching component
G_{2}
. Consider a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration induced by
|G_{1}+2L+G_{2}|
. Then all twigs of
G
are
1
-sections. By Lemma
2.6
(a)
the fiber
F_{W}
containing
W
is a sum of
W
and
(-1)
-curves, so
F_{W}=[1,2,1]
.
One of its tips, say
L^{\prime}
, meets at least two twigs of
G
, so
L^{\prime}+D
is not a sum of trees; a contradiction.

Having shown
\#D\geqslant 7
, we continue the proof of Proposition
6.1
. Recall that the curve
E
is a member of
|-K_{X}|
, so it satisfies
p_{a}(E)=1
and
9-\#D=E^{2}>0
. By Lemma
5.1
(a)
there is a blowup
\gamma\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X
at
9-\#D
smooth points of
E
such that the linear system of
E^{\prime}\coloneqq\gamma^{-1}_{*}E
induces a (quasi-)elliptic fibration
f\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Since
E^{\prime}\in|-K_{X^{\prime}}|
, by adjunction formula every
(-1)
-curve on
X^{\prime}
meets
E^{\prime}
once, and every
(-2)
-curve on
X^{\prime}
is disjoint from
E^{\prime}
. In particular, the fibers of
f
contain no
(-1)
-curves, i.e.
f
is minimal; and all components of
D^{\prime}\coloneqq\gamma^{-1}_{*}D
are contained in the fibers of
f
.

Let
U^{\prime}
be a component of a fiber
F^{\prime}
meeting
\operatorname{Exc}\gamma
. The components of
\operatorname{Exc}\gamma
are sections of
f
, so
U^{\prime}
has multiplicity
1
in
F^{\prime}
. By the Kodaira classification of elliptic fibers,
U^{\prime}
is non-branching in
F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}
. In particular,
U^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}\leqslant 2
, so
U\cdot D\leqslant 2
, where
U=\gamma(U^{\prime})
. Since
U^{\prime}
meets
\operatorname{Exc}\gamma
, we have
U^{2}\geqslant(U^{\prime})^{2}+1=-1
. On the other hand,
U^{2}\leqslant(U^{\prime})^{2}+(\rho(X^{\prime})-\rho(X))=-2+(9-\#D)=7-\#D\leqslant 0
by the claim above. If
U^{2}=0
then
|U|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most two, which is impossible. Hence
U^{2}=-1
.

Suppose
U\cdot D\leqslant 1
. Since
U+D
is not negative definite, it contains a fork
\langle 2;[2],[2],[1,(2)_{k}]\rangle
for some
k\geqslant 0
, which supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height two, a contradiction.

Thus
U\cdot D=2
. Because
U^{\prime}
has multiplicity one in
F^{\prime}
, the classification of elliptic fibers implies that the components of
F^{\prime}-U^{\prime}
meeting
U^{\prime}
are tips of
F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}-U^{\prime}
. Thus the components of
D
meeting
U
are tips of
D
. If
U
meets two disjoint tips of
D
, then their sum with
U
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height two, which is impossible. Hence
U
meets
D
in both tips of a connected component of type
[2,2]
or
[2]
. In particular,
F^{\prime}
meets
\operatorname{Exc}\gamma
once, so
\gamma
has only one base point, which gives
\#D=8
.

Applying this argument to every degenerate fiber of
f
, we get that every connected component of
D
is of type
[2,2]
or
[2]
, and meets a
(-1)
-curve in both tips. These
(-1)
-curves meet normally at the base point of
\gamma
.

Let
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
be two such
(-1)
-curves, and let
V_{i}
be the connected component of
D
meeting
U_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
induced by
|U_{1}+U_{2}|
. Then
D_{\mathrm{hor}}=V_{1}+V_{2}
, and each
V_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
is either a
2
-section or a chain of two
1
-sections. In particular,
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant(U_{1}+U_{2})\cdot D=4
.

By Lemma
2.6
(a)
, degenerate fibers of this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration consist of
(-1)
-curves and connected components of
D
, so by Lemma
2.4
(c)
they are supported on chains of type
[1,1]
,
[1,2,1]
,
[1,2,2,1]
or
[2,1,2]
.

Assume that
V_{1}=[2]
. Since
\#D=8
is even, we can take
V_{2}=[2]
, too. Then
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=2
. By Lemma
2.6
(b)
every fiber other than
U_{1}+U_{2}
has exactly one component not in
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
, so it is supported on a chain
[2,1,2]
. It follows that
D
is a disjoint union of
(-2)
-curves, i.e.
\overline{X}
is of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see Figure
6(b)
. We need to show that
\overline{X}
is constructed as in Example
8.1
(c)
.

Let
\varphi
be a contraction of
U_{2}
, followed by some contraction of remaining fibers to
0
-curves, so
\varphi(X)
is some Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{n}
. Put
H_{i}=\varphi(V_{i})
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then
H_{1}
is a smooth, rational
2
-section. Numerical properties of Hirzebruch surfaces imply that
n\leqslant 2
. We can choose
\varphi
so that
n=1
. Let
\varsigma_{1}\colon\mathbb{F}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of the negative section
\Xi_{1}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
we have
H_{i}\cdot\Xi_{1}=p_{a}(H_{i})=i-1
. Put
{p}=(\varsigma_{1}\circ\varphi)_{*}D
. Then
{p}={l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}+{c}+{q}
, where
{l}_{j}
’s are lines meeting at some
p_{0}
,
{c}=\varsigma_{1}(H_{1})
is a conic such that
({c}\cdot{l}_{j})_{p_{j}}=2
for some
p_{j}\neq p_{0}
,
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. It follows that
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
{q}=\varsigma_{1}(H_{2})
is a cubic passing through
p_{0}
and tangent to
{l}_{j}
at each
p_{j}
. After a standard quadratic transformation at
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
we get a configuration as in Example
8.1
(c)
. In Lemma
8.4
(c)
we will see that
0pt(\overline{X})=4
, so
(b)
holds.

Assume now that
V_{j}=[2,2]
for all
j
, i.e.
\overline{X}
is of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
. We need to show that
\overline{X}
is as in Example
7.1
.

Let
V_{j}^{+}
,
V_{j}^{-}
be the components of
V_{j}
. Now, all degenerate fibers except
U_{1}+U_{2}
are of type
[1,2,2,1]
. Let
V_{3},V_{4}
be the vertical connected components of
D
, and for
i\in\{3,4\}
let
F_{i}=L_{i}^{+}+V_{i}^{+}+V_{i}^{-}+L_{i}^{-}=[1,2,2,1]
be the fiber containing
V_{i}
, where
L_{i}^{+}
is the
(-1)
-curve meeting
V_{i}^{+}
. Since the intersection matrix of
L_{i}^{\pm}+D
is not negative definite, we have
L_{i}^{\pm}\cdot D\geqslant 2
, so
F\cdot D=4
implies that
L_{i}^{\pm}\cdot D=2
. In Figure
6(a)
,
the sections
V_{1}^{+}
,
V_{1}^{-}
,
V_{2}^{+}
,
V_{2}^{-}
are denoted by
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
,
Q
,
R
;
the curves
L_{3}^{+},V_{3}^{+},V_{3}^{-},L_{3}^{-}
are denoted by
A_{1},G_{1}^{(1)},G_{1}^{(2)},L_{1}^{\prime}
;
the curves
L_{4}^{+},V_{4}^{+},V_{4}^{-},L_{4}^{-}
are denoted by
L_{2}^{\prime},G_{2}^{(2)},G_{2}^{(1)},A_{2}
;
and the curves
U_{1},U_{2}
are denoted by
L
,
A_{0}
.

Let
\varphi
be the contraction of
U_{1}+V_{1}^{-}+\sum_{i=3}^{4}(F_{i}-L_{i}^{-})
. Then
\varphi(X)=\mathbb{P}^{2}
. If
V_{2}^{+}
meets
L_{i}^{+}
,
i\in\{3,4\}
, then
\varphi(V_{2}^{-})=[2]
, which is impossible. Hence, say,
V_{2}^{+}
meets
L_{3}^{+}
and
L_{4}^{-}
. Now
\varphi_{*}D
is as in Example
7.1
, with
{l}_{j}=\varphi(L_{j+2}^{+})
,
{q}=\varphi(V_{1}^{+})
, and
{l}=\varphi(U_{2})
. In Lemma
7.2
(a)
we will see that
0pt(\overline{X})=4
, so
(a)
holds.

∎

Q
A_{1}
G_{1}^{(1)}
G_{1}^{(2)}
L_{1}^{\prime}
A_{2}
G_{2}^{(1)}
G_{2}^{(2)}
L_{2}^{\prime}
L
A_{0}
R
L_{1}
L_{2}

If
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
then:
A_{0}\cap Q\cap R\neq\emptyset
,
L\cap L_{1}\cap L_{2}\neq\emptyset
.

(a)

4\mathrm{A}_{2}
, see Example
7.1
.

,

8.1
