7.
Del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}

We now give a construction of the surface of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
from Proposition
6.1
(a)
, and list some of its properties. Most importantly, in Lemma
7.2
(d)
below we will see that the automorphism group of
\overline{X}
acts transitively on the set of singular points of
\overline{X}
. We infer those properties from the geometry of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
4
found in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, see Figure
6(a)
.

Example 7.1

(Del Pezzo surface of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
, see Figure
6(a)
)
.

Let
{q}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a rational cubic with inflection points
p_{1},p_{2}
. Let
{l}_{i}
be the line tangent to
{q}
at
p_{i}
, and fix a line
{l}
tangent to the singular point
r\in{q}
, see Figure
7
. By Lemma
5.5
, the configuration
{q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}
is unique up to a projective equivalence; and since
{q}
has at least two inflection points, it singular point
r\in{q}
is a node if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
and a cusp if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
.

Blow up twice over
r
and three times over
p_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
; each time on the proper transform of
{l}
and
{l}_{j}
, respectively. Denote this morphism by
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and put
L_{j}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{j}
,
Q=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{q}
. Let
A_{0},A_{j}
be the
(-1)
-curves over
r,p_{j}
; and write
(\varphi^{*}{q})_{\mathrm{red}}=G+A_{0}=[2,2]+[1]
,
\varphi^{-1}(p_{j})=G_{j}+A_{j}=[2,2,1]
. Since
L_{1}+L_{2}=[2,2]
,
D\coloneqq(L_{1}+L_{2})+G+G_{1}+G_{2}
contracts to four
\mathrm{A}_{2}
-points on a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one, as needed.

We now construct a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
(X,D)
of height
4
. Put
L=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}
and let
L_{i}^{\prime}
be the proper transform of the line joining
r
with
p_{i}
. Then
|A_{1}+G_{1}+L_{1}^{\prime}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration. Its degenerate fibers are
A_{i}+G_{i}+L_{i}^{\prime}=[1,2,2,1]
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, and
A_{0}+L=[1,1]
. The horizontal part of
D
consists of four
1
-sections, namely
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
,
Q
and the first exceptional curve over
r
, call it
R
. They meet
D
in four components each, see Figure
6(a)
.

{l}
r
{l}_{1}
p_{1}
{l}_{2}
p_{2}
p_{0}
{q}

(a)

\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3

{l}
r
{l}_{1}
p_{1}
{l}_{2}
p_{2}
p_{0}
{q}

(b)

\operatorname{char}{k}=3

Figure 7
.
Configuration
{q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}
used in Example
7.1
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\overline{X}
be the del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
. We keep notation from Example
7.1
.

(a)

We have
0pt(\overline{X})=4
.

(b)

Let
\overline{T}
be an irreducible member of
|-K_{\overline{X}}|
. Then
\overline{T}
is smooth if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
and cuspidal if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
.

(c)

If
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
then the set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(4\mathrm{A}_{2})
of isomorphism classes of log surfaces
(\overline{X},\overline{T})
as in
(b)
has moduli dimension
1
, see Section
2F
. In fact, it is represented by an almost universal family over
B=\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}_{3}}
with symmetry group
\operatorname{Aut}(B)\cong\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
, see Table
2
.

(d)

The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})=\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\rtimes G
, where
G
is the alternating group
A_{4}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
, and a symmetric group
S_{4}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. The subgroup
G
acts transitively on the set of singular points of
\overline{X}
. Explicitly, the group
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is generated by the following automorphisms, see Figure
6(a)
:

(i)

(G_{1}^{(1)},G_{1}^{(2)},G_{2}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(2)},L_{1},L_{2},Q,R)\mapsto(G_{1}^{(2)},G_{1}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(2)},G_{2}^{(1)},L_{2},L_{1},R,Q)

(ii)

(G_{1}^{(1)},G_{1}^{(2)},G_{2}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(2)},L_{1},L_{2},Q,R)\mapsto(L_{2},L_{1},G_{1}^{(2)},G_{1}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(2)},Q,R)

(iii)

(G_{1}^{(1)},G_{1}^{(2)},G_{2}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(2)},L_{1},L_{2},Q,R)\mapsto(G_{2}^{(2)},G_{2}^{(1)},G_{1}^{(2)},G_{1}^{(1)},R,Q,L_{1},L_{2})

(iv)

(G_{1}^{(1)},G_{1}^{(2)},G_{2}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(2)},L_{1},L_{2},Q,R)\mapsto(G_{2}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(2)},G_{1}^{(1)},G_{1}^{(2)},L_{2},L_{1},Q,R)
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3

(a)
Suppose the contrary, i.e.
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D\leqslant 3
. By Lemma
2.6
(a)
its degenerate fibers are sums of
(-1)
-curves and components of
D
, so by Lemma
2.4
(c)
they are supported on chains
[2,1,2]
,
[1,2,1]
or
[1,2,2,1]
. Since
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}\leqslant 3
, Lemma
2.6
(b)
implies that there are at most two fibers with two
(-1)
-curves each, so since
\#D_{\mathrm{vert}}=\#D-\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 5
, there is at least one fiber
F
such that
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
. Each tip of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
once, and since the
(-1)
-curve in
F
has multiplicity
2
, inequality
F\cdot D\leqslant 3
implies that it does not meet
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Thus
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, and the remaining degenerate fibers meet them in
(-1)
-curves of multiplicity
1
. Since
\overline{X}
is of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
, there are two such fibers, both of type
[1,2,2,1]
. This is a contradiction with Lemma
2.6
(b)
.

(b)
Let
T
be the proper transform of
\overline{T}
on
X
, so
T\in|-K_{X}|
since
X
is canonical. Let
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2})
be as in Example
7.1
, see Figure
6(a)
. Since by adjunction
T
is disjoint from
D
and
T\cdot A=1
for every
(-1)
-curve on
X
, we see that
{t}\coloneqq\varphi(T)
is a cubic meeting
{q}
in
p_{1},p_{2},r\in{t}^{\mathrm{reg}}
with multiplicity
3
.

Assume first that
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
. Suppose
T
is singular, so
{t}
is a rational cubic with inflection points
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
. By Lemma
5.4
,
{t}^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a group law. In this group law we have
3p_{1}=3p_{2}=0
since
p_{1},p_{2}
are inflection points of
{t}
. Intersecting
{t}
with
{q}
we get
3p_{1}+3p_{2}+3r=0
, so
3r=0
, which means that
r
is an inflection point of
{t}
, too. Lemma
5.5
implies that
{t}
is nodal, and
p_{1},p_{2},{t}
lie on the same line, say
{l}
. Since
p_{1},p_{2}\in{q}
and
r\in\operatorname{Sing}{q}
we get
3<{l}\cdot{q}=3
, a contradiction.

Assume now that
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. Since
{q}
is a rational cubic with two inflection points
p_{1},p_{2}
, by Lemma
5.5
(b)
we can fix coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
{q}=\{y^{3}=x^{2}z\}
,
p_{1}=[1:0:0]
,
{l}_{1}=\{z=0\}
;
p_{2}=[1:1:1]
,
{l}_{2}=\{x=z\}
; and
{q}
has a cusp at
r=[0:0:1]
, with tangent line
\{x=0\}
. Now the pencil of cubics meeting
{q}
at
p_{1},p_{2},r
with multiplicity at least
3
is given by
\{\lambda(y^{3}-x^{2}z)=\mu(y^{3}-xz^{2})\}
,
[\lambda:\mu]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and every irreducible member of this pencil has a singular point at
[\mu(\lambda-\mu)^{1/3}:(\lambda\mu)^{2/3}:\lambda(\mu-\lambda)^{1/3}]
, which is a cusp by Lemma
5.5
.

(c)
The above construction shows that
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{[0:1],[1:0],[1:1],[1:-1]\}
is the base of the required almost universal family. We postpone a detailed proof for the moment, as it will be more clear from an equivalent construction of
\overline{X}
given in
[
KN22
, Proposition 5.1(5)]
, which we recall in Remark
7.3
below.

(d)
Clearly, we have
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})=\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
. Recall from Example
7.1
that the log surface
(X,D)
is constructed via a morphism
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
, where
{p}={q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}
,
{q}
is a rational cubic, and for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
{l}_{i}
is a line such that
({l}_{i}\cdot{q})_{p_{i}}=3
for some
p_{i}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. The singular point
r\in{q}
is a node if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
and a cusp if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, cf. Lemma
5.5
. Given the configuration
{p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
, the morphism
\varphi
constructed in Example
7.1
is uniquely determined, up to an action of
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
, by the choice of a line
{l}
tangent to
{q}
at
r
.

To construct an automorphism
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
, we define a morphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
as the contraction of a divisor which is supposed to be the image
\sigma_{*}(\operatorname{Exc}\pi)
. Next, we put
\sigma=\widetilde{\varphi}^{-1}\circ\varphi\colon X\dashrightarrow X
. The above uniqueness property of
\varphi
shows that
\sigma
extends to an automorphism of
(X,D)
if and only if

(4)

the second blowup at
r
in the decomposition of
\widetilde{\varphi}
is centered on the proper transform of
{l}
.

Note that if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
then condition (
4
) is automatically satisfied because
r\in{q}
is a cusp.

We keep notation
L_{i},G_{i}
etc. from Example
7.1
, see Figure
6(a)
. Write
\{p\}={l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}
and let
{l}_{12}
be the line joining
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
{l}_{i}^{\prime}
be the line joining
p_{i}
with
r
. Put
L_{12}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{12}
,
L_{i}^{\prime}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{i}^{\prime}
.

In case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
, we introduce further notation. Fix a primitive third root of unity
\zeta
. Let
\overline{\nu}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a parametrization of
{q}
such that
p_{1}=\nu[\zeta:-1]
,
r=\nu[0:1]=\nu[0:1]
and
{l}
is tangent to the branch of
{q}
whose preimage contains
[0:1]
. Then
p_{2}=\nu[\zeta^{2}:-1]
, and
{q}\cap{l}_{12}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\nu[1:-1]\}
, see Lemma
5.5
(a)
. The induced parametrization
\nu\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow X
of
Q
satisfies

(5)

Q\cap A_{0}=\nu[0:1],\ Q\cap R=\nu[1:0],\ Q\cap A_{1}=\nu[\zeta:-1],\ Q\cap A_{2}=\nu[\zeta^{2}:-1],\ Q\cap L_{12}=\nu[1:-1].

Moreover we fix a parametrization
\overline{\nu}_{1}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
of
{l}_{1}
such that
p=\overline{\nu}_{1}[1:0]
,
{l}_{1}\cap{l}=\overline{\nu}_{1}[0:1]
and
p_{1}=\overline{\nu}_{1}[\zeta^{2}:1]
. An explicit computation in coordinates of Lemma
5.5
(a)
, with
{l}=\{x=0\}
, shows that
\overline{\nu}_{1}[\zeta:1]={l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}^{\prime}
. Indeed, we have
p=[1:1:3]
,
{l}_{1}\cap{l}=[0:1:-3\zeta^{2}]
,
p_{1}=[\zeta:-1:0]
, so
\overline{\nu}_{1}[s:t]=[s:s+t:3s-3\zeta^{2}t]
; and
{l}_{2}^{\prime}=\{\zeta x+y=0\}
, so
{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}^{\prime}=[1:-\zeta:3-3\zeta]=\overline{\nu}_{1}[\zeta:1]
, as claimed. By symmetry, we have a parametrization
\overline{\nu}_{2}
of
{l}_{2}
such that
p=\overline{\nu}_{1}[1:0]
,
{l}_{2}\cap{l}=\overline{\nu}_{2}[0:1]
,
p_{2}=\overline{\nu}_{2}[\zeta:1]
and
{l}_{2}\cap{l}_{1}^{\prime}=\overline{\nu}_{2}[\zeta^{2}:1]
. Thus for
i\in\{1,2\}
, the induced parametrization
\nu_{i}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow X
of
L_{i}
satisfies

(6)

L_{i}\cap L=\nu_{i}[0:1],\ L_{1}\cap L_{2}=\nu_{i}[1:0],\ L_{i}\cap A_{i}=\nu_{i}[\zeta^{2i}:1],\ L_{i}\cap L_{3-i}^{\prime}=\nu_{i}[\zeta^{i}:1].

Having introduced this notation, we can proceed with the construction of each automorphism from
(d)
.

(d)(d.i)
Let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
(L_{1}^{\prime}+G_{1})+(L_{2}^{\prime}+G_{2})+(A_{0}+Q)
, such that
\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{1}^{\prime})=p_{1}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{2}^{\prime})=p_{2}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{1}\cap L_{2})=p
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{0})=r
. Then for
i\in\{1,2\}
we have
{l}_{i}=\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{3-i})
,
{l}_{i}^{\prime}=\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{i})
, and
\widetilde{\varphi}(R)
is a rational cubic with a singular point at
r
, tangent with multiplicity
3
to
{l}_{i}
at
p_{i}
, so
\widetilde{\varphi}(R)={q}
. In particular,
\widetilde{\varphi}_{*}D={p}
. The second blowup over
r
is centered on the image of
L
, so to verify condition (
4
), we need to check that
\widetilde{\varphi}(L)={l}
in case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
. Consider a parametrization of
{l}_{1}
given by
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\nu_{2}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Formulas (
6
) give
p=\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{1}\cap L_{2})=\widetilde{\nu}_{1}[1:0]
,
p_{1}=\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{2}\cap L_{1}^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{1}[\zeta^{2}:1]
and
{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}^{\prime}=\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{2}\cap A_{2})=\widetilde{\nu}_{1}[\zeta:1]
. Hence
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}=\overline{\nu}_{1}
. It follows from (
6
) that
{l}_{1}\cap{l}=\widetilde{\nu}_{1}[0:1]=\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{2}\cap L)
, so
{l}=\widetilde{\varphi}(L)
, as needed.

(d)(d.ii)
Let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
(A_{2}+L_{2}+L_{1})+(L_{12}+G_{1})+(A_{0}+R)
, such that
\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{2})=p_{1}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{12})=p_{2}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(G_{2}^{(1)}\cap G_{2}^{(2)})=p
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{0})=r
. Then
{l}_{1}=\widetilde{\varphi}(G_{2}^{(2)})
,
{l}_{2}=\widetilde{\varphi}(G_{2}^{(1)})
, and like before we see that
\widetilde{\varphi}(Q)={q}
, so
\widetilde{\varphi}_{*}D={p}
. It remains to check condition (
4
) in case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
. Consider a parametrization of
{q}
given by
\widetilde{\nu}=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\nu\circ\delta
, where
\delta[s:t]=[\zeta s:t]
, so
\delta\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
and
\delta^{-1}[s:t]=[\zeta^{2}s:t]
. Using formulas (
5
), we compute that
p_{1}=\widetilde{\varphi}(Q\cap A_{2})=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\nu[\zeta^{2}:-1]=\widetilde{\nu}[\zeta:-1]
,
p_{2}=\widetilde{\varphi}(Q\cap L_{12})=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\nu[1:-1]=\widetilde{\nu}[\zeta^{2}:-1]
; and
r=\widetilde{\nu}[0:1]=\widetilde{\nu}[1:0]
, so
\widetilde{\nu}=\overline{\nu}
. The second blowup over
r
in the decomposition of
\widetilde{\varphi}
is centered at the image of
Q\cap A_{0}=\nu[0:1]
; as needed.

(d)(d.iii)
Let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
(L_{2}^{\prime}+G_{2})+(A_{1}+G_{1})+(L+L_{2})
such that
\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{2}^{\prime})=p_{1}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{1})=p_{2}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(Q\cap R)=p
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(L)=r
. Then
\widetilde{\varphi}(R)={l}_{1}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(Q)={l}_{2}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{2})={l}_{1}^{\prime}
;
\widetilde{\varphi}(L_{1})={q}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{0})
is a line tangent to
{q}
at
r
. The second blowup over
r
is centered on the image of
A_{0}
, so to verify condition (
4
), we need to check that
\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{0})={l}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
. Let
\widetilde{\nu}_{2}\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\nu\circ\delta
be a parametrization of
{l}_{2}
, where
\delta[s:t]=[s:-t]
. Using formulas (
5
), we compute that
p_{2}=\widetilde{\varphi}(Q\cap A_{1})=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}[\zeta:1]
,
{l}_{2}\cap{l}_{1}^{\prime}=\widetilde{\varphi}(Q\cap A_{2})=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}[\zeta^{2}:1]
,
p=\widetilde{\varphi}(Q\cap R)=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}[1:0]
, so
\widetilde{\nu}_{2}=\overline{\nu}_{2}
. Now
\widetilde{\varphi}(Q\cap A_{0})=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}[0:1]={l}_{2}\cap{l}
by formula (
6
), so
\widetilde{\varphi}(A_{0})={l}
, as claimed.

(d)(d.iv)
Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, and let
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be a automorphism given by
(p,p_{1},p_{2},r)\mapsto(p,p_{2},p_{1},r)
. Since
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, this automorphism lifts to the required automorphism of
(X,D)
.

It remains to show that the group
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is generated by the automorphisms in
(d)
. First, we note that an element of
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is uniquely determined by its action on the set of components of
D
. Indeed, suppose that
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
fixes all components of
D
. Since
\rho(\overline{X})=1
, the group
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
is generated by
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}
and the classes of components of
D
, so
\sigma
acts trivially on
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
. Because
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
is negative definite, it follows that
\sigma
fixes each component of
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
, and therefore descends to an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
fixing
\varphi_{*}D={p}
componentwise. Thus
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
, as claimed.

Consider a group homomorphism
\alpha\colon\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)\longrightarrow S_{4}
given by the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
on the set of connected components of
D
. Clearly, the involution
\iota
from
(d)(d.i)
lies in
\ker\alpha
. We claim that
\ker\alpha=\langle\iota\rangle
.

Let
\sigma\in\ker\alpha
. Since
\sigma(Q+R)=Q+R
and
\sigma(L_{1}+L_{2})=L_{1}+L_{2}
, we have
\sigma(L)=L
and
\sigma(A_{0})=A_{0}
, so
\sigma
preserves the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
induced by
|A_{0}+L|
, see Figure
6(a)
. It follows that
\sigma(A_{i}+L_{i}^{\prime})=A_{i}+L_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Composing
\sigma
with
\iota
if needed, we can assume that
\sigma(A_{1})=A_{1}
, so
\sigma(L_{1}^{\prime})=L_{1}^{\prime}
. Then
\sigma
fixes components of
D
meeting
A_{1}
, in particular
\sigma(Q)=Q
. Since
L_{2}^{\prime}
does not meet
Q
, we have
\sigma(A_{2})=A_{2}
, so
\sigma(L_{2}^{\prime})=L_{2}^{\prime}
. It follows that
\sigma
fixes each component of
D
, so
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
, which proves the claim.

Let
G\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
be the subgroup generated by the remaining automorphisms listed in
(d)
. The restriction
\alpha|_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow S_{4}
is injective, its image equals
A_{4}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
and
S_{4}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. It remains to show that
\alpha
is not surjective if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
. Suppose the contrary. Then there is
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
such that
\sigma(G_{1})=G_{2}
,
\sigma(L_{1}+L_{2})=L_{1}+L_{2}
and
\sigma(Q+R)=Q+R
. Composing
\sigma
with
\iota
, we can assume that
\sigma(G_{1}^{(i)})=G_{2}^{(i)}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. If
\sigma(Q)=R
then
\sigma(L_{12})
meets
G_{1}^{(2)}
,
G_{2}^{(2)}
and
R
, so its image
{c}\coloneqq\varphi(\sigma(L_{12}))
satisfies

3\deg{c}={c}\cdot{q}=(3\cdot(\sigma(L_{12})\cdot A_{1})+1)+(3\cdot(\sigma(L_{12})\cdot A_{2})+1)+(3\cdot\sigma(L_{12})\cdot A_{0}+2)\equiv 1\pmod{3},

which is false. Hence
\sigma(Q)=Q
. If
\sigma(L_{1})=L_{2}
then
\sigma(A_{1})
meets
G_{2}^{(1)}
,
L_{2}
and
Q
, so
{a}\coloneqq\varphi(\sigma(A_{1}))
satisfies

0=({l}_{1}-{l}_{2})\cdot{a}=3(\sigma(A_{1})\cdot A_{1})+2-3(\sigma(A_{1})\cdot A_{2})\equiv 2\pmod{3},

which again is false. Thus
\sigma
is as in
(d)(d.iv)
, so it induces an automorphism
\overline{\sigma}
of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{l})
interchanging
{l}_{1}
with
{l}_{2}
. Since
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
,
\overline{\sigma}({l})
is the other line tangent to the node of
{q}
; a contradiction.

∎

Remark 7.3

(Alternative construction in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, see
[
KN22
, Proposition 5.1(5)]
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. Then a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one and type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
can be constructed as follows. Fix an
\mathbb{F}_{3}
-rational point
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
, let
{l},{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{8}
be all
\mathbb{F}_{3}
-rational lines not passing through
p
, and let
p_{1},\dots,p_{8}
be all
\mathbb{F}_{3}
-rational points not lying on
{l}\cup\{p\}
. Let
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the blowup at
p_{1},\dots,p_{8}
, and let
X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
\psi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,8\}
. Clearly,
\overline{X}
is a surface of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
, hence by Proposition
6.1
it is isomorphic to the one constructed in Example
7.1
. More explicitly, choosing coordinates
[x:y:z]
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
p=[0:0:1]
and
{l}=\{z=0\}
, the morphism
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
from Example
7.1
is given by the contraction of
(-1)
-curves
\psi^{-1}_{*}\{x=0\}
,
\psi^{-1}[1:0:1]
,
\psi^{-1}[1:1:1]
and
(-2)
-curves
\psi^{-1}_{*}\{x+z=0\}
,
\psi^{-1}_{*}\{x=y+z\}
,
\psi^{-1}_{*}\{y=x+z\}
,
\psi^{-1}_{*}\{y=z\}
,
\psi^{-1}_{*}\{y+z=0\}
. Indeed,
\varphi(\psi^{-1}_{*}\{x=z\})
is a cuspidal cubic;
\varphi(\psi^{-1}_{*}\{x=z\})
is the line tangent to its cusp, and
\varphi(\psi^{-1}_{*}\{x+y=z\})
,
\varphi(\psi^{-1}_{*}\{x+y+z=0\})
are its inflectional tangent lines.

Now it is clear that
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
acts transitively on
\operatorname{Sing}\overline{X}
, as claimed in Lemma
7.2
(d)
. Moreover, every irreducible member
\overline{T}
of
|-K_{\overline{X}}|
is a proper transform of a cubic passing through
p_{1},\dots,p_{8}
; and a direct computation shows that every such cubic is cuspidal, as claimed in Lemma
7.2
(b)
. Indeed, choosing coordinates
[x:y:z]
as above, we see that every such cubic is of the form
{q}_{[a:b]}=\{ax(x^{2}-z^{2})=by(y^{2}-z^{2})\}
for
[a:b]\in\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\coloneqq\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}_{3}}
, and
{q}_{[a:b]}
has a cusp at
r_{[a:b]}\coloneqq[b^{1/3}:a^{1/3}:0]
.

We can now complete the proof of Lemma
7.2
(c)
, by constructing an almost universal family
f
representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(4\mathrm{A}_{2})
. Fix coordinates
[x:y:z]
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
as above; and let
[a:b]
be coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In the product
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, consider curves
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{p_{i}\}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
,
\Delta=\{xa=yb,z=0\}
and divisors
\mathcal{L}_{i}={l}_{i}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
,
\mathcal{Q}=\{a^{3}x(x^{2}-z^{2})=b^{3}y(y^{2}-z^{2})\}
. Let
\Psi\colon\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
be a composition of blowups at
\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{8}
and three blowups over
\Delta
, each time on the proper transform of
\mathcal{Q}
. Let
\mathcal{D}=\Psi^{-1}_{*}(\mathcal{Q}+\sum_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i})
. Then
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
represents
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(4\mathrm{A}_{2})
. The fibers of
f
over
z,w\in\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
are isomorphic if and only if there is an element of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{3})=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},p,\{p_{1},\dots,p_{8}\})
mapping
r_{z}
to
r_{w}
. This happens if and only if
z,w
lie in the same orbit of the induced action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
on
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Thus
f
is almost faithful, with symmetry group
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
. We claim that the formal germ of
f
at any
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is a versal deformation of
(X_{z},D_{z})
. To see this, consider any infinitesimal deformation
\widecheck{f}\colon(\widecheck{\mathcal{X}},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow T
of
(X_{z},D_{z})
. Performing a sequence of
K_{\widecheck{\mathcal{X}}/T}
-negative contractions we get a blowdown
\widecheck{\mathcal{X}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}_{T}
, mapping
\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}
to a configuration of lines and a cubic as above. Choosing coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{T}=\mathbb{P}^{2}\times T
we can assume that the image of
\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}
is
\sum_{i}({l}_{i}\times T)+\mathcal{Q}_{T}
for some family of cuspidal cubics
\mathcal{Q}_{T}
. The computation above shows that every such cubic has a cusp on
\widetilde{{l}}\times T
, where
\widetilde{{l}}={l}\setminus\sum_{i}{l}_{i}\cong\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
; in fact
\operatorname{Sing}\mathcal{Q}_{T}=(\alpha_{0}(t),t)\in\widetilde{{l}}\times T
for some morphism
\alpha_{0}\colon T\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Putting
\alpha=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}},\alpha_{0})\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\times T\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
we get
\Delta_{T}=\alpha^{*}\Delta
and thus
\mathcal{Q}_{T}=\alpha^{*}\mathcal{Q}
. By the universal property of blowing up we conclude that
\widecheck{f}
is a pullback of
f
along
\alpha_{0}
, as needed.

It remains to prove that the germ of
f_{z}
at
z
is a semiuniversal deformation of
(X_{z},D_{z})
. Suppose it is not. Since it is versal, it follows from
[
Ser06
, 2.2.7(iii)]
that the semiuniversal deformation of
(X_{z},D_{z})
is trivial; hence
f_{z}
induces a trivial formal deformation. By
[
Art69
, Theorem 1.7]

f_{z}
becomes a trivial deformation after an étale base change. This is impossible since the fibers of
f
are pairwise non-isomorphic; a contradiction.

8.
Del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and types
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
,
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
and
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
(
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
)

Throughout this section we assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. We now construct surfaces of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
from Proposition
6.1
(b)
and study their properties. In particular, we prove in Lemma
8.4
(c)
,
(d)
that their height equals
4
and their automorphism groups act transitively on the singular loci; as it was the case for type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
studied in Section
7
above. This automorphism group is computed in
[
KN20
, Corollary 5.22]
. Nonetheless, we provide a self-contained argument based on the geometry of the witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration found in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, see Figure
6(b)
, which makes it slightly more explicit.

It will be convenient for our future applications to gather here a construction and basic properties of other canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one which occur only if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, namely those of types
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
and
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
. They are of height
2
, and as such appeared in
[
PP24
, Examples 5.5 and 5.6(c)]
, see Figures
9(g)
and
9(m)
. Here we present an alternative construction of these surfaces, used e.g. in
[
KN20
]
.

Example 8.1

(Del Pezzo surfaces of types
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
,
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+D_{4}
and
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
)
.

Let
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
be all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational points, and let
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{7}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines: each
{l}_{j}
contains exactly three
p_{i}
’s, and each
p_{i}
lies on exactly three
{l}_{j}
’s, see Figure
8
(note that such a line arrangement cannot be realized unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
).

Put
{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{7}{l}_{j}
, let
\varphi\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a blowup at
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
, and let
A_{i}=\varphi^{-1}(p_{i})
,
D_{Y}=(\varphi^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}
.

{l}
p_{7}
p_{6}
p_{5}
p_{1}
p_{4}
p_{3}
p_{2}
{q}

Figure 8
.
Example
8.1
: the Fano plane, a non
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational line
{l}
, and a cubic
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}
.

(a)

Type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
.

Let
Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
be the contraction of
D_{Y}
. Then
\overline{Y}
is a del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see Figure
9(g)
. It is unique up to an isomorphism since
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
is; cf.
[
KN20
, Proposition 5.10]
.

This surface is isomorphic to the one constructed in
[
PP24
, Example 5.5]
with
\nu=3
. Indeed, let
\sigma\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a standard quadratic transformation centered at
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
. Then
\sigma^{-1}_{*}{p}
is precisely the configuration from loc. cit., and
\sigma
lifts to the required isomorphism between minimal log resolutions.

(b)

Type
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
.
Put
A_{1}^{\circ}\coloneqq A_{1}\setminus D_{Y}
,
D_{Y}^{\prime}\coloneqq D_{Y}+A_{1}
. Let
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a blowup at a point of
A_{1}^{\circ}
, and let
X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
\tau^{-1}_{*}D_{Y}^{\prime}
. Then
\overline{X}
is of type
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
, see Figure
9(m)
.
The set of isomorphism classes of such surfaces has moduli dimension
1
, see
[
KN20
, Proposition 5.17]
or
[
PP24
, Example 5.6(c)]
, with representing family over
A_{1}^{\circ}
parametrizing non–
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines through
p_{1}
, see Figure
8
. The symmetry group of this family is

\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p},p_{1})\cong\operatorname{Aut}(A_{1}^{\circ})\cong S_{3}
. It has a non-trivial stabilizer corresponding to the non–
\mathbb{F}_{2}
- but
\mathbb{F}_{4}
-rational lines through
p_{1}
, see
[
KN20
, Corollary 5.20]
.

(c)

Type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the net of cubics passing through
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
, see Figure
8
. It is given by

(7)

\{ayz(y+z)+bzx(z+x)+cxy(x+y)=0\},\quad[a:b:c]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}.

Its irreducible members correspond to
[a:b:c]\not\in{p}
. Any such member has a cusp at the point
[\sqrt{a}:\sqrt{b}:\sqrt{c}]
.

Pick
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
. Let
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}
be a cubic with cusp at
p
, let
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a blowup at
\varphi^{-1}(p)
and let
X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
(\varphi\circ\tau)^{-1}_{*}({p}+{q})
. Then
\overline{X}
is of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see Figure
6(b)
. The set of isomorphism classes of such surfaces is represented by an almost faithful family over
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
, with symmetry group

\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})=\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
, see

[
KN20
, Proposition 5.21]
. Its action has non-trivial stabilizers at
\mathbb{F}_{8}
-rational points of
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
, see
[
KN20
, Corollary 5.22]
.

Lemma 8.2

(Type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, cf.
[
KN20
, 5.10, 5.11]
or
[
KN22
, 5.9 – 5.11]
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be the del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
. Let
\pi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},0)
be its minimal log resolution, and let
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be as in Example
8.1
(a)
, i.e.
{p}
is the sum of all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{7}
, and
\varphi
is a blowup at all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational points
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

All irreducible members of
|-K_{\overline{X}}|
are cuspidal. The set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(7\mathrm{A}_{1})
has moduli dimension
2
. In fact, it is represented by an almost universal family over
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
with symmetry group
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
, see Table
2
.

(b)

We have
K_{X}=-\tfrac{3}{7}D-\tfrac{2}{7}U
, where
U\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi

(c)

The only
(-1)
-curves on
X
are the preimages of
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
.

(d)

We have
0pt{(\overline{X})}=2
.

(e)

We have natural isomorphisms
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})=\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})=\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
. In particular, the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})
acts transitively on the set
\operatorname{Sing}\overline{X}
.

(a)
Since
\overline{X}
is canonical, we have
K_{X}=\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}
. Let
Q
be an irreducible member of
|-K_{X}|
. By adjunction
Q\cdot A_{i}=1
for all
i
, so
Q
is isomorphic to its direct image
\varphi_{*}Q
. The latter is an irreducible member of the net of cubics
\mathcal{Q}
given by formula (
7
), so it is cuspidal, as needed.

To construct an almost universal family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(7\mathrm{A}_{1})
, we argue as in Remark
7.3
. Put
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}=\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
, where as above
{p}
is the sum of all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines. Let
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
be the diagonal, let
\overline{{p}}={p}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and let
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
be a hypersurface
\{a^{2}yz(y+z)+b^{2}zx(z+x)+c^{2}xy(x+y)=0\}
, where
[x:y:z]
and
[a:b:c]
are coordinates of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, respectively, cf. formula (
7
). Blow up at
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and three times over
\Delta
, each time at the proper transform of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. Denote the resulting family by
f\colon\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be the proper transform of
\overline{{p}}+\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. Then
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is a family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(7\mathrm{A}_{1})
. It is almost faithful, with symmetry group
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
: indeed, the diagonal action of
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
lifts to an action on
\mathcal{X}
such that two fibers of
f
are isomorphic if and only if they lie in the same orbit. Moreover, the germ of
f
at every
z\in\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is versal. Indeed, every infinitesimal deformation
\widecheck{f}\colon(\widecheck{\mathcal{X}},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow T
of
(X_{z},D_{z})
blows down to a configuration of all seven
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines and a cuspidal cubic
{q}_{T}
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{T}
. Such a configuration is a pullback of
\overline{{p}}+\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
along the morphism
\alpha\colon T\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
whose graph, viewed as a point of
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{T}
, is the singular locus of
{q}_{T}
. By the universal property of blowing up we get
\widecheck{f}=\alpha^{*}f
, which proves that the germ of
f
at
z
is versal. Exactly as in Remark
7.3
, we conclude that it is semiuniversal. Indeed, if it is not then by
[
Ser06
, 2.2.7(iii)]
the morphism from the germ of
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
at
z
to the base of a semiuniversal deformation of
(X_{z},D_{z})
contracts some curve, hence the restriction of
f
to this curve, call it
f_{0}
, is a trivial formal deformation. By
[
Art69
, Theorem 1.7]

f_{0}
becomes trivial after an étale base change, which is impossible, since it has pairwise non-isomorphic fibers.

(b)
We have
K_{X}=\varphi^{*}K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}+U=-\tfrac{3}{7}\varphi^{*}{p}+U=-\tfrac{3}{7}D-\tfrac{2}{7}U
, as claimed.

(c)
Let
A\subseteq X
be a
(-1)
-curve, and suppose
A\neq\varphi^{-1}(p_{i})
,
i\in\{1,\dots,7\}
. We have
\pi(A)^{2}>0>A^{2}
, so
A\cdot D\geqslant 1
. Part
(b)
and adjunction formula give
1=-K_{X}\cdot A=\frac{3}{7}D\cdot A+\frac{2}{7}U\cdot A
, so
U\cdot A=\frac{7}{2}-\frac{3}{2}D\cdot A\leqslant 2
. If
A
meets only one component of
U
then
\varphi(A)^{2}=-1+(U\cdot A)^{2}\in\{0,3\}
, which is impossible. Hence
A
meets exactly two components of
U
, so
\varphi(A)
is a line joining two base points of
\varphi
, a contradiction since
A\not\subseteq D
.

(d)
Let
F
be a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
. Part
(b)
and adjunction give
F\cdot D=-\frac{7}{3}F\cdot K_{X}-\frac{2}{3}F\cdot U=\frac{2}{3}(7-F\cdot U)\in\{4,2\}
, since
F\cdot D\geqslant 1
. The pencil of conics passing through
p_{1},\dots,p_{4}
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
2
on
(X,D)
, see Figure
9(g)
.

(e)
Clearly,
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})=\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
. Part
(c)
implies that every automorphism of
(X,D)
fixes
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
, so
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})=\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
.

∎

Similar description for type
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
is not strictly needed now, but will be convenient as a future reference.

Lemma 8.3

(Type
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
, cf.
[
KN20
, 5.19, 5.20]
or
[
KN22
, 5.20, 5.21]
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
. Let
\widehat{\varphi}\coloneqq\varphi\circ\tau\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be as in Example
8.1
(b)
, that is,
{p}
is the sum of all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{7}
;
\varphi
is a blowup at all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational points
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
; and
\tau
is a blowup at a point
p\in\varphi^{-1}(p_{1})\setminus D_{Y}
. Say that
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{4}
do not pass through
p_{1}
.
Then the following hold.

(a)

All irreducible members of
|-K_{\overline{X}}|
are cuspidal. The set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4})
has moduli dimension
2
. In fact, it is represented by an almost universal family over
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
, whose symmetry group is the stabilizer of
p_{1}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
in
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
, which is isomorphic to
S_{4}
, see Table
2
.

(b)

The
(-1)
-curves on
X
are:
\operatorname{Exc}\tau
,
\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}(p_{i})
,
i\in\{2,\dots,7\}
,
\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}_{*}{l}
and
\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}{c}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,4\}
; where
{l}
is a line through
p_{1}
such that
p\in\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}
, and
{c}_{i}
is the conic passing through all four
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational points not lying on
{l}_{i}
and tangent to
{l}
at
p_{1}
.

(c)

We have
0pt{(\overline{X})}=2
.

(d)

The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})=\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}\rtimes G
, where
G=\mathbb{Z}/3
if the point
p\in\varphi^{-1}(p_{1})\setminus D_{Y}
is
\mathbb{F}_{4}
-rational and
G=\{1\}
otherwise. Explicitly,
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is generated by the following automorphisms:

(i)

(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3},D_{4},T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\mapsto(D_{2},D_{1},D_{4},D_{3},T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})

(ii)

(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3},D_{4},T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\mapsto(D_{3},D_{4},D_{1},D_{2},T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})

(iii)

(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3},D_{4},T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\mapsto(D_{2},D_{3},D_{1},D_{4},T_{2},T_{3},T_{1})
if
p
is
\mathbb{F}_{4}
-rational

where
D_{i}=\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}_{*}{l}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,4\}
; and for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
,
T_{i}
is the twig of the
(-2)
-fork in
D
such that the lines
\widehat{\varphi}(T_{i}),{l}_{i}
and
{l}_{4}
meet at one point.

In particular, the subgroup
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}
acts transitively on the set of
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-type singularities of
\overline{X}
.

Put
A=\operatorname{Exc}\tau
. Let
\pi_{Y}\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
be the contraction of
D_{Y}
, so
\overline{Y}
is the surface from Example
8.1
(a)
.

(a)
Let
Q\subseteq X
be an irreducible member of
|-K_{X}|
. We have
Q\cdot A=1
by adjunction, hence
Q\cong\tau(Q)\in|-K_{Y}|
is cuspidal by Lemma
8.2
(a)
. As before, the assertion about
|-K_{\overline{X}}|
follows since
\overline{X}
is canonical.

The second statement follows from Lemma
8.2
(a)
and
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.19(1)]
. Indeed, let
f_{Y}\colon(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{D}_{Y})\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
be an almost universal family representing the set of minimal log resolutions of log surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(7\mathrm{A}_{1})
, constructed in Lemma
8.2
(a)
. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{Y}
be the component of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
corresponding to a member of
|-K_{Y}|
, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a divisor on
\mathcal{Y}
whose restriction to each fiber is a
(-1)
-curve meeting three components of the boundary. Now
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.19(1)]
implies that a composition of
f_{Y}
with a blowup at
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{Q}
is an almost universal family representing the set of minimal log resolutions of log surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4})
, as needed. The base of this family is the same as in
8.2
(a)
, namely
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
, and the symmetry group is the stabilizer of the image of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p},p_{0})
. We note that this group is isomorphic to
S_{4}
permuting
\{{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{4}\}
.

(b)
Let
C
be a
(-1)
-curve on
X
. Put
C_{Y}=\tau(C)
. By Lemma
8.2
(c)
, the
(-1)
-curves on
Y
are
A_{i}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}(p_{i})
,
i\in\{1,\dots,7\}
. If
C\cdot A=0
then
C_{Y}=[1]
, so
C_{Y}=A_{i}
for some
i\in\{2,\dots,7\}
. Thus we can assume
C\cdot A\geqslant 1
.

Denote by
T
the fork in
D
, and let
B
be its branching component, so
\tau(B)=A_{1}
. The class
K_{X}\in\operatorname{NS}(X)
is uniquely determined by equalities
K_{X}^{2}=10-\rho(X)=1
and
K_{X}\cdot L=0
for every component
L
of
D
, so
K_{X}=-T-B-A
. By adjunction
1=-C\cdot K_{X}=C\cdot(T+B)+C\cdot A
, so
C\cdot A=1
. In particular,
C_{Y}=[0]
.

Since
\pi_{Y}(C_{Y})^{2}>0
, we have
C_{Y}\cdot D_{Y}>0
. By Lemma
8.2
(b)
, we have
K_{Y}=-\frac{3}{7}D_{Y}-\frac{2}{7}U_{Y}
, where
U_{Y}=\sum_{i=1}^{7}A_{i}
. By adjunction
2=-K_{Y}\cdot C_{Y}=\frac{3}{7}D_{Y}\cdot C_{Y}+\frac{2}{7}U_{Y}\cdot C_{Y}
, so
U_{Y}\cdot C_{Y}\in\{1,4\}
. If
U_{Y}\cdot C_{Y}=1
then
\varphi(C)={l}
. Assume that
U_{Y}\cdot C_{Y}=4
. Suppose
C_{Y}\cdot A_{i}>1
for some
i\in\{2,\dots,7\}
. We have
C_{Y}\cdot A_{i}\leqslant C_{Y}\cdot(U_{Y}-A_{1})=3
, so such
i
is unique, and
\varphi(C)^{2}=C_{Y}^{2}+C_{Y}\cdot(U_{Y}-A_{i})+(C_{Y}\cdot A_{i})^{2}\in\{6,10\}
, which is impossible. Hence
C_{Y}
meets four components of
U_{Y}
, once each, so
\widehat{\varphi}(C)={c}_{i}
for some
i
, as needed.

(c)
Pulling back a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration from
Y
, we see that
0pt\overline{X}\leqslant 0pt\overline{Y}=2
by Lemma
8.2
(d)
. To see that
0pt\overline{X}=2
, consider a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration which is not a pullback from
Y
, so
A
is horizontal. Clearly, some degenerate fiber
F
meets
A
in a component
C\not\subseteq D
. By Lemma
2.6
(a)

C
is a
(-1)
-curve. If
\widehat{\varphi}(C)={c}_{i}
then
C
meets
D_{i}
twice, so
D_{i}
is horizontal and
F\cdot D\geqslant 2
, as needed. If
\widehat{\varphi}(C)=p_{i}
or
{l}
then
C
meets three or four components of
D
, so at least one of them is horizontal; and if exactly one is horizontal then
C
has multiplicity
2
in
F
. In any case, we get
F\cdot D\geqslant 2
, as needed.

(d)
Part
(b)
implies that
A
is a unique
(-1)
-curve on
X
meeting
D
once, so any automorphism of
(X,D)
fixes
A
. Thus we have a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)\ni\sigma\mapsto\varphi\circ\sigma\circ\varphi^{-1}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{l})
.

Consider the group homomorphism
\alpha\colon\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{l})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
given by the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{l})
on the pencil of lines passing through
p_{1}
. Its kernel is the subgroup of
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
consisting of elements which fix all three
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines passing through
p_{1}
; so
\ker\alpha
is generated by involutions
(d)(d.i)
and
(d)(d.ii)
. The image of
\alpha
is the subgroup of
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
consisting of matrices whose one eigenvector is
p\in\varphi^{-1}(p)\setminus D_{Y}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
. Thus if
p
is not
\mathbb{F}_{4}
-rational then
\operatorname{Im}\alpha
is trivial; otherwise
\operatorname{Im}\alpha\cong\mathbb{Z}/3
is generated by the image of the automorphism
(d)(d.iii)
.

∎

We conclude with the main result of this section.

Lemma 8.4

(Type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, cf.
[
KN20
, 5.21, 5.22]
or
[
KN22
, 5.22, 5.23]
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
. Let
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{q})
be as in Example
8.1
(c)
, i.e.
{p}
is the sum of all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{7}
;
{q}
is a cubic passing through
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
, with a cusp at
p\not\in{p}
, and
\varphi
is a blowup at
p
and at all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational points
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Put
D_{i}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{i}
,
D_{0}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{q}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

All irreducible members of
|-K_{\overline{X}}|
are cuspidal. The set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(8\mathrm{A}_{1})
has moduli dimension
3
; in fact, it is represented by an almost universal family over the threefold (
8
), with symmetry group
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
.

(b)

The only
(-1)
-curves on
X
are:
\varphi^{-1}(p)
and
\varphi^{-1}(p_{i})
,
\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{i}^{\prime}
,
\varphi^{-1}_{*}{c}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,7\}
, where
{l}_{i}^{\prime}
is the line joining
p
with
p_{i}
, and
{c}_{i}
is the conic passing through
p
and all
p_{j}\not\in{l}_{i}
.

(c)

We have
0pt(\overline{X})=4
.

(d)

The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})=\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3}\rtimes G
, where
G=\mathbb{Z}/7
if the point
p
is
\mathbb{F}_{8}
-rational, and
G=\{1\}
otherwise. Explicitly,
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is generated by the following automorphisms.

(i)

For
j\in\{1,\dots,7\}
:
D_{0}\mapsto D_{j}
,
D_{i}\mapsto D_{k}
whenever
{l}_{i},{l}_{j},{l}_{k}
meet at one point

(ii)

If
p
is
\mathbb{F}_{8}
-rational:
D_{0}\mapsto D_{0}
,
D_{i}\mapsto D_{i+1}
, where
D_{8}=D_{1}
.

In particular, the subgroup
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3}
acts transitively on
\operatorname{Sing}\overline{X}
.

(a)
Like in Lemma
8.2
(a)
, the proper transform of the pencil
|-K_{\overline{X}}|
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is contained in the net of cubics
\mathcal{Q}
given by formula (
7
), whose all irreducible members are cuspidal.

For the second assertion, we upgrade the family constructed in the proof of Lemma
8.2
(a)
to keep track of the additional cubic. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}=\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus{p}
. Consider the product
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, with coordinates
[x:y:z]
,
[a:b:c]
,
[\alpha:\beta:\gamma]
, and its subvarieties
\overline{{p}}^{\prime}\coloneqq{p}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
,
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{(u,u,v):u,v\in\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\}
,
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{a^{2}yz(y+z)+b^{2}zx(z+x)+c^{2}xy(x+y)=0\}
,
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\sigma(\Delta_{1}^{\prime})
,
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\sigma(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}^{\prime})
, where
\sigma(u,v,w)=(u,w,v)
. Let
\Delta\subseteq\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
be the diagonal, and let
\overline{{p}},\Delta_{i}
,
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
be the restrictions of
\overline{{p}}^{\prime}
,
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times B
, where

(8)

B\coloneqq\{\alpha^{2}bc(b+c)+\beta^{2}ca(c+a)+\gamma^{2}ab(a+b)=0\}\subseteq(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{2})\setminus\Delta.

Each fiber of the projection
(\mathbb{P}^{2}\times B,\overline{{p}}+\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}+\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{2})\longrightarrow B
is
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{q}_{1}+{q}_{2})
, where
{q}_{1},{q}_{2}
are members of the pencil (
7
), such that the cusp of
{q}_{1}
is a smooth point of
{q}_{2}\setminus{p}
. Moreover, we have
\operatorname{Sing}\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\Delta_{i}
. Blowing up once at
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\times B
, once at
\Delta_{1}
, and three times over
\Delta_{2}
, each time on the proper transform of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
, we get a family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(8\mathrm{A}_{1})
. Exactly as in the proof of Lemma
8.2
(a)
we conclude that this family is almost universal and its symmetry group is
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
.

(b)

Put
C_{0}=\varphi^{-1}(p)
. Let
L\subseteq X
be a
(-1)
-curve,
L\neq C_{0}
. As in Example
8.1
(c)
, let
\tau\colon(X,D-D_{0})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of
C_{0}
, so
\pi_{Y}\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},0)
is a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface of type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
. We have
\pi_{Y}\circ\tau(D_{0})\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
, so since
\overline{Y}
is canonical,
\tau(D_{0})\in|-K_{Y}|
. Since
\tau(C_{0})
is a point of multiplicity
2
on
\tau(D_{0})
, we get
D_{0}+C_{0}\in|-K_{X}|
. By adjunction
1=-L\cdot K_{X}=L\cdot D_{0}+L\cdot C_{0}
, so
L\cdot C_{0}\in\{0,1\}
. If
L\cdot C_{0}=0
then
\tau(L)
is a
(-1)
-curve on
Y
, so
\varphi(L)=p_{i}
by Lemma
8.2
(c)
. Assume
L\cdot C_{0}=1
. Then
L_{Y}\coloneqq\tau(L)
is a
0
-curve on
Y
. Since
\pi_{Y}(L_{Y})^{2}>0
, we have
L_{Y}\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 1
. Put
U_{Y}=\tau_{*}(\operatorname{Exc}\varphi)
. Then Lemma
8.2
(b)
and adjunction give
2=-L_{Y}\cdot K_{Y}=\frac{3}{7}L_{Y}\cdot D_{Y}+\frac{2}{7}L_{Y}\cdot U_{Y}
, so
L_{Y}\cdot U_{Y}\in\{1,4\}
. It follows that
\widehat{\varphi}(L)
is a line or a conic, so
L=V_{i}
or
C_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,7\}
, as claimed.

(c)
To see that
0pt\overline{X}\geqslant 4
, consider a fiber
F
of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
. By Lemma
8.2
(d)
, we have
F\cdot D_{Y}=2
, so by adjunction
2=-F\cdot K_{Y}=F\cdot\tau(D_{0})
, and therefore
\tau^{*}F\cdot D=F\cdot D_{Y}+F\cdot D_{0}=4
. Thus
|\tau^{*}F|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
4
, as needed. For the converse inequality, let
F
be a degenerate fiber of some
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
, and let
L
be a
(-1)
-curve of multiplicity
\mu\geqslant 1
in
F
. If
\varphi(L)=p_{i}
or
{l}_{i}^{\prime}
then
L
meets four
(-2)
-curves in
D
, so combining Lemma
2.6
(a)
with Lemma
2.4
(c)
we see that
4\leqslant\mu L\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}\leqslant F\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, as needed. If
\varphi(L)={c}_{i}
then
L\cdot D_{i}=2
, so the curve
D_{i}
is horizontal, and since
L\cdot(D-D_{i})=0
, the fiber
F
contains another
(-1)
-curve
L^{\prime}
, which again satisfies
L^{\prime}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 2
. Thus
F\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 4
, as claimed.

(d)
Let
C_{0}\coloneqq\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}(p)
,
A_{i}\coloneqq\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}(p_{i})
,
V_{i}\coloneqq\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}_{*}{l}_{i}^{\prime}
and
C_{i}\coloneqq\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}_{*}{c}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,7\}
be all
(-1)
-curves on
X
. An element
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
is uniquely determined by the images
\sigma(C_{0})
and
\sigma(A_{1}),\dots,\sigma(A_{7})
. Indeed, if
\sigma
fixes
C_{0},A_{1},\dots,A_{7}
then
\sigma
descends to automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
fixing
{p}+{q}
componentwise, so
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
.

It follows from
(b)
that if a
(-1)
-curve
L\subseteq X
satisfies
L\cdot D=4
then
L=A_{i}
or
V_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,7\}
. Hence every
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
fixes
\sum_{i=1}^{7}A_{i}+V_{i}
, which is a sum of disjoint chains
A_{i}+V_{i}=[1,1]
. Let
\alpha\colon\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)\longrightarrow S_{7}
be the group homomorphism given by the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
on the set of those chains.

Part
(b)
implies also that if a
(-1)
-curve
L
satisfies
L\cdot D=2
then
L=C_{i}
for some
i\in\{0,\dots,7\}
, in which case
L\cdot D_{i}=2
and
L\cdot(D-D_{i})=0
. It follows that every
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
satisfies
\sigma(C_{0})=C_{j}
for some
j\in\{0,\dots,7\}
, and
\sigma(C_{i})=C_{k}
if and only if
\sigma(D_{i})=D_{k}
.

Let
\sigma_{j}
be an element of
\ker\alpha
such that
\sigma_{j}(C_{0})=C_{j}
. We have
\sigma_{j}(A_{i})\cdot C_{j}=\sigma_{j}(A_{i})\cdot\sigma_{j}(C_{0})=A_{i}\cdot C_{0}=0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,7\}
. Thus
\sigma_{j}(A_{i})=V_{i}
if
A_{i}
meets
C_{j}
and
\sigma_{j}(A_{i})=A_{i}
otherwise. We conclude that

(9)

\sigma_{j}(A_{i})=A_{i}\mbox{ if }p_{i}\in{l}_{j},\quad\sigma_{j}(A_{i})=V_{i}\mbox{ if }p_{i}\not\in{l}_{j},\quad\mbox{and }\sigma_{j}(C_{0})=C_{j}.

In particular,
\sigma_{j}
is uniquely determined by
j\in\{0,\dots,7\}
. We have
\sigma_{0}=\mathrm{id}
, and
\sigma_{j}(C_{j})=C_{0}
, so
\sigma_{j}^{2}=\mathrm{id}
. Fix
k\in\{1,\dots,7\}\setminus\{j\}
. The curve
\sigma_{j}(C_{k})
meets
\sigma_{j}(A_{i}+V_{i})
once, and it meets
\sigma_{j}(A_{i})
if
p_{i}\not\in{l}_{k}
and
\sigma_{j}(V_{i})
otherwise. Condition (
9
) shows that
\sigma_{j}(C_{k})
meets
A_{i}
if and only if
p_{i}\in{l}_{j}\setminus{l}_{k}
or
p_{i}\in{l}_{k}\setminus{l}_{j}
; so the conic
\varphi(\sigma_{j}(C_{k}))
contains all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
rational points away from the third
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational line passing through
{l}_{j}\cap{l}_{k}
, say
{l}_{r}
. It follows that
\varphi(\sigma_{j}(D_{k}))={l}_{r}
, so
\sigma_{j}(D_{k})=D_{r}
. Thus
\sigma_{j}
is as in
(d)(d.i)
.

Assume for now that all automorphisms
\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{7}
satisfying (
9
) exist. Then
\ker\alpha=\{\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{7}\}
is generated by
\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j},\sigma_{k}
for any triple
\{i,j,k\}
such that
{l}_{i}\cap{l}_{j}\cap{l}_{k}=\emptyset
, so
\ker\alpha
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3}
and acts transitively on the set of components of
D
. Let
G\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
be the subgroup consisting of elements fixing
C_{0}
. Then for every
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
there is
\sigma_{j}\in\ker\alpha
such that
\sigma_{j}\circ\sigma\in G
, so the restriction
\alpha|_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{Im}\alpha
is surjective. Every element of
G
descends to an automorphism of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{q})
, i.e. an element of
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
with eigenvector
p
. If
p
is not
\mathbb{F}_{8}
-rational then
G=\{\mathrm{id}\}
; otherwise
G\cong\mathbb{Z}/7
permutes
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
cyclically, as needed.

The existence of an automorphism
\sigma_{j}\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
satisfying the condition (
9
) can be inferred from
[
Hos96
, Theorem 2.1]
, see
[
KN20
, p. 24]
. We provide an explicit construction.
Say that
j=1
and
p_{5},p_{6},p_{7}\in{l}_{1}
. Let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
C_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}V_{i}+\sum_{i=5}^{7}A_{i}
, such that
\widetilde{\varphi}(V_{i})=p_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,4\}
. Then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{*}D={p}+{q}^{\prime}
, where
{q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}
is a cubic passing through
p_{1},\dots,p_{7}
, with a cusp at
p^{\prime}\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(C_{1})
. We put
\sigma_{j}\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}^{-1}\circ\varphi\colon X\dashrightarrow X
. It remains to prove that
\sigma_{j}
is regular. This happens if and only if
{q}^{\prime}={q}
, i.e.
p^{\prime}=p
.

We prove the last equality by a direct computation. Write
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\varphi^{-1}\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
as a composition
\tau_{2}\circ\tau_{1}
, where
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
are quadratic Cremona maps;
\tau_{1}
is centered at
p,p_{1},p_{2}
; and
\tau_{2}
is centered at
q_{1}\coloneqq\tau_{1}(p_{3})
,
q_{2}\coloneqq\tau_{1}(p_{4})
and
q\coloneqq\tau_{1}({l}_{12})
, where
{l}_{12}
is the line joining
p_{1}
with
p_{2}
. Moreover, the coordinates on the target
\mathbb{P}^{2}
are chosen in such a way that
\tau_{2}(p_{i}^{\prime})=p_{i}
, where
p_{i}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}({l}_{i}^{\prime})
,
i\in\{1,2\}
; and
\tau_{2}({l}_{i}^{\prime\prime})=p_{i+2}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, where
{l}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is the line joining
q_{i}
with
q
. To find the point
p^{\prime}
, we note that
\tau_{1}({c}_{1})
is a line joining
q_{1}
with
q_{2}
, so the centers of
\tau_{2}^{-1}
are:
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
, and the point
p^{\prime}
we are looking for.

Fix coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
p_{1}=[1:0:0]
,
p_{2}=[0:0:1]
,
p_{3}=[1:1:0]
,
p_{4}=[0:1:1]
. Then
p=[a:1:b]
for some
a\neq b
,
a,b\not\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the standard Cremona transformation
\tau[x:y:z]=[yz:zx:xy]
. Fix
\delta_{1}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
such that
\delta_{1}(p_{1})=[1:0:0]
,
\delta_{1}(p_{2})=[0:0:1]
and
\delta_{1}(p)=[0:1:0]
, say

\delta_{1}[x:y:z]=[x+ay:y:z+by],

and take
\tau_{1}=\tau\circ\delta_{1}
. We have
\delta_{1}(p_{3})=[1+a:1:b]
,
\delta_{1}(p_{4})=[a:1:1+b]
, so
q_{1}=\tau_{1}(p_{3})=[b:b(1+a):1+a]
,
q_{2}=\tau_{1}(p_{4})=[1+b:a(1+b):a]
. By definition,
q=\tau_{1}({l}_{12})=[0:1:0]
,
p_{1}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}({l}_{1}^{\prime})=[0:0:1]
,
p_{2}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}({l}_{2}^{\prime})=[1:0:0]
. Fix
\delta_{2}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
such that
\delta_{2}(q_{1})=[1:0:0]
,
\delta_{2}(q_{2})=[0:0:1]
,
\delta_{2}(q)=[0:1:0]
, say

\delta_{2}[x:y:z]=[ax+(1+b)z:a(1+a)x+(1+a+b)y+b(1+b)z:(1+a)x+bz].

and put
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\tau\circ\delta_{2}
. Then
\tau_{2}^{\prime}({l}_{1}^{\prime\prime})=[0:0:1]
,
\tau_{2}^{\prime}({l}_{2}^{\prime\prime})=[1:0:0]
. Since
\delta_{2}(p_{1}^{\prime})=[1+b:b(1+b):b]
,
\delta_{2}(p_{2}^{\prime})=[a:a(1+a):1+a]
, we have
\tau_{2}^{\prime}(p_{1}^{\prime})=[b:1:1+b]
,
\tau_{2}^{\prime}(p_{2}^{\prime})=[1+a:1:a]
. Now
\tau_{2}=\delta_{3}\circ\tau_{2}^{\prime}
, where
\delta_{3}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
is given by
\delta_{3}[b:1:1+b]=[1:0:0]
,
\delta_{3}[1+a:1:a]=[0:0:1]
,
\delta_{3}[0:0:1]=[1:1:0]
,
\delta_{3}[1:0:0]=[0:1:1]
. We compute that

\delta_{3}[x:y:z]=[ay+z:x+y+z:x+by]

The centers of
\tau_{2}^{-1}
are
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
and
p^{\prime}
, so
p^{\prime}=\delta_{3}[0:1:0]=[a:1:b]=p
, as claimed.

∎

9.
Proof of Proposition
1.5
: rational anti-canonical curves on canonical del Pezzo surfaces

In this section we prove Proposition
1.5
, classifying log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, where
\overline{Y}
is a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, and
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a rational curve of arithmetic genus one. This result, together with the height computation in Proposition
9.1
below, completes the proof of Lemma
4.10
and thus of Proposition
3.2
.

Let
Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
be the minimal resolution of
\overline{Y}
, let
D_{Y}
be its exceptional divisor, and let
T
be the proper transform of
\overline{T}
on
Y
. Since
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
, the equality
p_{a}(\overline{T})=1
implies that
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
, so
T\in|-K_{Y}|
as
\overline{Y}
is canonical. In particular,
\rho(Y)=10-T^{2}=10-\overline{T}^{2}
by Noether’s formula, which proves
1.5
(d)
. We now prove the remaining parts of Proposition
1.5
by analyzing each type separately. In each case,
D_{Y}
consists of
(-2)
-curves, drawn as solid lines in Figures
6
–
10
. Some
(-1)
-curves on
Y
are shown as dashed curves. By adjunction,
T
meets each such
(-1)
-curve once; and of course is disjoint from
D_{Y}
.

Case

0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 3
, see Figure
6
.
By Proposition
6.1
,
\overline{Y}
is the surface of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
from Example
7.1
, or one of the surfaces of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
from Example
8.1
(c)
. By Lemmas
7.2
(b)
and
8.4
(a)
we have
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
or
2
, respectively, all irreducible members of
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
are cuspidal, as claimed in
1.5
(a)
, and the corresponding sets
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(4\mathrm{A}_{2})
,
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(8\mathrm{A}_{1})
have moduli dimensions
1
and
3
, respectively, as claimed in
1.5
(b)
.

Case

0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, see Figure
9
.
Now
\overline{Y}
is one of the surfaces constructed in
[
PP24
, §5A]
. Note that loc. cit. gives two constructions of the same surface of type
\mathrm{A}_{3}+\mathrm{D}_{5}
, here we use the one from Remark 5.7(c). Let
E\subseteq Y
be the sum of
(-1)
-curves corresponding to dashed lines in Figure
9
: we note that the horizontal ones in Figure
9(f)
are not explicitly constructed in loc. cit, we will construct them in the proof below.

Assume first that
\overline{Y}
is of one of the types in
[
PP24
, Example 5.6]
, see Figures
9(h)
–
9(m)
. As in loc. cit. let
Y\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}
be the contraction of
(-1)
-tips of
D_{Y}+E
and its images. Let
D_{Y}^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
be the sum of
(-2)
- and
(-1)
-curves in the image of
D_{Y}+E
. Then
(Y,D_{Y})
is a minimal resolution of a del Pezzo surface shown in one of the Figures
9(a)
–
9(g)
, call it
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
. The image
\overline{T}^{\prime}
of
\overline{T}
on
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
is a member of
|-K_{\overline{Y}^{\prime}}|
contained in the smooth part of
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
and isomorphic to
\overline{T}
. Since the center of each blowup in the decomposition of
Y\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}
is uniquely determined as the common point of the image of
T
and a specific
(-1)
-curve, universal property of blowing up gives a natural one-to-one correspondence between log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
and
(\overline{Y}^{\prime},\overline{T}^{\prime})
, cf.
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.18]
. Thus it is enough to prove Proposition
1.5
for surfaces shown in Figures
9(a)
–
9(g)
.

(a)

3\mathrm{A}_{2}

(b)

\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}

(c)

2\mathrm{D}_{4}

(d)

2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2

(e)

\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}

(f)

2\mathrm{A}_{4}

(g)

7\mathrm{A}_{1}
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=2

(h)

\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}

(i)

\mathrm{A}_{8}

(j)

\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}

(k)

\mathrm{A}_{3}+\mathrm{D}_{5}

(l)

\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}

(m)

4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=2

Figure 9
.
Canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height
2
, see
[
PP24
, §5A]
.

Let
\psi\colon(Y,D_{Y}+E)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be the morphism contracting all curves which are not thick in the corresponding figure.
The image of
T
after each blowdown in
\psi
is a member of the anti-canonical system, hence by adjunction it meets every
(-1)
-curve exactly once. It follows that
{q}\cong T
and
{q}\in|-K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}|
is a rational cubic.

We remark that in cases depicted in Figures
9(a)
–
9(d)
the morphism
\psi
is the same as the one used in
[
PP24
, §5A]
to construct
Y
. Moreover, in case
\#D_{Y}=8
, which is most complicated from our viewpoint, all morphisms
Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
are classified in
[
BBD84
]
. Our argument does not use this classification.

Type

3\mathrm{A}_{2}
, see Figure
9(a)
.
The image
{p}
of
D_{Y}+E
is a triangle, say
{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}
. Write
\{p_{0}\}={l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}
,
\{p_{j}\}={l}\cap{l}_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
. We have
({q}\cdot{l})_{p_{1}}=3
,
({q}\cdot{l}_{2})_{p_{0}}=3
; so by Lemma
5.4

{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a group law with
p_{0}=0
, and
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
or
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. We have
3p_{1}=0
and
p_{1}\neq 0
, so
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
and
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
; i.e.
{q}
is nodal or cuspidal, respectively. The configuration
{q}+{p}
exists and is unique up to a projective equivalence, see Lemma
5.5
. Moreover, if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
then the automorphism of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{q})
given by
(p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},r)\mapsto(p_{1},p_{0},p_{2},r)
, where
r
is the node of
{q}
, switches the analytic branches of
{q}
at
r
, as needed.

Type

\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, see Figure
9(b)
.
As before,
{p}
is a sum of lines
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{3}
passing through a point
p_{0}
, and a line
{l}
which does not. Write
\{p_{j}\}={l}_{j}\cap{l}_{0}
. We have
({q}\cdot{l}_{1})_{p_{0}}=({q}\cdot{l}_{2})_{p_{2}}=({q}\cdot{l}_{3})_{p_{3}}=2
. It follows that
{q}
has an inflection point: indeed, otherwise by Lemma
5.5
we have
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
and
{q}
is cuspidal, so the projection from
p_{0}
induces a morphism from the normalization of
{q}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, of degree
2
, ramified at the preimages of
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
and the cusp, which is impossible by the Hurwitz formula.

Thus
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a group law as in Lemma
5.4
. Looking at the intersection of
{q}
with lines
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{3}
and
{c}
, we get equalities
2p_{0}+p_{1}=2p_{2}+p_{0}=2p_{3}+p_{0}=p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}=0
in this group law. Hence
p_{1}=-2p_{0}
and
p_{1}=-(p_{2}+p_{3})
, so
3p_{1}=-2p_{0}-2(p_{2}+p_{3})=-(2p_{2}+p_{0})-(2p_{3}+p_{0})=0
and thus we can take
p_{1}=0
. If
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
then the conditions
2p_{0}=0
,
p_{0}\neq 0
imply that
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, so
p_{0}=-2p_{2}=0
; which is false. Hence
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
, i.e.
{q}
is nodal. Since
p_{0}\neq p_{1}
, the equality
2p_{0}=0
implies that
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
and
p_{0}=-1\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Now since
2p_{j}+p_{0}=0
for
j\in\{2,3\}
, we get
\{p_{2},p_{3}\}=\{\imath,-\imath\}
. Up to the action of
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{q})
given by
(p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\mapsto(p_{0},p_{1},p_{3},p_{2})
, we get a unique solution
(p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})=(1,-1,\imath,-\imath)
.

It follows that the configuration
{p}+{q}
exists and is unique up to a projective equivalence. Indeed, to construct it, fix a nodal cubic
{q}
with an inflection point
p_{0}
, hence with a group law on
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Next, let
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
be the points of
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
corresponding in the group law to
-1,\imath,-\imath
. They line on one line
{c}
. Letting
{l}_{j}
be the line joining
p_{0}
with
p_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, we get the required configuration
{p}+{q}
. Moreover,
\tau
interchanges the branches of
{q}
at its node. It follows that log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, where
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a nodal member of
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
exists, is unique up to an isomorphism, and admits an automorphism switching branches of
\overline{T}
at the node, as claimed.

Type

2\mathrm{D}_{4}
, see Figure
9(c)
.
Now
{p}
is a sum of lines
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{4}
passing through a point
p_{0}
, and one line
{l}
which does not. Write
\{p_{j}\}={l}\cap{l}_{j}
. We have
({l}_{1}\cdot{q})_{p_{0}}=3
;
({l}_{j}\cdot{q})_{p_{j}}=2
;
j\in\{2,3\}
and
({l}_{4}\cdot{q})_{p_{4}}=1
. By Lemma
5.4
,
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a group law with
p_{0}=0
and
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
or
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Looking at the intersection of
{q}
with
{l}_{2}
and
{l}_{3}
we get
2p_{2}=2p_{3}=0
. Since
p_{0}\neq p_{2},p_{3}
, we get
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
and
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. But then
2p_{4}=0
, so
({l}_{4}\cdot{q})_{p_{4}}=2
; a contradiction. Hence all members of
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
contained in
\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
are smooth, as claimed.

Type

2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, see Figure
9(d)
.
We have
{p}={l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}+{c}
, where
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{3}
are lines meeting at a point
p_{0}
, and
{c}
is a conic tangent to
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
at some points
p_{1},p_{2}
, and meeting
{l}_{3}
at two points, say
p_{3}
,
p_{3}^{\prime}
. The cubic
{q}
passes through
p_{0}
and is tangent to each
{l}_{j}
at
p_{j}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
,
p_{j}\neq p_{0}
. The pencil of lines passing through
p_{0}
induces a morphism from the normalization of
{q}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree two, ramified at three points. Since by assumption in this case we have
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, we get a contradiction with the Hurwitz formula
[
Har77
, Corollary IV.2.4]
. Thus the linear system
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
has no singular member in
\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, as claimed.

Type

\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
, see Figure
9(e)
.
Now
{p}
is a sum of a smooth conic
{c}
; two lines
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
tangent to
{c}
at, say,
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
; and a line joining
p_{1}
with
p_{2}
. The cubic
{q}
meets
{c}
at three points
p_{1},p_{2},q\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, and we have
({q}\cdot{l}_{j})_{p_{j}}=3
,
({q}\cdot{l}_{j})_{p_{j}}=({q}\cdot{c})_{q}=2
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Hence
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a group law such that
3p_{j}=0
,
2p_{1}+2p_{2}+2q=0
. Since
3p_{1}=0
, we can take
p_{1}=0
. Then the equality
3p_{2}=0
implies that
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
and
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3\}
then
2p_{2}+2q=0
implies that
p_{2}+q=0
, so
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
q
are collinear: this is false since these three points lie on a smooth conic
{c}
. Thus
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
. Changing the origin of
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
to a point off
\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
we get
(p_{1},p_{2},q)=(\zeta,\zeta^{2},-1)
for some primitive third root of unity
\zeta
. The two choices of
\zeta
are equivalent by an automorphism of
{p}+{q}
given by
(p_{1},p_{2},q,{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2})\mapsto(p_{2},p_{1},q,{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2})
.

As before, we conclude that the configuration
{p}+{q}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
exists, is unique up to a projective equivalence, and admits an automorphism switching the branches of
{q}
at its node; hence the same is true for
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
.

Type

2\mathrm{A}_{4}
, see Figure
9(f)
.
First, we construct the horizontal
(-1)
-curves shown in Figure
9(f)
. Let
\overline{\psi}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the morphism from
[
PP24
, Example 5.3]
, defined as follows. Choose a section
H\subseteq D_{Y}
and order the chains
F_{1}=[1,2,2,1]
,
F_{2}=[2,1,2]
supporting the degenerate fibers in such a way that the first tip of
F_{j}
meets
H
. Now let
\overline{\psi}
be the contraction of
F_{1}-F_{1}^{(2)}+F_{2}-F_{2}^{(1)}+H
. Then each
\overline{{l}}_{j}\coloneqq\overline{\psi}_{*}F_{j}
is a line, and
\overline{{c}}\coloneqq\overline{\psi}_{*}(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a smooth conic passing through
\{p_{0}\}\coloneqq\overline{{l}}_{1}\cap\overline{{l}_{2}}
. Write
\overline{{l}}_{j}\cap\overline{{c}}=\{\overline{p}_{j},\overline{p}_{0}\}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. The required
(-1)
-curves are proper transforms of the line joining
\overline{p}_{1}
with
\overline{p}_{2}
, and of the line tangent to
\overline{{c}}
at
\overline{p}_{1}
.

Now we can consider the morphism
\psi\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
defined like before, as the contraction of all thin lines in Figure
9(f)
. Let
{l}_{1},{l}_{2}
and
{l}_{1}^{\prime},{l}_{2}^{\prime}
be images of vertical and horizontal components of
D_{Y}
, respectively. Then
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
,
{l}_{1}^{\prime}
,
{l}_{2}^{\prime}
are lines in general position. Write
\{q_{1}\}={l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}^{\prime}
,
\{q_{2}\}={l}_{2}\cap{l}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\{q_{j}^{\prime}\}={l}_{j}\cap{l}_{j}^{\prime}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. The points
q_{1},q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}
are smooth points of the cubic
{q}
, such that

({q}\cdot{l}_{j})_{q_{j}}=({q}\cdot{l}_{j}^{\prime})_{q_{j}^{\prime}}=2
. The remaining component of
{p}
is a line meeting
{q}
at
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
and some
q\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

We claim that
{q}
has an inflection point. Suppose the contrary, so the cubic
{q}
is as in Lemma
5.5
(c)
. Applying this result to the point
p_{1}=q_{1}
, we get, in the notation of
5.5
(c)

p_{2}=q_{1}^{\prime}
,
p_{3}=q_{2}
and
p_{1}=q_{2}^{\prime}
, so
q_{2}^{\prime}=q_{1}
, a contradiction. Thus
{q}
has an inflection point, so
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
admits a group law as in Lemma
5.4
.

In this group law, we have
2q_{1}+q_{1}^{\prime}=2q_{2}+q_{2}^{\prime}=2q_{1}^{\prime}+q_{2}=2q_{2}^{\prime}+q_{1}=0
, so
(q_{1},q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})=(q_{1},4q_{1},13q_{1},7q_{1})
and
15q_{1}=0
. Moreover, we have
q=-q_{1}-q_{2}=-5q_{1}
. Hence
3q=0
, so
q
is an inflection point of
{q}
, and we can take
q=0
. Then
5q_{1}=0
, so
(q_{1},q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})=(q_{1},4q_{1},3q_{1},2q_{1})
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
then
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
, so
{q}
is cuspidal, the configuration
{p}+{q}
is unique up to a projective equivalence, and the result follows.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 5
. Then
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
, so
{q}
is nodal. In the group law,
q_{1}
is a primitive fifth root of unity. The automorphism
\tau
of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{q},q)
switching branches of
{q}
at the node induces a map
t\mapsto t^{-1}
in the group law of
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, so
\tau
maps
(q_{1},q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})
to
(q_{2},q_{1},q_{2}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime})
, and therefore preserves
{p}
. Hence up to the action of
\tau
, we have two choices of
q_{1}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
, namely
\zeta
and
\zeta^{2}
, where
\zeta
is a fixed primitive fifth root of unity. This way, we get exactly two cubics
{q}_{1}
,
{q}_{2}
as above; and for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have an automorphism
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{q}_{i})
switching the branches of
{q}_{i}
at the node.

It remains to prove that the morphisms
\psi_{i}\colon(Y_{i},D_{Y_{i}}+T_{i})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p}+{q}_{i})
constructed as above for
i\in\{1,2\}
yield isomorphic log surfaces
(Y_{i},D_{Y_{i}}+T_{i})
. The above uniqueness result for the cubics
{q}_{1}
,
{q}_{2}
implies that the automorphism
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
given by
\iota\colon(q_{1},q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})\mapsto(q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime},q_{1},q_{2})
satisfies
\iota({q}_{1})={q}_{2}
. Definition of
\psi_{i}
implies that the birational map
\psi_{2}^{-1}\circ\iota\circ\psi_{1}\colon Y_{1}\dashrightarrow Y_{2}
is regular: indeed, when constructing
\psi
, we blow up at
(q_{1},q_{2})
and
(q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})
in the same way. Thus we get an isomorphism
(Y_{1},D_{Y_{1}}+T_{1})\longrightarrow(Y_{2},D_{Y_{2}}+T_{Y_{2}})
, as needed. Moreover, the same argument shows that the map
\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\tau\circ\psi_{i}
is regular, and it yields an automorphism of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
switching the branches of
\overline{T}
at the node, as needed.

Type

7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see Figure
9(g)
.
Here the result follows from Lemma
8.2
(a)
.

Case

0pt(\overline{Y})=1
, see Figure
10
.
The log surface
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in
[
PP24
, Remark 4.5]
. As in case
0pt=2
, contracting
(-1)
-curves meeting the boundary once we can assume that all degenerate fibers are supported on chains of type
[2,1,2]
, call them
F_{1},\dots,F_{\nu}
. By assumption
Y\not\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
, so
\nu\in\{1,2,3\}
and
\overline{Y}
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
, or
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
, see Figures
10(b)
–
10(d)
. Let
H
be the section in
D_{Y}
, say that it meets each
F_{j}^{(1)}
. Let
\varphi\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be the contraction of
H+F_{1}^{(1)}+F_{1}^{(2)}+\sum_{j=2}^{\nu}(F_{j}^{(2)}+F_{j}^{(3)})
. Then
{l}_{j}\coloneqq\varphi_{*}F_{j}
are lines meeting at
\{p_{1}\}\coloneqq\varphi(H)
; and
{q}\coloneqq\varphi(T)
is a rational cubic. Moreover,
({q}\cdot{l}_{1})_{p_{1}}=3
, and for
j>1
we have
{l}_{j}\cap{q}=\{p_{1},p_{j}\}
for some
p_{j}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\setminus\{p_{1}\}
such that
({q}\cdot{l}_{j})_{p_{j}}=2
.

.
Canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height one, see
[
