, Remark 4.5]
.

If
\nu=1
then
{p}+{q}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
consists of a rational cubic and an inflectional tangent line. By Lemma
5.5
there are exactly two such configurations, one with
{q}
nodal and the other with
{q}
cuspidal. The former admits an automorphism switching the branches of
{q}
at the node, as needed.

Assume
\nu\geqslant 2
. Since
p_{1}\in{q}
is an inflection point, we can choose a group law on
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
with
p_{1}=0
. Then
2p_{j}=0
for
j\in\{2,\dots,\nu\}
. Assume that
{q}
is nodal. Then
p_{j}=\pm 1
in
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
, so the fact that
p_{1},\dots,p_{\nu}
are distinct implies that
\nu=2
,
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
; and
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
is unique. Moreover, the automorphism interchanging branches of
{q}
at the node, given by
t\mapsto t^{-1}
on
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, preserves
{p}
, as needed.

Assume that
{q}
is cuspidal. Then the equality
2p_{2}=0
in
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
implies that
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, and we can take for
p_{2},\dots,p_{\nu}
any
\nu-1
distinct points of
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\setminus\{p_{1}\}
. To prove the uniqueness result
1.5
(b)
, fix a cuspidal cubic
{q}
, which is unique up to a projective equivalence, and let
q
be its cusp. Since
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
by Lemma
5.5
(b)

{q}
has a unique inflection point
p_{1}
. The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{q})\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
fixes
p_{1}
and acts transitively on
{q}\setminus\{q,p_{1}\}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
. Thus a point
p_{2}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\setminus\{p_{1}\}
is unique up to the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{q})
.

If
\nu=2
it follows that
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
is unique up to an isomorphism. Assume
\nu=3
. Since
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{q},\{p_{1},p_{2}\})
is finite, the choice of
p_{3}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}\setminus\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
parametrizes an almost universal family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4})
. To be more precise, we argue as follows. Let
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a minimal log resolution of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4})
, let
H^{\prime}=[2]
be the
1
-section in
D^{\prime}
, and let
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime}-H^{\prime})\longrightarrow(Z,D_{Z})
be the contraction of the proper transform of the line joining
p_{1}
with the cusp of
{q}
. Then
D_{Z}
is a sum of four disjoint chains
[2,1,2]
and a
(-4)
-curve
Q_{Z}
meeting each of those chains once, in a
(-1)
-curve, see
[
PP24
, Figure 22]
. By Lemma 5.16 loc. cit., the set of isomorphism classes of such log surfaces
(Z,D_{Z})
has moduli dimension
1
. By Lemma 2.20 loc. cit. the same holds for
(Z,D_{Z}+H_{Z})
, where
H_{Z}=[1]
is the proper transform of
H^{\prime}
. Applying Lemma 2.18 loc. cit. to the inner blowup
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(Z,D_{Z}+H_{Z})
we get the required statement for
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4})
.

We have constructed an almost universal family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4})
over the same base as in
[
PP24
, Lemma 5.16]
, i.e. over
B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{{0,1,\infty}\}
. As noted at the beginning of this section,
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.18]
applied to a blowup at one of the points of
D_{Y}\cap T
shows that this family lifts to one representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{6})
, over the same base. We claim that their symmetry groups are
S_{3}
and
\mathbb{Z}/2
, respectively, see Table
2
.

The point
b\in B
corresponds to the cross-ratio of the four points
Q_{Z}\cap(D_{Z}-Q_{Z})
on
Q_{Z}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, call them
q_{0},q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
. Say that
q_{0}
lies on the fiber containing the base point of the morphism
Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow Z
; and for the type
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{6}
say that
q_{1}
is the image of the center of
\tau
. The fibers corresponding to
(q_{0},q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})
and
(q_{0}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime},q_{3}^{\prime})
are isomorphic if and only if there is an automorphism of
Q_{Z}
mapping
\{q_{0},q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\}
to
\{q_{0}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime},q_{3}^{\prime}\}
and mapping
q_{0}
(resp.
q_{0},q_{1}
) to
q_{0}^{\prime}
(resp.
q_{0}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime}
). The image in
\operatorname{Aut}(B)=S_{3}
of the group of such automorphisms is
S_{3}
and
\mathbb{Z}/2
, respectively, as needed.
∎

Our last result completes the proof of Proposition
3.2
by computing the height of log surfaces in
3.2
(6)
. This result follows from
[
PP24
, Lemma 6.9(a)]
, but for completeness we give a direct argument here.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be one of the log surfaces listed in Proposition
3.2
(6)
, that is,
\overline{Y}
is a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, not of type
\mathrm{E}_{6}
,
\mathrm{E}_{7}
or
\mathrm{E}_{8}
, whose minimal resolution has at least
6
exceptional curves; and
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a cuspidal member of
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
; see Table
1
. Then
0pt(\overline{Y},\overline{T})=0pt(\overline{Y})+2
.

Let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be the minimal log resolution. It factors through the minimal log resolution
(Y,D_{Y}^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},0)
. Let
T_{Y}
be the proper transform of
\overline{T}
on
Y
, and let
T_{Y}^{2}
. Since
T_{Y}\in|-K_{Y}|
, by Noether’s formula
T_{Y}^{2}=9-\#D_{Y}^{\prime}
, and by adjunction
T_{Y}
is a
2
-section for any
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
. Pulling back such fibrations to
X
, we get
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
. It remains to prove the opposite inequality.

Let
T
,
D_{Y}
be the proper transforms of
T_{Y}
and
D_{Y}^{\prime}
on
X
, and let
D_{0}
be the connected component of
D
containing
T
, so
D_{0}
is a fork with branching
(-1)
-curve
R
and twigs
T_{2}=[2]
,
T_{3}=[3]
,
T=[k]
, where
k=6-T_{Y}^{2}=\#D_{Y}-3\geqslant 3
by assumption; and
D_{Y}=D-D_{0}
. Then the morphism
X\longrightarrow Y
contracts
R+T_{2}+T_{3}
, and is an isomorphism near
D_{Y}
. Since
T_{Y}\in|-K_{Y}|
, we have
-K_{X}=T+T_{2}+T_{3}+2R
.

Claim
.

Let
L\neq R
be a
(-1)
-curve meeting
T_{3}
. Then
L\cdot T_{3}=L\cdot D_{0}=1
and
L\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})
. Moreover, if
L\cdot D_{Y}=1
then the component of
D_{Y}
meeting
L
is not a tip of
D_{Y}
.

By adjunction, we have
L\cdot(T+T_{2}+T_{3}+2R)=1
, so
L\cdot T_{3}=L\cdot D_{0}=1
. The image of
L
on
Y
is a
0
-curve, so it meets
D_{Y}^{\prime}
at least
0pt(\overline{Y})
times, as needed.

Suppose that
L\cdot D_{Y}=1
, and the component
H
of
D_{Y}
meeting
L
is a tip of
D_{Y}
. The image of
H
on
Y
, say
H^{\prime}
, is a
1
-section, and
(D_{Y}^{\prime})_{\mathrm{hor}}=H^{\prime}
. By Lemma
2.6
every degenerate fiber contains exactly one
(-1)
-curve, so it meets
H^{\prime}
in a component of
D_{Y}^{\prime}
, see Figure
10
. Since
H^{\prime}
is a tip of
D_{Y}^{\prime}
, it follows that there is only one degenerate fiber, and Lemma
2.4
(c)
shows that it is supported on a fork
\langle 2;[2],[2],[1,(2)_{k-4}]\rangle
, where
k=\#D_{Y}\geqslant 6
. Thus
\overline{Y}
is of type
\mathrm{E}_{k}
, contrary to our assumptions.

∎

Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
with a fiber
F
. By adjunction we have
2=F\cdot(T+T_{2}+T_{3}+2R)
, so either
R
is a
1
-section and
T
,
T_{2}
,
T_{3}
are vertical; or
R
is vertical, and
(D_{0})_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections or one
2
-section.

Assume that
T_{3}
lies in some fiber, say
F_{3}
. Then
T_{3}
meets a vertical
(-1)
-curve
L\neq R
: indeed, otherwise
T_{3}
is a tip of
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}
by Lemma
2.6
, so the
(-1)
-curve
R
meeting it has multiplicity at least
3
in
F_{3}
, which is impossible. Assume that
L
meets a vertical component
V
of
D_{Y}
. If
R
is vertical then
F_{3}=R+T_{3}+2L+V
, so
F_{3}\cdot D=2+2(L\cdot D_{Y}-1)+\beta_{D_{Y}}(V)\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+L\cdot D_{Y}+\beta_{D_{Y}}(V)\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
by the Claim above. If
R
is horizontal then
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}=[3,1,2,2]
or
[1,3,1,2]
. In the first case,
F_{3}\cdot D\geqslant 1+3(L\cdot D_{Y}-1)+2(\beta_{D_{Y}}(V)-1)=3L\cdot D_{Y}+2\beta_{D_{Y}}(V)-4\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
by the Claim. In the second case, denoting the other
(-1)
-curve in
F_{3}
by
L^{\prime}
, we have
F_{3}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 1+2(L\cdot D_{Y}-1)+\beta_{D_{Y}}(V)+L^{\prime}\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
as before. Eventually, assume that every
(-1)
-curve
L\neq R
meeting
T_{3}
is disjoint from
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}
. Then either
R
is horizontal and
F_{3}=\langle 3;[1],[1],[1]\rangle
, so
F_{3}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 1+30pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
; or
R
is vertical and
F_{3}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 2+0pt(\overline{Y})
; as needed.

Assume now that
T_{3}
is horizontal, so
R
lies in a fiber
F_{R}
, which contains all vertical components of
D_{0}
. Since
(D_{0})_{\mathrm{vert}}=[1,k]
,
[1,2]
or
[2,1,k]
the fiber
F_{R}
contains at most
k-2
vertical
(-2)
-curves. If
F_{R}
is a unique degenerate fiber then
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 1+\#(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 1+\#D_{Y}-(k-2)=6\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
, as needed. Thus we can assume that there is a degenerate fiber
F\neq F_{R}
. Such a fiber meets
T_{3}
in a vertical
(-1)
-curve
L\neq R
.

If
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=0
then, since
F_{R}
has at least two
(-1)
-curves, Lemma
2.6
(b)
implies that
\#(D_{0})_{\mathrm{hor}}=2
, so
T_{3}
is a
1
-section; and
L
is the unique
(-1)
-curve in
F
, so it has multiplicity at least
2
in
F
, which is impossible. Thus
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}\neq 0
. In particular
F\cdot D=2+F\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 3
, so we can assume
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
.

If
T_{3}
is a
2
-section then either
L
has multiplicity
2
in
F
; or
T_{3}
meets two
(-1)
-curves in
F
. In any case, the Claim gives
F\cdot D\geqslant 2+2\cdot(0pt(\overline{Y})-L\cdot D_{\mathrm{vert}})\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
since
L\cdot D_{\mathrm{vert}}\leqslant 2
and
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
by assumption.

Assume now that
T_{3}
is a
1
-section. Then
L
has multiplicity one in
F
, so
L\cdot D_{\mathrm{vert}}\leqslant 1
. Like before, we compute
F\cdot D\geqslant 2+0pt(\overline{Y})-L\cdot D_{\mathrm{vert}}
. The lemma follows unless
L\cdot D_{\mathrm{vert}}=1
,
F-L
is disjoint from
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, and
F\cdot D_{Y}=0pt(\overline{Y})-1
. Thus we can assume that all degenerate fibers are:
F_{R},F_{1},\dots,F_{s}
, where
F_{i}=[1,(2)_{s_{i}},1]
for some
s_{i}\geqslant 1
, and
F_{i}
meets
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}+T_{3}
only in the first component, with multiplicity
0pt(\overline{Y})
.

Let
T^{\prime}\in\{T,T_{2}\}
be the vertical component of
D_{0}-R
. Write
T^{\prime}=[k^{\prime}]
, so
k^{\prime}\in\{k,2\}
. Since
F_{R}
is supported on the sum of
T^{\prime}
, some
(-2)
-curves in
D_{Y}
, and
(-1)
-curves, we have
F_{R}=R+T^{\prime}+\sum_{j=1}^{v}V_{j}
, where
V_{j}
is a chain
[1,(2)_{v_{j}}]
, meeting
T^{\prime}
in a
(-1)
-curve; for some
v_{j}\geqslant 0
such that
k^{\prime}-1=\sum_{j}(v_{j}+1)=v+\sum_{j}v_{j}
.

Suppose that some component
H
of
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets a tip of
F_{R}
. Contracting all vertical curves which are disjoint from
H
we obtain a morphism onto a Hirzebruch surface such that the images of
H
and
T_{3}
have self-intersection
-2
and
-1
, which is impossible. It follows that
D_{Y}
does not contain any
1
-sections; and
v_{j}\geqslant 1
for all
j
. The latter implies that
k^{\prime}\geqslant 3
, so
k^{\prime}=k
, i.e.
T
is vertical. Moreover,
F\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 2
, so
0pt(\overline{Y})=F\cdot((D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}+T_{3})\geqslant 3
. By Proposition
6.1
,
\overline{Y}
is of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
or
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
. It follows that
v_{j}\in\{1,2\}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,v\}
. Since
8=\#D_{Y}=3+k=4+v+\sum_{j}v_{j}
, we get
v=2
and
v_{1}=v_{2}=1
. Thus
\overline{Y}
is of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, so
s_{i}=1
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}
. Since
\#(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}=\sum_{i}s_{i}+\sum_{j}v_{j}=s+2
and
\#D_{Y}=8
, we get
\#(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=8-s-2=6-s
, so
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=8-s
. By Lemma
2.6
(b)
we have
s+v=\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}-1
, so
s+2=8-s-1
, and eventually
s=\frac{5}{2}
, a contradiction.

∎
